    WHAT IS CILIUM?
    ───────────────
    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  CILIUM = Next-Generation Kubernetes Networking & Security  │
    │                                                              │
    │  ⚡ Powered by eBPF (extended Berkeley Packet Filter)        │
    │  🔒 Identity-Based Security (not just IP-based)              │
    │  🔍 Deep Observability with Hubble (like a microscope)      │
    │  🌐 Service Mesh Built-In (no sidecar needed!)              │
    │  🔐 Transparent Encryption (WireGuard/IPSec)                │
    │  🌍 Multi-Cluster Connectivity (Cluster Mesh)               │
    │  📊 L7 Network Policies (HTTP, gRPC, Kafka, DNS aware!)    │
    │                                                              │
    │  Think of Cilium as: The "Smart Brain" that replaces        │
    │  your old CNI plugin (Calico/Flannel) AND adds a            │
    │  service mesh AND observability — all in one!               │
    └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

PART 1: FOUNDATION — KUBERNETES NETWORKING BASICS                        🟢
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Chapter 1: Why Kubernetes Networking is Hard (And Why You Need Cilium)
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ How pods communicate on the same node and across nodes
         ✅ What the Kubernetes Network Model requires (4 immutable rules)
         ✅ What a CNI plugin is and what it does
         ✅ Why traditional iptables-based networking doesn't scale — and how Cilium's identity-based eBPF model fixes it
         ✅ Where Cilium fits in the Kubernetes stack
         ✅ Real networking problems anihpj faces and how Cilium solves them
         ✅ Key networking terminology refresher (IP, CIDR, DNS, ports, etc.)

    1.1  The "Hello World" of K8s Networking — What Happens When a Pod Talks?
         - Two pods on the same node: how do they communicate?
         - Two pods on different nodes: the cross-node challenge
         - The Kubernetes Network Model — 4 fundamental rules every pod must follow
         - Analogy: Pod networking is like an office phone system
           • Each desk (pod) has its own extension (IP address)
           • The office PBX (CNI plugin) routes calls between extensions
           • Calling another building (cross-node) requires inter-office routing

         📖 Key Term: Kubernetes Network Model
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  THE 4 RULES OF THE KUBERNETES NETWORK MODEL:                │
         │                                                              │
         │  1. Every Pod gets its OWN unique, cluster-wide IP address   │
         │     (no NAT, no port mapping) — like every house has its     │
         │      own street address. Containers WITHIN one pod share     │
         │      that single IP and reach each other over localhost.     │
         │                                                              │
         │  2. All pods can reach ALL other pods on ALL nodes           │
         │     directly, WITHOUT NAT (Network Address Translation)      │
         │     (like calling any extension in any building, direct)     │
         │                                                              │
         │  3. Agents on a node (kubelet, daemons) can communicate      │
         │     with ALL pods on that node                               │
         │     (like the building manager can reach every desk)         │
         │                                                              │
         │  4. A pod's own view of its IP matches what every other      │
         │     pod sees — no "inside vs outside" IP translation         │
         │     (this is a COROLLARY of rule 2's no-NAT guarantee,       │
         │      not a separately-numbered rule on kubernetes.io)        │
         │                                                              │
         │  Rules 1-3 paraphrase the official Kubernetes docs at        │
         │  kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/services-networking; rule 4     │
         │  traces to the original K8s networking design proposal's     │
         │  "NAT-less flat address space" goal. CNI plugins implement   │
         │  all 4 differently — Cilium implements them with eBPF.       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [VISUAL — Pod-to-Pod Communication on Same Node]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                  WORKER NODE                      │
         │                                                  │
         │  ┌──────────┐              ┌──────────┐         │
         │  │  Pod A    │              │  Pod B    │         │
         │  │ 10.0.0.1  │              │ 10.0.0.2  │         │
         │  │ (anihpj)  │              │(postgres) │         │
         │  └─────┬─────┘              └─────┬─────┘         │
         │        │ veth0                    │ veth1         │
         │        └──────────┬──────────────┘               │
         │                   │                              │
         │            ┌──────┴──────┐                       │
         │            │  Linux Bridge │  ← cbr0/cni0        │
         │            │  (L2 Switch)  │                      │
         │            └──────────────┘                       │
         │                                                  │
         │  ✅ Pod A → Pod B: Direct bridge forwarding       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [VISUAL — Pod-to-Pod Communication Across Nodes]
         ┌──────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────┐
         │    NODE 1         │         │     NODE 2            │
         │                   │         │                       │
         │  ┌──────────┐    │         │    ┌──────────┐       │
         │  │  Pod A    │    │         │    │  Pod C    │       │
         │  │ 10.0.0.1  │    │         │    │ 10.1.0.1  │       │
         │  │ (anihpj)  │    │         │    │ (redis)   │       │
         │  └─────┬─────┘    │         │    └─────┬─────┘       │
         │        │          │         │          │             │
         │   ┌────┴────┐     │         │     ┌────┴────┐        │
         │   │ Bridge  │     │         │     │ Bridge  │        │
         │   └────┬────┘     │         │     └────┬────┘        │
         │        │          │         │          │             │
         │   ┌────┴────┐     │         │     ┌────┴────┐        │
         │   │  eth0   │     │         │     │  eth0   │        │
         │   │10.0.0.1 │─────┼─VXLAN──┼─────│10.0.0.2 │        │
         │   └─────────┘     │  TUNNEL │     └─────────┘        │
         └──────────────────┘         └──────────────────────┘
         ❌ Without Cilium: Packets go through VXLAN tunnel (overhead!)
         ✅ With Cilium: eBPF routes directly at kernel level (fast!)

    1.2  The CNI (Container Network Interface) Explained — Like Plugging in a Cable
         - What is a CNI plugin? Think of it as the "network driver" for Kubernetes
         - Analogy: CNI is like a USB standard
           • USB defines HOW devices connect (the interface)
           • CNI defines HOW pods get network connectivity (the interface)
           • Calico, Flannel, Cilium = different "USB devices" that follow the CNI spec
         - Popular CNIs: Calico (iptables), Flannel (VXLAN), Cilium (eBPF)
         - How CNI works: kubelet asks the container runtime (via CRI) to start the pod → the container runtime invokes the CNI plugin binary → CNI assigns IP → Pod gets network
         - CNI configuration file: /etc/cni/net.d/*.conf

         📖 Key Term: Container Network Interface (CNI)
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CNI is a SPECIFICATION, not software. It defines:           │
         │                                                              │
         │  • ADD command: "Give this container a network!"             │
         │    → CNI plugin allocates IP, creates veth pair, sets routes │
         │                                                              │
         │  • DEL command: "Remove this container's network!"           │
         │    → CNI plugin releases IP, removes veth, cleans up rules   │
         │                                                              │
         │  • CHECK command: "Is this container's network healthy?"     │
         │    → CNI plugin verifies connectivity                        │
         │                                                              │
         │  • VERSION command: "What CNI spec version do you support?"  │
         │                                                              │
         │  CNI plugins are BINARIES on the node filesystem. But since  │
         │  Kubernetes v1.24 removed CNI management from kubelet, it is │
         │  the CONTAINER RUNTIME (containerd/CRI-O) that invokes them  │
         │  during RunPodSandbox — NOT kubelet directly (kubelet only   │
         │  talks to the runtime over CRI; see Common Pitfall 2 below). │
         │  Cilium's cilium-cni binary is a thin shim: it runs once per │
         │  pod, then calls the ALREADY-RUNNING cilium-agent daemon     │
         │  (over a local Unix socket) to do the real work — so Cilium's│
         │  design absolutely DOES need a persistent daemon.            │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CNI FLOW DIAGRAM — kubelet → Container Runtime (via CRI) → CNI Plugin]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  ①  kubelet watches API Server for new Pod assignments       │
         │        │                                                     │
         │        │  "New pod assigned to me! anihpj-abc123"            │
         │        ▼                                                     │
         │  ②  kubelet calls CRI RunPodSandbox on the container         │
         │     runtime (this is a CRI request, not a CNI call yet)      │
         │        │                                                     │
         │        ▼                                                     │
         │  ③  Container runtime (containerd/CRI-O) creates the POD     │
         │     SANDBOX (pause container) — no network attached yet!     │
         │        │                                                     │
         │        │  "Sandbox created — now attach it to a network!"    │
         │        ▼                                                     │
         │  ④  The CONTAINER RUNTIME itself invokes the CNI plugin      │
         │     binary — NOT kubelet (kubelet stopped managing CNI       │
         │     directly as of Kubernetes v1.24):                        │
         │     /opt/cni/bin/cilium-cni ADD <config>                     │
         │        ├── 4a. Allocate IP address (from Pod CIDR)           │
         │        ├── 4b. Create veth pair (one end in pod netns,       │
         │        │        one end on the host)                         │
         │        ├── 4c. Assign IP to the pod's network interface      │
         │        ├── 4d. Set up routes (default gateway, pod routes)   │
         │        └── 4e. Apply NetworkPolicy (eBPF, via the already-   │
         │                 running cilium-agent daemon)                 │
         │        │                                                     │
         │        ▼                                                     │
         │  ⑤  Sandbox now has networking — runtime starts the app      │
         │     container(s) inside it; kubelet marks the pod RUNNING    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CNI COMPARISON — Popular Plugins]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CNI        METHOD         ENCAP     L7?     ENCRYPT?  OBS?  │
         │  ─────────  ─────────────  ────────  ─────  ────────  ─────  │
         │  Flannel    VXLAN overlay  VXLAN     ❌     ❌        ❌     │
         │  Calico     iptables/BGP   None/IPIP ❌     ❌(WireG) ❌     │
         │  Weave      VXLAN overlay  VXLAN     ❌     ✅        ❌     │
         │  Cilium ☸️   eBPF kernel    None/VXLN ✅     ✅        ✅    │
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 Only Cilium does ALL of: L7 policies, Encryption,        │
         │     and Observability — in ONE tool with NO sidecars!        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         📖 Key Term: Identity-Based Security — Why iptables Doesn't Scale
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Traditional tools (iptables) match packets by IP + port,    │
         │  walking an ORDERED LIST of rules for every single packet    │
         │  — O(n) lookup that gets slower as rules pile up.            │
         │                                                              │
         │  Kubernetes pods are ephemeral — IPs churn constantly.       │
         │  Cilium's own docs note conventional tools need "hundreds    │
         │  of thousands of rules that need to be updated with a        │
         │  continuously growing frequency" just to keep up.            │
         │                                                              │
         │  Cilium instead firewalls by IDENTITY, not IP. Every pod     │
         │  gets a cluster-wide Identity derived from its security-     │
         │  relevant Kubernetes labels — pods sharing labels share      │
         │  one Identity. eBPF looks it up in a hash map: O(1), flat,   │
         │  no matter how large the cluster grows.                      │
         │                                                              │
         │  IPs can churn all day — the Identity (and the policy        │
         │  attached to it) never has to change.                        │
         │                                                              │
         │  You'll go deep on how Identities are allocated and          │
         │  enforced in Chapter 4 (Architecture). For now: Cilium       │
         │  firewalls by WHO a pod is, not WHERE its IP happens to be.  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    1.3  Where Cilium Fits in the Kubernetes Stack
         - Cilium replaces your old CNI (Calico/Flannel/Weave)
         - But it also replaces: kube-proxy, service mesh sidecars, network policy engine
         - One Cilium agent on each node = 4 tools consolidated into 1

         [STACK SIMPLIFICATION — How Cilium Consolidates Your Cluster]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  TRADITIONAL STACK (4 tools!)      CILIUM STACK (1 tool!)    │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────┐       ┌─────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ Tool 1: Calico/Flannel  │       │                     │   │
         │  │         (CNI: pod net)  │       │   ☸️ CILIUM AGENT   │   │
         │  ├─────────────────────────┤       │   (DaemonSet pod    │   │
         │  │ Tool 2: kube-proxy      │  ──▶  │    per node)        │   │
         │  │         (Service LB)    │       │                     │   │
         │  ├─────────────────────────┤       │   Replaces 4 + 2:   │   │
         │  │ Tool 3: Istio Sidecar   │       │   ✅ Pod Networking  │   │
         │  │         (Service Mesh)  │       │   ✅ Load Balancing  │   │
         │  ├─────────────────────────┤       │   ✅ Service Mesh   │   │
         │  │ Tool 4: Calico NetPol   │       │   ✅ Network Policy  │   │
         │  │         (NetworkPolicy) │       │   ✅ Encryption     │   │
         │  └─────────────────────────┘       │   ✅ Observability  │   │
         │                                     └─────────────────────┘   │
         │  ❌ 4 DaemonSets/Deployments         ✅ 1 DaemonSet            │
         │  ❌ 4 configs to manage              ✅ 1 install command      │
         │  ❌ 4x the resource overhead         ✅ Minimal overhead       │
         │  ❌ 4 places to troubleshoot         ✅ 1 place to debug       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         📖 Key Term: The Cilium Toolset — More Than One Binary
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  "Cilium" isn't one binary. The official Component Overview  │
         │  names three layers:                                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  1) DAEMONS (always running, do the real work):              │
         │     • cilium-agent - one per node (DaemonSet); programs      │
         │       eBPF, enforces policy, embeds the Hubble server        │
         │     • cilium-operator - ONE per cluster (not per node);      │
         │       handles cluster-wide jobs like IP-allocation           │
         │     • Hubble Relay - cluster-wide; aggregates every node's   │
         │       Hubble server into one gRPC API                        │
         │                                                              │
         │  2) THE CNI PLUGIN (short-lived, one shot per pod):          │
         │     • cilium-cni - invoked by the container runtime, calls   │
         │       the already-running cilium-agent to do the work        │
         │                                                              │
         │  3) CLIs humans actually type (3 SEPARATE binaries!):        │
         │     • cilium - remote cluster management (install, status,   │
         │       connectivity tests)                                    │
         │     • cilium-dbg - node-local debug CLI; inspects THIS       │
         │       node's agent state & eBPF maps directly                │
         │     • hubble - queries flow data from Relay or a local       │
         │       Hubble server                                          │
         │                                                              │
         │  Exam trap: cilium-dbg is NOT the cilium CLI - don't mix     │
         │  them up. Chapter 4 covers each component in full depth.     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    1.4  Real-World Networking Problems anihpj Faces — Before vs After Cilium
         - How does jobpost app talk to PostgreSQL securely?
         - How do we block the Django admin panel (/admin) from external access?
         - How do we see which pod is sending too many requests?
         - How do we encrypt database traffic without changing app code?
         - How do we connect anihpj running in two different K8s clusters?

         [anihpj PROBLEM → CILIUM SOLUTION — Before and After]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  🔴 PROBLEM 1: "Someone is brute-forcing the Django /admin"   │
         │                                                               │
         │  WITHOUT Cilium:                  WITH Cilium:                │

         │  │ Block IP ranges   │            │ L7 CiliumNetworkPolicy│    │
         │  │ at the WAF/nginx  │            │ Block POST /admin    │    │
         │  │ level — fragile!  │            │ from external CIDRs  │    │
         │  │ IPs change daily  │            │ at the KERNEL level! │    │
         │  └──────────────────┘            │ 🟢 Fast, permanent   │    │
         │                                   └──────────────────────┘    │
         │                                                               │
         │  🔴 PROBLEM 2: "Which pod is slow? We have 20 Django pods!"  │
         │                                                               │
         │  WITHOUT Cilium:                  WITH Cilium:                │
         │  ┌──────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────┐    │
         │  │ kubectl top pods  │            │ Hubble UI: see latency│    │
         │  │ Only shows CPU/RAM│            │ PER pod, PER endpoint│    │
         │  │ No network info!  │            │ Pod X slow on /jobs/ │    │
         │  │ 😱 Flying blind   │            │ 🟢 Pinpoint in seconds│   │
         │  └──────────────────┘            └──────────────────────┘    │
         │                                                               │
         │  🔴 PROBLEM 3: "Encrypt database traffic for compliance"      │
         │                                                               │
         │  WITHOUT Cilium:                  WITH Cilium:                │
         │  ┌──────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────┐    │
         │  │ Reconfigure Django│            │ cilium install       │    │
         │  │ to use TLS       │            │ --set encryption.     │    │
         │  │ Configure Postgres│            │ enabled=true          │    │
         │  │ for SSL           │            │ --set encryption.     │    │
         │  │ Manage certs      │            │ type=wireguard        │    │
         │  │ 😱 Weeks of work  │            │ 🟢 DONE. ZERO code   │    │
         │  └──────────────────┘            │    changes!           │    │

         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    1.5  Key Networking Concepts You'll Need (Quick Refresher)
         - IP addresses, subnets, CIDR notation
         - TCP vs UDP, ports, protocols
         - DNS resolution (how anihpj finds postgres.anihpj.svc)
         - Load balancing basics (round-robin, least connections)
         - Firewall rules / iptables concepts
         - Overlay vs underlay networks
         - MTU and packet fragmentation
         - Network namespaces in Linux
         - Veth pairs (virtual Ethernet devices)
         - Encapsulation (VXLAN, Geneve)

         [NETWORKING CONCEPTS — Quick Reference Table with anihpj Examples]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TERM          ANALOGY              anihpj EXAMPLE            │
         │  ────────────  ───────────────────  ──────────────────────── │
         │                                                               │
         │  IP Address    House address        10.0.0.55 (Django Pod IP) │
         │  Port          Apartment number     8000 (Django), 5432 (DB) │
         │  Subnet/CIDR   Neighborhood         10.0.0.0/16 (Pod CIDR)   │
         │  DNS           Phone book           postgres.anihpj.svc → IP │
         │  TCP           Certified mail        DB queries (reliable)   │
         │  UDP           Postcard              DNS queries (fast)      │
         │  Load Balancer Receptionist          Service → 3 Django pods │
         │  Firewall      Security guard        CiliumNetworkPolicy     │
         │  iptables      Paper checklist       "Rule 1? No. Rule 2..." │
         │  eBPF          Computer database     "Lookup: pod→ID→allow"  │
         │  VXLAN Tunnel  Underground tunnel     Cross-node pod traffic │
         │  Network NS    Private office         Pod's isolated network  │
         │  veth pair     Ethernet cable         Pod ↔ Host bridge      │
         │  MTU           Box size limit         1500 bytes (default)   │
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 These building blocks are the foundation of EVERYTHING   │
         │     Cilium does. Master them, and Cilium becomes intuitive.  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [7-LAYER OSI MODEL — Where Cilium Operates]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  LAYER    NAME              CILIUM CAPABILITY                │
         │  ───────  ────────────────  ──────────────────────────────── │
         │  L7       Application       HTTP/gRPC/Kafka/DNS policies     │
         │           (HTTP, gRPC)      "Block POST /admin"              │
         │  L4       Transport         TCP/UDP port policies            │
         │           (TCP, UDP)        "Allow port 5432 only"           │
         │  L3       Network           IP/CIDR rules, BGP routing,      │
         │           (IP, ICMP, BGP)   IPsec/WireGuard encryption       │
         │  L2       Data Link         MAC, VLAN, ARP (L2 neighbor      │
         │           (Ethernet, ARP)   discovery)                       │
         │  L1       Physical          NIC hardware only — Cilium's     │
         │                             eBPF/XDP hooks attach here       │
         │                                                              │
         │  Traditional CNIs mostly work at L3-L4.                      │
         │  Cilium reaches L3, L4, AND L7 — understanding your app!     │
         │  (BGP and WireGuard/IPsec are L3 concerns, not L2/L1 —       │
         │   a common simplification in OSI cheat-sheets to avoid.)     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 1):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Thinking "pods automatically have network security"
            → TRUTH: By default, all pods can talk to all pods! No isolation.
            → FIX: You MUST create NetworkPolicies (Chapter 7).

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Confusing CNI with CRI (Container Runtime Interface)
            → TRUTH: CNI handles networking. CRI handles container lifecycle.
            → FIX: CNI = networking. CRI = start/stop containers. Different.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Thinking all CNIs are the same
            → TRUTH: Calico uses iptables. Flannel uses VXLAN. Cilium uses eBPF.
            → FIX: They differ hugely in performance, features, and complexity.

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 1):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. Without a CNI plugin installed, what is the state of a newly created pod?
        A) Running    B) Pending    C) ContainerCreating (stuck)    D) CrashLoopBackOff

    Q2. Name 3 separate tools that Cilium replaces in a traditional Kubernetes stack.

    Q3. Why is iptables-based networking slower than eBPF-based networking at scale?
        A) iptables uses more RAM    B) iptables does linear rule matching O(n)
        C) iptables runs in userspace    D) iptables doesn't support IPv6

    Q4. What are the 4 rules of the Kubernetes Network Model?

    Q5. How does kubelet invoke the CNI plugin? What information does it pass?
        A) Via gRPC call to a CNI daemon
        B) By executing the CNI binary with JSON config via stdin
        C) By writing to a CNI socket file
        D) Via the Kubernetes API Server

    Q6. If anihpj pod (10.0.0.55) needs to talk to postgres pod (10.0.1.99) on a
        different node, what happens in a VXLAN-based CNI vs an eBPF-based CNI?

    Q7. What is the difference between an overlay network and an underlay network?
        Give an example of each in Kubernetes.

    Q8. Why can't two containers in the same pod both listen on port 8000?

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 1):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ The Kubernetes Network Model locks in 4 rules every CNI must satisfy: each
            Pod gets its own unique IP (no NAT), pods reach every pod on every node
            without translation, node agents reach every pod on their node, and a
            pod's own view of its IP matches what other pods see.

         ✅ CNI (Container Network Interface) is a SPECIFICATION, not software — it
            defines the ADD/DEL/CHECK/VERSION commands kubelet executes directly
            against a plugin binary (e.g. /opt/cni/bin/cilium-cni) to allocate a pod's
            IP, veth pair, and routes.

         ✅ Cilium consolidates 4 separate cluster tools — the CNI, kube-proxy, a
            service mesh sidecar, and a network policy engine — into ONE eBPF-powered
            agent running as a single DaemonSet per node.

         ✅ The anihpj before/after examples (brute-forced /admin, 'which of 20 pods
            is slow?', encrypting Postgres traffic) show fragile WAF rules, kubectl
            top blindness, and weeks of TLS work replaced by an L7
            CiliumNetworkPolicy, Hubble UI's per-pod visibility, and one --set
            encryption.enabled=true flag.

         ✅ The 7-layer OSI model shows traditional CNIs like Calico and Flannel only
            operate at L3 (IP/CIDR) and L4 (TCP/UDP) — Cilium alone also reaches L7
            (HTTP/gRPC/Kafka/DNS), enabling policies like 'block POST /admin' that
            pure L3/L4 tools cannot express.

    ─────────────────────────
         Chapter 2 opens the hood on how a Pod actually gets that IP address in the
         first place. You'll see how Linux namespaces isolate each container, how the
         pause container holds the shared network namespace, how IPAM hands out the
         IP, how CoreDNS and Services power discovery, and how kube-proxy's
         iptables/IPVS modes set the stage for why Cilium's eBPF replacement is so
         much faster.


Chapter 2: Containers, Pods, and the Network Namespace — The Foundation of Pod Networking
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ What Linux namespaces are and how they isolate containers
         ✅ How containers in the same pod share a network namespace
         ✅ The "pause container" pattern — the secret behind pod networking
         ✅ How pods get their IP addresses (IPAM basics)
         ✅ How Kubernetes Service discovery works (DNS, ClusterIP, Endpoints)
         ✅ How kube-proxy works — and why Cilium replaces it
         ✅ How Cilium's eBPF-based kube-proxy replacement achieves O(1) lookups

    2.1  What Happens When You Run "docker run anihpj"?
         - The Linux kernel features behind containers: namespaces (isolation) + cgroups (limits)
         - Types of namespaces: PID, Mount, UTS, IPC, User, and NETWORK
         - (The kernel also ships two more: the Cgroup namespace — virtualizes a
           process's view of its cgroup hierarchy, and is the SAME kernel construct
           Cilium's eBPF programs attach to for the socket-based kube-proxy
           replacement covered in 2.5/Chapter 11 — and the newer Time namespace,
           used mainly for container checkpoint/restore. This guide focuses on the
           six most networking-relevant namespaces above.)
         - Network namespace: each container gets its own virtual network stack
           • Own interfaces (lo, eth0)
           • Own routing table
           • Own iptables rules
           • Own IP addresses (no conflict with host or other containers!)

         📖 Key Term: Linux Network Namespace
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  A network namespace is like giving each container its own   │
         │  PRIVATE NETWORK ROOM. Inside the room:                      │
         │                                                               │
         │  • It sees its own network cards (interfaces)                │
         │  • It has its own routing table ("how to reach the internet")│
         │  • It has its own firewall rules (iptables/nftables)         │
         │  • It has its own IP addresses                               │
         │                                                               │
         │  But it CAN'T see what's in OTHER rooms!                      │
         │  (unless connected via bridges/veth pairs)                   │
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 Namespaces are the reason containers can all use port    │
         │     8000 without conflicting — they're in different rooms!   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CONTAINER ISOLATION — What Namespaces Provide]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  HOST LINUX SYSTEM                                           │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │                    KERNEL (shared by all containers)      │ │
         │  │                                                          │ │
         │  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐   │ │
         │  │  │  Container A │  │  Container B  │  │  Container C │   │ │
         │  │  │  (anihpj)    │  │  (postgres)   │  │  (nginx)     │   │ │
         │  │  │              │  │               │  │              │   │ │
         │  │  │ Own NET NS   │  │ Own NET NS    │  │ Own NET NS   │   │ │
         │  │  │ Own PID NS   │  │ Own PID NS    │  │ Own PID NS   │   │ │
         │  │  │ Own MNT NS   │  │ Own MNT NS    │  │ Own MNT NS   │   │ │
         │  │  │              │  │               │  │              │   │ │
         │  │  │ IP: 10.0.0.1 │  │ IP: 10.0.0.2  │  │ IP: 10.0.0.3 │   │ │
         │  │  │ Port 8000 ✅ │  │ Port 5432 ✅  │  │ Port 80 ✅   │   │ │
         │  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘   │ │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 Each container thinks it has its OWN network stack!      │
         │     But they share the same kernel — magic of namespaces.    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.2  How Containers in the Same Pod Share a Network Namespace
         - A Pod is NOT a container — it's a GROUP of containers sharing resources
         - The KEY shared resource: NETWORK NAMESPACE
         - The "pause container" (infra container) — the first container, holds the NS
         - Other containers JOIN the pause container's network namespace
         - Result: All containers in pod share localhost, same IP, same port space!

         [POD NETWORK NAMESPACE — The "Pause Container" Pattern (CRITICAL CONCEPT)]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  POD: anihpj-web (One Pod, Three Containers)                  │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │                                                          │ │
         │  │  ① PAUSE CONTAINER (created FIRST)                       │ │
         │  │     ┌────────────────────────────────────┐               │ │
         │  │     │  Creates & HOLDS the Network NS     │               │ │
         │  │     │  • IP Address: 10.0.0.55            │               │ │
         │  │     │  • Network interfaces: eth0, lo     │               │ │
         │  │     │  • Routing table, iptables rules    │               │ │
         │  │     │  • Just sleeps forever (PID 1)      │               │ │
         │  │     └───────────┬────────────────────────┘               │ │
         │  │                 │ "Join my network namespace!"            │ │
         │  │     ┌───────────┼───────────┐                            │ │
         │  │     │           │           │                            │ │
         │  │     ▼           ▼           ▼                            │ │
         │  │  ② DJANGO      ③ NGINX      ④ LOG SHIPPER                │ │
         │  │  Container     Container    Container                     │ │
         │  │  :8000         :80          (any port)                   │ │
         │  │                                                          │ │
         │  │  ✅ All 3 share: 10.0.0.55 (same IP!)                    │ │
         │  │  ✅ Can communicate via localhost (127.0.0.1)             │ │
         │  │  ✅ Django → Nginx = curl localhost:80 ← SAME MACHINE!   │ │
         │  │  ⚠️  Can't both listen on port 8000 (shared port space!) │ │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 This is why CNI only needs to network the PAUSE          │
         │     container — all other containers automatically           │
         │     inherit its network namespace!                           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [THE PAUSE CONTAINER — Why It Exists]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  "Why not just network the first app container?"             │
         │                                                              │
         │  ❌ PROBLEM: If the first container crashes, the network      │
         │     namespace is destroyed. ALL other containers lose        │
         │     their network! 😱                                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ SOLUTION: The pause container does NOTHING except sleep.  │
         │     It never crashes. It holds the network namespace stable. │
         │                                                              │
         │  The pause container is a tiny (~700KB) static binary that   │
         │  literally just calls: pause() — the simplest C program.     │
         │                                                              │
         │  docker run -d --name pause registry.k8s.io/pause:3.10       │
         │  # That's it. It sleeps forever, holding the network NS.     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.3  How Pods Get Their IP Addresses — IPAM (IP Address Management)
         - IPAM: the process of allocating IPs to pods
         - Cluster-wide Pod CIDR: e.g., 10.0.0.0/16 (65,536 possible pod IPs)
         - Per-node Pod CIDR: e.g., Node1 gets 10.0.0.0/24, Node2 gets 10.0.1.0/24
         - How Cilium does IPAM differently: for Cluster Scope/Multi-Pool/cloud
           modes, the Cilium operator manages allocation via the CiliumNode CRD
           (v2.CiliumNode) instead of relying on kube-controller-manager; Kubernetes
           Host Scope mode is the exception — it still reads the CIDR straight off
           the Kubernetes Node object, just like most other CNIs
         - Cilium IPAM modes (official names): Kubernetes Host Scope, Cluster Scope
           (a.k.a. "Cluster Pool"), Multi-Pool — plus cloud-native modes (AWS ENI,
           Azure IPAM, Google/GKE)

         [IPAM ALLOCATION — How Pod CIDRs are Divided]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  CLUSTER POD CIDR: 10.0.0.0/16 (65,536 total IPs)            │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │                                                          │ │
         │  │  NODE 1: 10.0.0.0/24      NODE 2: 10.0.1.0/24          │ │
         │  │  (256 IPs)                (256 IPs)                      │ │
         │  │  ┌────────────────┐       ┌────────────────┐            │ │
         │  │  │10.0.0.1: Pod A │       │10.0.1.1: Pod E │            │ │
         │  │  │10.0.0.2: Pod B │       │10.0.1.2: Pod F │            │ │
         │  │  │10.0.0.3: Pod C │       │10.0.1.3: Pod G │            │ │
         │  │  │10.0.0.4: FREE  │       │10.0.1.4: Pod H │            │ │
         │  │  │...             │       │...             │            │ │
         │  │  │10.0.0.255: FREE│       │10.0.1.255: FREE│            │ │
         │  │  └────────────────┘       └────────────────┘            │ │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 When a new Pod is scheduled to Node 1:                    │
         │     CNI takes the next FREE IP from Node 1's range            │
         │     (10.0.0.4 in this example)                                │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  IPAM MODE — What Cilium's Official Docs Actually List       │
         │                                                              │
         │  IPAM MODE         SOURCE OF POD CIDR          CONFIG VALUE  │
         │  ───────────────── ─────────────────────────── ──────────────│
         │  Kubernetes Host   Kubernetes v1.Node object   kubernetes    │
         │    Scope           (spec.podCIDR/podCIDRs)                   │
         │                                                              │
         │  Cluster Scope     Cilium operator carves per- cluster-pool  │
         │  ("Cluster Pool")  node CIDRs from a pool                    │
         │                    (default 10.0.0.0/8) into                 │
         │                    CiliumNode CRD                            │
         │                                                              │
         │  Multi-Pool        CiliumPodIPPool CRD; pod/ns multi-pool    │
         │                    picks a pool by annotation                │
         │                    or label selector                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  AWS ENI, Azure    Cloud API assigns real VPC/ eni,          │
         │  (Delegated) IPAM, VNet IPs to pods directly — azure,        │
         │  Google GKE        no overlay needed           gke           │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Only Kubernetes Host Scope sources CIDRs from the         │
         │     built-in v1.Node object. Every other mode is CRD-backed  │
         │     IPAM (CiliumNode/CiliumPodIPPool tracks allocation).     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.4  Service Discovery: How anihpj Finds the Database
         - Kubernetes Services: stable IP (ClusterIP) + DNS name
         - CoreDNS: the cluster's internal DNS server
         - Service DNS format: <service>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local
         - Example: postgres.anihpj.svc.cluster.local → 10.100.50.10
         - Endpoints: the actual pod IPs behind a Service
         - EndpointSlices: scalable endpoint tracking — the DEFAULT mechanism
           kube-proxy/Cilium read since Kubernetes v1.19 GA (a Service can have
           MANY EndpointSlices instead of one giant Endpoints object); the legacy
           Endpoints API still exists for backward compatibility, it's just no
           longer the primary mechanism

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  "Endpoint" Means Two Different Things — Don't Confuse Them  │
         │                                                              │
         │  TERM              WHAT IT MEANS               OWNED BY      │
         │  ───────────────── ─────────────────────────── ──────────────│
         │  Kubernetes        The pod IPs (+ports) that   Kubernetes    │
         │  Endpoints /       back a Service — used for   control       │
         │  EndpointSlice     load-balancing/routing      plane         │
         │                                                              │
         │  Cilium Endpoint   ANY workload Cilium put on  Cilium        │
         │  (CiliumEndpoint   the network — a pod, VM, or agent         │
         │  CRD)              host — plus its assigned                  │
         │                    security Identity used for                │
         │                    policy enforcement                        │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 A Kubernetes "Endpoint" answers "which real IP do I       │
         │     send this packet to?" A Cilium "Endpoint" answers        │
         │     "who IS this, and what's it allowed to talk to?" —      │
         │     every pod is a Kubernetes Endpoints entry on its         │
         │     Service AND a separate Cilium Endpoint at the same       │
         │     time.                                                    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [SERVICE DISCOVERY — Complete Flow from anihpj to PostgreSQL]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  STEP 1: DNS RESOLUTION                                      │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ anihpj Pod (10.0.0.55)          CoreDNS (10.96.0.10)    │ │
         │  │      │                                │                  │ │
         │  │      │ ① DNS Query: "What's the IP   │                  │ │
         │  │      │    of postgres.anihpj.svc      │                  │ │
         │  │      │    .cluster.local?"            │                  │ │
         │  │      │───────────────────────────────▶│                  │ │
         │  │      │                                │ ② Look up        │ │
         │  │      │                                │   Service in     │ │
         │  │      │                                │   API Server     │ │
         │  │      │                                │                  │ │
         │  │      │ ③ DNS Response:                │                  │ │
         │  │      │   "10.100.50.10" (ClusterIP)   │                  │ │
         │  │      │◀───────────────────────────────│                  │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────┘                  │ │
         │                                                               │
         │  STEP 2: TRAFFIC ROUTING (kube-proxy or Cilium)              │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │                                                          │ │
         │  │  anihpj connects to 10.100.50.10:5432                    │ │
         │  │       │                                                  │ │
         │  │       ▼                                                  │ │
         │  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐         │ │
         │  │  │  SERVICE LOAD BALANCING                     │         │ │
         │  │  │                                            │         │ │
         │  │  │  postgres Service → Endpoints:             │         │ │
         │  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐ │         │ │
         │  │  │  │ Pod A    │  │ Pod B    │  │ Pod C    │ │         │ │
         │  │  │  │10.0.0.99 │  │10.0.1.88 │  │10.0.1.99 │ │         │ │
         │  │  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘ │         │ │
         │  │  │                                            │         │ │
         │  │  │  kube-proxy/Cilium picks ONE pod           │         │ │
         │  │  │  and rewrites the destination IP            │         │ │
         │  │  │  10.100.50.10 → 10.0.0.99 (real pod IP!)  │         │ │
         │  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────┘         │ │
         │  │       │                                                  │ │
         │  │       ▼                                                  │ │
         │  │  ┌──────────────┐                                       │ │
         │  │  │ PostgreSQL   │ ← Receives the connection!             │ │
         │  │  │ 10.0.0.99    │                                       │ │
         │  │  │ :5432        │                                       │ │
         │  │  └──────────────┘                                       │ │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.5  kube-proxy Modes and Why Cilium Bypasses It Entirely
         - iptables mode: sequential rule matching — O(n) rules, slow at scale
         - IPVS mode: hash-based lookup — O(1), better but still in netfilter
         - nftables mode: newer kernel framework — O(1) via hash-based "verdict
           maps" (NOT sequential); GA as a supported kube-proxy mode in Kubernetes
           v1.33, purpose-built to fix iptables' O(n) problem
         - Cilium eBPF mode: O(1) hash lookup, kernel-native, NO iptables at all!
         - The performance gap (illustrative, not an official benchmark): at ~5,000
           services, a full iptables-restore resync can run 30+ seconds (kube-proxy
           re-applies the WHOLE ruleset, not just the delta — see table below) while
           Cilium's eBPF map update is near-instant

         [KUBE-PROXY PERFORMANCE — iptables vs IPVS vs eBPF (Cilium)]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  METRIC               IPTABLES      IPVS         CILIUM eBPF │
         │  ───────────────────  ────────────  ───────────  ─────────── │
         │                                                               │
         │  Rule Lookup Type     O(n) linear   O(1) hash    O(1) hash   │
         │  10 Services          ~1ms          <0.5ms       ~0.01ms     │
         │  100 Services         ~10ms         ~1ms         ~0.01ms     │
         │  1000 Services        ~100ms        ~5ms         ~0.02ms     │
         │  5000 Services        ~500ms+       ~25ms        ~0.05ms ⚡  │
         │                                                               │
         │  Rule Update Time     30+ seconds   500ms         INSTANT     │
         │  CPU at 5K Services   80%           40%          5% ⚡        │
         │  Connection Tracking   Yes           Yes          Yes (eBPF) │
         │  L7 Aware?            ❌            ❌            ✅ HTTP etc│
         │  DSR Mode?            ❌            ❌            ✅          │
         │  Observability?       ❌            ❌            ✅ Hubble   │
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 At scale, iptables becomes UNUSABLE. Cilium stays fast.  │
         │     This is why Cilium was built — iptables couldn't handle  │
         │     modern Kubernetes clusters with 1000s of services.       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  KUBE-PROXY NFTABLES MODE — A 4th Mode, Also Fixing O(n)     │
         │                                                              │
         │  nftables mode (kube-proxy) replaced the linear iptables     │
         │  chain walk with an nftables "verdict map" — a single        │
         │  hash-table-backed lookup per packet. Service dispatch is    │
         │  roughly O(1), like IPVS, and updates only touch the         │
         │  Services/endpoints that actually changed (not a full        │
         │  reload). GA as the supported kube-proxy mode since          │
         │  Kubernetes v1.33.                                           │
         │                                                              │
         │  METRIC            IPTABLES/IPVS               NFTABLES      │
         │  Lookup            O(n) / O(1)                 O(1) (map)    │
         │  Update cost       full resync                 delta only    │
         │  Still needs       kube-proxy pod              kube-proxy pod│
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 nftables mode is STILL kube-proxy running in              │
         │     userspace, programming kernel netfilter rules — it       │
         │     narrows Cilium's performance edge but does NOT remove    │
         │     kube-proxy the way Cilium's eBPF replacement does.       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [IPTABLES RULE EXPLOSION — Why It Gets Slow]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  For ONE Service with 3 endpoints, iptables creates:          │
         │                                                               │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │ Chain KUBE-SERVICES (all services)                    │    │
         │  │   ├── Rule 1: match 10.100.50.10:5432 → jump SVC-1  │    │
         │  │   ├── Rule 2: match 10.100.60.20:80   → jump SVC-2  │    │
         │  │   ├── Rule 3: match ...                              │    │
         │  │   ├── ... (5000 more rules!)                         │    │
         │  │   └── Rule 5000: match 10.100.99.99:9090 → jump SVC-N│   │
         │  │                                                       │    │
         │  │ Chain SVC-1 (per-service — 3 endpoints)              │    │
         │  │   ├── Rule 1: 33.3% chance → DNAT to 10.0.0.99:5432 │    │
         │  │   ├── Rule 2: 50.0% chance → DNAT to 10.0.1.88:5432 │    │
         │  │   └── Rule 3: 100% chance  → DNAT to 10.0.1.99:5432 │    │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                                                               │
         │  EVERY new connection walks through 5000+ rules! 🐌          │
         │                                                               │
         │  CILIUM eBPF:                                                 │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │ Hash Table lookup: key(10.100.50.10:5432) →          │    │
         │  │   → [endpoint1, endpoint2, endpoint3]                │    │
         │  │   → Pick one (Maglev hash) → DNAT → DONE            │    │
         │  │                                                       │    │
         │  │ ONE lookup. O(1). No chain walking. ⚡              │    │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 2):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Thinking "each container in a pod has its own IP"
            → TRUTH: All containers in a pod share ONE IP address.
            → FIX: Use different PORTS for different containers in a pod.

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Deleting the pause container manually
            → TRUTH: This destroys the network namespace for ALL pod containers.
            → FIX: Never manually touch the pause container.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Confusing Service ClusterIP with Pod IP
            → TRUTH: ClusterIP is VIRTUAL (no interface has this IP). Pod IPs are REAL.
            → FIX: Service IP only exists in iptables/eBPF rules — not on any interface.

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 2):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. Why do containers in a pod share a network namespace? What are the benefits
        and drawbacks?

    Q2. What is the "pause container" and why is it critical for pod networking?
        A) It runs health checks    B) It holds the network namespace
        C) It proxies network traffic    D) It allocates IP addresses

    Q3. How does CoreDNS resolve postgres.anihpj.svc.cluster.local to an IP address?
        Walk through the complete resolution process.

    Q4. Why does iptables performance degrade significantly with more Services?
        A) iptables runs out of RAM
        B) iptables rules are matched sequentially (O(n) chain traversal)
        C) iptables must reload the kernel module
        D) iptables converts rules to eBPF at runtime

    Q5. What does Cilium's kube-proxy replacement do differently from iptables-based
        kube-proxy? Explain at the architectural level.

    Q6. If pod A (10.0.0.55) and pod B (10.0.1.88) are on different nodes, and
        Service S has ClusterIP 10.100.50.10 with both pods as endpoints, what
        happens when pod C connects to 10.100.50.10? Walk through each step.

    Q7. What's the difference between a Service's ClusterIP and its Endpoints?
        Why does this separation exist?

    Q8. In the pause container pattern, what happens if you manually kill the
        pause container process with `kill -9`?

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 2):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ Linux namespaces (PID, Mount, UTS, IPC, User, NETWORK) give each container
            its own isolated view of interfaces, routing table, and iptables rules —
            the network namespace specifically is what lets every container reuse port
            8000 without conflict.

         ✅ The pause container is created first, holds the pod's network namespace
            (IP, eth0/lo, routes), and does nothing but sleep forever — every other
            container in the pod joins that namespace, so a crash in Django or Nginx
            never takes the network down.

         ✅ IPAM allocates a cluster-wide Pod CIDR (e.g. 10.0.0.0/16) into per-node
            slices (e.g. Node1 gets 10.0.0.0/24); Cilium's official IPAM modes are
            Kubernetes Host Scope (CIDR sourced from the Kubernetes Node object),
            Cluster Scope/"Cluster Pool" and Multi-Pool (both CRD-backed via
            CiliumNode/CiliumPodIPPool), plus cloud-native modes like AWS ENI and
            Azure IPAM.

         ✅ Service discovery resolves a name like postgres.anihpj.svc.cluster.local
            via CoreDNS to a virtual ClusterIP, then kube-proxy (or Cilium) rewrites
            that ClusterIP to one real pod IP drawn from the Service's
            Endpoints/EndpointSlices.

         ✅ iptables matches Service rules sequentially (O(n), 30+ second updates at
            5,000 services) and IPVS improves lookups to O(1) but still lives in
            netfilter — Cilium's eBPF kube-proxy replacement does one O(1) hash-table
            lookup with instant rule updates and no iptables at all.

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         Every mechanism in this chapter — namespaces, the pause container, IPAM,
         DNS-based discovery, and the eBPF kube-proxy replacement — is implemented
         using one underlying kernel technology. Chapter 3 pulls back the curtain on
         eBPF itself: what it is, how it lets Cilium run custom logic inside the
         kernel without kernel modules, and why it underpins literally everything
         Cilium does.
