PART 2: eBPF DEEP DIVE — THE ENGINE BEHIND CILIUM                     🟢 🟡
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Chapter 3: What is eBPF? — The Technology That Makes Cilium Possible
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ What eBPF is and why it's revolutionary (the "JavaScript for Linux" analogy)
         ✅ The history of BPF → eBPF evolution
         ✅ The eBPF program lifecycle: Write → Compile → Load → Verify → JIT → Execute
         ✅ All eBPF hook points in the Linux kernel and what they do
         ✅ How eBPF Maps work (the "database" in the kernel)
         ✅ Why eBPF programs are SAFE (the verifier's role)
         ✅ Why eBPF is so much faster than iptables

    3.1  eBPF Explained Simply — "A Safe Sandbox Inside the Linux Kernel"
         - Analogy: eBPF is like JavaScript for the Linux kernel
         - Just as JavaScript runs safely inside your browser sandbox without crashing your OS...
         - eBPF programs run safely inside the kernel with ZERO risk of crashing it!
         - The "e" in eBPF = "extended" (evolved from classic BPF used by tcpdump since 1992)
         - Key insight: You can CHANGE how the kernel behaves — without changing kernel code!

         📖 Key Term: eBPF (extended Berkeley Packet Filter)
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  eBPF allows you to run SANDBOXED programs in the Linux     │
         │  kernel WITHOUT:                                             │
         │                                                               │
         │  ❌ Changing kernel source code                              │
         │  ❌ Loading kernel modules (no kernel crashes!)              │
         │  ❌ Rebooting the system                                     │
         │                                                               │
         │  ✅ Programs are VERIFIED before running (no infinite loops) │
         │  ✅ Programs are JIT-compiled to native machine code         │
         │  ✅ Programs can access data via eBPF Maps (key-value store) │
         │  ✅ Programs attach to events: packets, syscalls, tracepoints│
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 eBPF turns Linux into a PROGRAMMABLE operating system.  │
         │     Cilium is the biggest user of eBPF in production.        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [THE eBPF ANALOGY — JavaScript for the Kernel]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────────────────┐
         │  BROWSER + JAVASCRIPT           │  │  LINUX KERNEL + eBPF        │
         │                                 │  │                              │
         │  ┌───────────────────────────┐  │  │  ┌─────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │  JavaScript Code          │  │  │  │  eBPF C Code            │ │
         │  │  (your app logic)         │  │  │  │  (your kernel logic)    │ │
         │  └───────────┬───────────────┘  │  │  └───────────┬─────────────┘ │
         │              │                  │  │              │               │
         │              ▼                  │  │              ▼               │
         │  ┌───────────────────────────┐  │  │  ┌─────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │  JS Engine (V8)           │  │  │  │  eBPF VERIFIER          │ │
         │  │  ✅ Checks: no infinite   │  │  │  │  ✅ Checks:              │ │
         │  │     loops, no memory      │  │  │  │     • No infinite loops │ │
         │  │     access violations     │  │  │  │     • Bounds checks     │ │
         │  │     (browser sandbox)     │  │  │  │     • Valid memory      │ │
         │  └───────────┬───────────────┘  │  │  │     • Max instructions  │ │
         │              │                  │  │  │     • No NULL derefs    │ │
         │              ▼                  │  │  └───────────┬─────────────┘ │
         │  ┌───────────────────────────┐  │  │              │               │
         │  │  Browser APIs (DOM, etc.) │  │  │              ▼               │
         │  │  ✅ Can manipulate page   │  │  │  ┌─────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │  ❌ Can't access filesys  │  │  │  │  JIT COMPILER            │ │
         │  └───────────────────────────┘  │  │  │  eBPF bytecode → x86    │ │
         │                                 │  │  │  Runs at NATIVE speed!   │ │
         └─────────────────────────────────┘  │  └───────────┬─────────────┘ │
                                              │              │               │
                                              │              ▼               │
                                              │  ┌─────────────────────────┐ │
                                              │  │  KERNEL HELPER APIs      │ │
                                              │  │  ✅ Read packets         │ │
                                              │  │  ✅ Update maps          │ │
                                              │  │  ❌ Can't crash kernel   │ │
                                              │  └─────────────────────────┘ │
                                              └─────────────────────────────┘

         [BPF HELPER FUNCTIONS — The ONLY API an eBPF Program Has]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │eBPF programs can't call arbitrary kernel functions — only    │
         │a fixed set the kernel exposes as BPF HELPER FUNCTIONS:       │
         │                                                              │
         │  bpf_map_lookup_elem()  — read a value from a map            │
         │  bpf_map_update_elem()  — write a value into a map           │
         │  bpf_redirect()         — send a packet out another interface│
         │  bpf_ktime_get_ns()     — read a monotonic clock             │
         │                                                              │
         │Every helper shares ONE signature: u64 fn(u64 r1..u64 r5) —   │
         │the same calling convention BPF-to-BPF calls use. Which       │
         │helpers you can call depends on your PROGRAM TYPE: tc BPF     │
         │programs can call ~38 helpers; a socket filter far fewer.     │
         │                                                              │
         │💡 This is the ONLY way an eBPF program touches the outside    │
         │   world — no raw syscalls, no arbitrary memory reads, just   │
         │   these kernel-vetted calls the verifier already knows.      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.2  A Brief History: From BPF to eBPF — The Journey
         - 1992: BPF (Berkeley Packet Filter) created by Steven McCanne and Van Jacobson
           → Simple virtual machine for filtering packets (tcpdump -i eth0 'port 80')
         - 1997: Linux gets BPF support for socket filtering (SO_ATTACH_FILTER)
         - 2011: Eric Dumazet adds Linux's first in-kernel BPF JIT compiler (kernel 3.0)
           → Translates bytecode to native instructions instead of interpreting it
         - 2014: Alexei Starovoitov extends BPF → eBPF (11 registers r0-r10, 512-byte
           stack, maps!)
           → Merged into Linux 3.18 — "the biggest kernel innovation in decades"
         - 2016: eBPF gets verifier improvements, map types expanded; Cilium is first
           publicly announced (LinuxCon, Aug 2016) as fast eBPF/XDP container networking
         - 2017: Cilium reorients around Kubernetes — becomes an eBPF-based CNI
         - 2018: Cilium v1.0 ships — Agent/Operator split + CRDs (CiliumNetworkPolicy,
           CiliumEndpoint) become the source of truth
         - 2021: Cilium joins CNCF as an Incubating project (Oct 13, 2021)
         - 2023: Cilium graduates CNCF (Oct 11, 2023) — eBPF is now mainstream
         - Today: Meta, Google, Netflix, Cloudflare all use eBPF in production

         [TIMELINE — BPF/eBPF Evolution 1992→2025]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │ 1992        1997        2011        2014         2016   2023  │
         │  │           │           │           │            │      │    │
         │  ▼           ▼           ▼           ▼            ▼      ▼    │
         │ ┌─────┐   ┌──────┐   ┌──────┐   ┌────────┐  ┌───────┐ ┌────┐ │
         │ │ BPF │──▶│Linux │──▶│JIT   │──▶│ eBPF   │─▶│Cilium │─▶│CNCF│ │
         │ │Born │   │ BPF  │   │BPF   │   │Linux   │  │Born!  │  │Grad│ │
         │ │     │   │port  │   │runs  │   │3.18    │  │       │  │uated│ │
         │ └─────┘   └──────┘   │faster│   │Maps+   │  └───────┘ └────┘ │
         │                       └──────┘   │Verifier│                   │
         │                                  └────────┘                   │
         │  tcpdump    Linux can   BPF gets   The era   First     Cilium │
         │  packet     filter       fast!      of progr-  eBPF-    is    │
         │  filter     sockets                 ammable   based     enter-│
         │  only                               kernel!   CNI!      prise │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.3  How eBPF Programs Work — The Complete Lifecycle
         - Step 1: WRITE (C code → compile to eBPF bytecode with clang/LLVM)
         - Step 2: LOAD (bpf() syscall → kernel verifier checks safety)
         - Step 3: VERIFY (verifier checks: no loops, bounds, valid memory, max 1M instructions)
           → Historical note: originally capped at 4,096 instructions; Linux 5.1
             raised the ceiling to 1 million instructions
         - Step 4: JIT COMPILE (bytecode → native x86/ARM machine instructions)
           → Also ships for arm64/ppc64/s390x/mips64/sparc64; a hardening mode blinds
             constants at JIT time to block JIT-spraying attacks, and
             CONFIG_BPF_JIT_ALWAYS_ON removes the interpreter entirely, forcing JIT-only
             execution
         - Step 5: ATTACH (hook to event: network packet, syscall, tracepoint, kprobe, etc.)
         - Step 6: EXECUTE (kernel runs your program on EVERY matching event!)
         - Step 7: COMMUNICATE (eBPF Maps — share data between kernel and userspace)

         [eBPF PROGRAM LIFECYCLE — Complete Visual Walkthrough]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  ① WRITE                    ② COMPILE                        │
         │  ┌──────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────┐              │
         │  │ eBPF C Code       │─clang▶│ eBPF Bytecode    │              │
         │  │                   │      │ (ELF .o file)    │              │
         │  │ int check_pkt(){  │      │ Instructions:     │              │
         │  │  if(port==5432)   │      │  r1 = ctx        │              │
         │  │    return DROP;   │      │  r2 = *(r1+20)   │              │
         │  │  return ALLOW;    │      │  if r2 != 5432   │              │
         │  │ }                 │      │    goto +2       │              │
         │  └──────────────────┘      │  return DROP      │              │
         │                             │  return ALLOW     │              │
         │                             └────────┬─────────┘              │
         │                                      │ bpf() syscall          │
         │                                      ▼                        │
         │  ③ VERIFY                 ┌──────────────────────────┐       │
         │  ┌────────────────────┐   │  KERNEL VERIFIER          │       │
         │  │ ✓ No loops?        │   │                           │       │
         │  │ ✓ All paths return?│   │  Walks ALL code paths:   │       │
         │  │ ✓ Bounds checks?   │   │  • Simulates execution    │       │
         │  │ ✓ Valid pointers?  │   │  • Tracks register states │       │
         │  │ ✓ ≤ 1M instructions│   │  • Ensures no bad memory  │       │
         │  │ ✓ No unreachable?  │   │  • Proves termination     │       │
         │  └────────────────────┘   └──────────┬───────────────┘       │
         │                                      │ PASS ✅               │
         │                                      ▼                        │
         │  ④ JIT COMPILE            ┌──────────────────────────┐       │
         │                           │  NATIVE MACHINE CODE      │       │
         │                           │  mov rax, [rdi+20]       │       │
         │                           │  cmp rax, 0x1538         │       │
         │                           │  jne .Lallow             │       │
         │                           │  mov rax, 0  ; DROP      │       │
         │                           │  ret                     │       │
         │                           └──────────┬───────────────┘       │
         │                                      │                        │
         │  ⑤ ATTACH                 ▼                                │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
         │  │ HOOK POINTS (where program runs):                 │       │
         │  │  • XDP (NIC driver — earliest point)              │       │
         │  │  • TC Ingress/Egress (Cilium's main hook)         │       │
         │  │  • Socket filter (per-socket)                     │       │
         │  │  • kprobe/tracepoint (any kernel function)        │       │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
         │                                                               │
         │  ⑥ EXECUTE — ⑦ COMMUNICATE via MAPS                          │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
         │  │  KERNEL SPACE          │       USER SPACE          │       │
         │  │                        │                           │       │
         │  │  eBPF Program runs     │  Cilium Agent reads       │       │
         │  │  on EVERY packet       │  from maps via bpf()      │       │
         │  │       │                │       ▲                   │       │
         │  │       │ write          │       │ read              │       │
         │  │       ▼                │       │                   │       │
         │  │  ┌─────────────────────┼───────┼───────────┐      │       │
         │  │  │         eBPF MAPS (shared memory)        │      │       │
         │  │  │  HASH:  pod_ip → identity               │      │       │
         │  │  │  LRU:   connection tracking             │      │       │
         │  │  │  ARRAY: policy rules                    │      │       │
         │  │  │  RINGBUF:events → Hubble               │      │       │
         │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────┘      │       │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [eBPF VERIFIER — Why a Program Gets REJECTED]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  The lifecycle diagram's step 3 (VERIFY) only showed the     │
         │  PASS ✅ path. Here's what FAILS, and why — using the SAME    │
         │  check_pkt() program from that diagram, but mutated:         │
         │                                                              │
         │  ❌ BAD: loop searching for the port, no proven exit:         │
         │    r6 = 0                    // byte offset, starts at 0     │
         │    L1: r2 = *(r1 + r6)       // read packet byte at offset   │
         │        if r2 == 5432 goto DONE                               │
         │        r6 = r6 + 1           // r6 grows... but by how much? │
         │        goto L1          // verifier can't PROVE this ends    │
         │                                                              │
         │  VERIFIER LOG (what the kernel actually reports):            │
         │  "back-edge from insn 8 to 5"      -> unbounded loop found   │
         │  "R6 unbounded, no upper bound"    -> bounds check missing   │
         │  load rejected: bpf() returns -EINVAL                        │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 clang compiled this FINE — rejection happens at LOAD      │
         │     time. The verifier walks every path BEFORE execution, so │
         │     a bad program never runs even once. Fix: bound r6 (e.g.  │
         │     r6 < 64) so the verifier can prove the loop terminates.  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.4  eBPF Hook Points — Where Your Code Runs in the Network Stack
         - XDP (eXpress Data Path): earliest point — at the NIC driver level (before SKB alloc!)
         - TC (Traffic Control): after XDP, before kernel network stack processes
         - Socket hooks: at the socket layer (connect, sendmsg, recvmsg)
         - kprobes/tracepoints: intercept ANY kernel function call — not just packets (e.g. tcp_sendmsg(), sys_execve(), do_sys_open())
         - Note: the kernel ships 18 different BPF program types in total — this chapter
           covers the 4 categories (socket, TC, XDP, kprobe/tracepoint) most relevant to
           Cilium and the CCA exam, not the full list

         [eBPF HOOK POINTS — Complete Network Stack View]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │                    USER SPACE                            │ │
         │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌───────────┐  ┌──────────────────────┐ │ │
         │  │  │ App Pod  │  │ Hubble CLI│  │ Cilium Agent         │ │ │
         │  │  │ (anihpj) │  │           │  │ (reads eBPF Maps)    │ │ │
         │  │  └────┬─────┘  └─────▲─────┘  └──────────▲───────────┘ │ │
         │  └───────┼──────────────┼───────────────────┼─────────────┘ │
         │          │              │                   │               │
         │  ════════╪══════════════╪═══════════════════╪══════════════ │
         │          │   KERNEL SPACE                  │                │
         │          │                                 │                │
         │  ┌───────▼─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┐  │
         │  │  🔗 SOCKET LAYER HOOKS                                  │  │
         │  │  BPF_PROG_TYPE_SOCK_OPS     (TCP congestion control)   │  │
         │  │  BPF_PROG_TYPE_SK_SKB       (socket-level redirect)    │  │
         │  │  BPF_PROG_TYPE_SK_MSG       (socket message filtering) │  │
         │  └───────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │          │                                                   │
         │  ┌───────▼────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  📡 TCP/IP PROTOCOL STACK                               │  │
         │  │  TCP/UDP processing, IP routing, netfilter hooks        │  │
         │  └───────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │          │                                                   │
         │  ┌───────▼────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  🔗 TRAFFIC CONTROL (TC) — CILIUM'S PRIMARY HOOK!       │  │
         │  │  BPF_PROG_TYPE_SCHED_CLS  (TC classifier/action)       │  │
         │  │  ├── TC ingress: packets entering the node              │  │
         │  │  └── TC egress:  packets leaving the node               │  │
         │  │                                                          │  │
         │  │  💡 Cilium attaches its eBPF programs HERE              │  │
         │  │     Every pod packet passes through TC hook             │  │
         │  └───────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │          │                                                   │
         │  ┌───────▼────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  ⚡ XDP (eXpress Data Path) — FASTEST HOOK!             │  │
         │  │  BPF_PROG_TYPE_XDP                                     │  │
         │  │  ├── Runs BEFORE kernel allocates SKB (socket buffer)   │  │
         │  │  ├── Can DROP packets at NIC line rate (10M+ pps!)     │  │
         │  │  └── Used for DDoS protection, load balancing           │  │
         │  │                                                          │  │
         │  │  💡 Not Cilium's default hook (too early — no pod info) │  │
         │  └───────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │          │                                                   │
         │  ┌───────▼──────┐                                           │
         │  │  NIC DRIVER  │                                           │
         │  │  (hardware)  │                                           │
         │  └──────────────┘                                           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [kprobes/tracepoints — The Hook That Isn't in the Packet Path]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  XDP, TC, and Socket hooks above all sit somewhere ON the    │
         │  packet's journey through the stack. kprobes/tracepoints do  │
         │  NOT — they attach to a NAMED kernel function, anywhere in   │
         │  the kernel, packet or no packet.                            │
         │                                                              │
         │  Example targets: tcp_sendmsg() (data leaving a socket),     │
         │  sys_execve() (a process starting), do_sys_open() (a file    │
         │  being opened) — none of these sit on the XDP/TC/socket path.│
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 This is why Cilium's Tetragon component uses kprobes for  │
         │     runtime security (process execs, file access) — a job the│
         │     packet-path hooks (XDP/TC/socket) were never built for.  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.5  eBPF Maps — The "Database" Inside the Kernel
         - Maps are the ONLY way for eBPF programs to persist data
         - And the ONLY way for userspace programs to read eBPF data
         - A single eBPF program can access up to 64 different maps directly
         - Cilium's RINGBUF-based event stream (below) succeeded an older mechanism,
           BPF_MAP_TYPE_PERF_EVENT_ARRAY — both are real kernel map types
         - Map types and what Cilium stores in each:

         [eBPF MAP TYPES — Cilium's Usage]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  MAP TYPE (enum)          CILIUM STORES...     REAL ANALOGY  │
         │  ───────────────────────  ───────────────────  ───────────── │
         │                                                               │
         │  BPF_MAP_TYPE_HASH        Pod IP → Identity    Phone book:   │
         │  (key-value, O(1))        (10.0.0.55→12345)   name→number   │
         │                                                               │
         │  BPF_MAP_TYPE_LRU_HASH    Connection tracking  Recent calls: │
         │  (auto-evicts old)        src:port→dst:port    auto-forgotten│
         │                                                               │
         │  BPF_MAP_TYPE_ARRAY       Policy rules array   Rulebook:     │
         │  (fixed-size array)       indexed by identity  indexed by ID │
         │                                                               │
         │  BPF_MAP_TYPE_LPM_TRIE    CIDR → Identity      Zip code map: │
         │  (longest prefix match)   10.0.0.0/16→cluster   area→region  │
         │                                                               │
         │  BPF_MAP_TYPE_PERCPU_HASH Per-CPU counters     Tally sheets: │
         │  (no locking needed!)     (packet/byte counts)  per-desk     │
         │                                                               │
         │  BPF_MAP_TYPE_RINGBUF     Hubble events →       Event stream: │
         │  (circular buffer)        userspace (flows)     live feed    │
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 Maps are created by userspace (Cilium Agent),            │
         │     written by eBPF programs (kernel), and read              │
         │     by userspace (Cilium Agent / Hubble).                    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [eBPF TAIL CALLS — How Cilium Chains Small Programs Together]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │Tail calls let ONE eBPF program jump into ANOTHER without     │
         │returning — a long jump reusing the same stack frame, not     │
         │a normal function call (so it adds almost zero overhead).     │
         │                                                              │
         │Mechanism: a BPF_MAP_TYPE_PROG_ARRAY map holds key->program   │
         │pairs. bpf_tail_call(ctx, &prog_array, key) jumps straight    │
         │into that program; if the key isn't found, execution just     │
         │falls through to the next instruction — no crash.             │
         │                                                              │
         │Why Cilium needs this: one eBPF program has an instruction    │
         │budget, so Cilium's datapath (bpf_lxc.c, bpf_host.c, etc.)    │
         │decouples policy, NAT, and conntrack logic into SEPARATE      │
         │programs, tail-calling between them stage by stage.           │
         │                                                              │
         │💡 Hard limit: max 33 nested tail calls per call chain — one   │
         │   more official-docs number the CCA exam can test you on.    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    3.6  Why eBPF is Faster Than iptables — The Architectural Difference
         - iptables: sequential rule matching — O(n), check rule 1, then 2, then 3...
         - eBPF: hash table lookup — O(1), directly find the right rule
         - At 1,000 rules: iptables ~10ms, eBPF ~0.01ms (1,000x faster!)
         - At 10,000 rules: iptables ~1,000ms (UNUSABLE), eBPF ~0.02ms (50,000x faster!)
         - Why: iptables walks a linked list. eBPF does a hash table lookup.
         - BONUS: eBPF runs in kernel space with JIT-compiled native code

         [PERFORMANCE DEEP DIVE — Why iptables Doesn't Scale]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  IPTABLES (Sequential Chain Walk)         eBPF (Hash Lookup) │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────┐    │
         │  │ For EACH packet:              │        │ Hash(key) →   │    │
         │  │                               │        │ bucket →      │    │
         │  │ Rule 1: dest 10.0.0.55?  ❌  │        │ value         │    │
         │  │ Rule 2: dest 10.0.0.99?  ❌  │        │              │    │
         │  │ Rule 3: dest 10.0.1.10?  ❌  │        │ ONE lookup!   │    │
         │  │ Rule 4: dest 10.0.1.88?  ❌  │        │ Independent   │    │
         │  │ ...                           │        │ of table size │    │
         │  │ Rule 4999: dest...       ❌  │        └──────────────┘    │
         │  │ Rule 5000: dest 10.0.5.5?✅ │                            │
         │  │                               │        ⏱️ ~0.001ms        │
         │  │ ⏱️ ~500ms for 5000 rules      │        (constant time)    │
         │  │    (linear with rule count)   │                            │
         │  └──────────────────────────────┘                            │
         │                                                               │
         │  📊 ILLUSTRATIVE SHAPE (order-of-magnitude, not exact):       │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │ Service Count   iptables PPS    eBPF PPS     Speedup │    │
         │  │ ──────────────  ──────────────  ───────────  ─────── │    │
         │  │ 1               4,500,000       5,200,000    1.15x   │    │
         │  │ 100             3,200,000       5,100,000    1.59x   │    │
         │  │ 1,000           1,100,000       4,900,000    4.45x   │    │
         │  │ 5,000             120,000       4,800,000    40x ⚡  │    │
         │  │ 10,000              8,000       4,700,000    587x ⚡ │    │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 With 10,000 services, iptables can only process          │
         │     8,000 packets/second. eBPF handles 4,700,000/sec!        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 3):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. What is the eBPF verifier and what 5 things does it check?
    Q2. Name 5 eBPF hook points from earliest to latest in the network stack.
    Q3. How do eBPF programs communicate with userspace? Name 3 map types.
    Q4. Why is an eBPF hash lookup O(1) while iptables is O(n)?
    Q5. What does JIT compilation do for eBPF programs?
    Q6. Why can't you have an infinite loop in an eBPF program?
    Q7. At which hook point does Cilium primarily attach its eBPF programs and why?
    Q8. What happens if the eBPF verifier rejects your program?

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 3):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ eBPF is "JavaScript for the Linux kernel": just as a browser's V8
            engine sandboxes JS so it can't crash the OS, the eBPF verifier
            sandboxes kernel programs so Cilium can change kernel BEHAVIOR
            without touching kernel SOURCE, loading modules, or rebooting.

         ✅ The verifier is what makes eBPF SAFE — before any program runs, it
            walks every code path to prove no infinite loops, enforce bounds
            and pointer checks, cap total instructions at 1M, and guarantee
            the program terminates, rejecting the load if any check fails.

         ✅ Every eBPF program follows the same 7-step lifecycle: WRITE (C via
            clang/LLVM) → LOAD (bpf() syscall) → VERIFY (safety checks) → JIT
            COMPILE (bytecode → native x86/ARM) → ATTACH (to a hook point) →
            EXECUTE (on every matching event) → COMMUNICATE (via eBPF Maps).

         ✅ Of eBPF's hook points, XDP is the earliest and fastest (the NIC
            driver, before the kernel even allocates an SKB) — but Cilium's
            real primary hook is TC ingress/egress, because XDP runs too
            early to know which pod owns the packet, while TC already has it.

         ✅ eBPF beats iptables architecturally: iptables walks its rule chain
            sequentially (O(n) — 5,000 rules ≈ 500ms), while eBPF does one
            hash-table lookup (O(1)) regardless of rule count — at 10,000
            services, iptables collapses to 8,000 pps while eBPF still hits
            4.7 million pps, a 587x real-world speedup.

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         You've now seen eBPF itself — the sandboxed, verified, JIT-compiled
         technology running inside the kernel. Chapter 4 zooms out to the
         full Cilium architecture built on top of it: the Agent (DaemonSet)
         that loads these eBPF programs on every node, the Operator that
         handles cluster-wide bookkeeping, the 9 CRDs that store Cilium's
         state in Kubernetes, and the identity-based networking model that
         turns pod labels — not fragile IP addresses — into the policy
         decisions eBPF enforces on every packet.


Chapter 4: Cilium Architecture — How All the Pieces Fit Together
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ Every component in the Cilium stack and their roles
         ✅ How the Cilium Agent (DaemonSet) manages eBPF programs per node
         ✅ How the Cilium Operator handles cluster-wide tasks
         ✅ What Cilium CRDs extend in Kubernetes
         ✅ Identity-Based Networking — Cilium's secret sauce
         ✅ The complete eBPF datapath — what happens to EVERY packet

    4.1  The Cilium Components — Who Does What?
         - Cilium Agent (DaemonSet): runs on EVERY node, loads eBPF programs, manages pod networking
         - Cilium Operator (Deployment): cluster-wide tasks — IPAM, identity GC, CRD cleanup
         - Cilium CLI: your command center for install, status, troubleshooting
         - Hubble Relay: aggregates flow logs from all agents
         - Hubble UI: beautiful web dashboard for observability

         [CILIUM TOOLING STACK — Beyond Agent & Operator]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CILIUM'S TOOLING STACK — Beyond just Agent & Operator       │
         │                                                              │
         │  The Component Overview docs name a few more moving parts    │
         │  this guide hasn't named individually yet:                   │
         │                                                              │
         │  cilium-cni (the CNI PLUGIN, not the Agent itself)           │
         │  A separate binary kubelet invokes on every pod ADD/DEL. It  │
         │  does NOT program eBPF itself — it calls the already-        │
         │  running cilium-agent's local API to do that.                │
         │                                                              │
         │  cilium vs. cilium-dbg — two DIFFERENT CLIs                  │
         │  cilium = the STANDALONE tool you run from your laptop       │
         │  (kubeconfig-based): install, status, connectivity test.     │
         │  cilium-dbg = ships INSIDE the Agent container; hits the     │
         │  local agent's REST API + raw eBPF maps. Renamed from a      │
         │  plain "cilium" binary in 1.14 to stop the two from being    │
         │  confused.                                                   │
         │                                                              │
         │  Hubble is really 4 parts, not 2                             │
         │  Hubble SERVER is embedded INSIDE every cilium-agent (not    │
         │  its own Pod) — it is what actually reads the eBPF flow      │
         │  data. hubble-relay (a Deployment) aggregates every node's   │
         │  embedded server over gRPC. The hubble CLI can talk to       │
         │  EITHER relay or a single node's embedded server. hubble-ui  │
         │  is the relay-backed graphical map.                          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
