         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CILIUM OPERATOR — Its FULL Job, Per the Docs (Not Just 3)   │
         │                                                              │
         │  This guide's diagram named 3 Operator tasks (IPAM,          │
         │  Identity GC, CiliumNode cleanup). The official Operator     │
         │  internals doc lists 10 duties total — the rest, verified:   │
         │                                                              │
         │  • CRD Registration — creates Cilium's CRDs in the API       │
         │    server on first startup (default behavior)                │
         │  • IPAM — but ONLY in Azure IPAM, AWS ENI, or Cluster        │
         │    Scope (default) modes; in Kubernetes Host-Scope mode      │
         │    the Agent reads podCIDR straight off the Node object      │
         │    instead                                                   │
         │  • LB IPAM — hands out addresses to type:LoadBalancer        │
         │    Services (Ch12.3)                                         │
         │  • KVStore operations — heartbeat + sync, ONLY if the        │
         │    optional external kvstore is enabled                      │
         │  • CiliumEndpoint garbage collection — deletes orphaned      │
         │    CEPs left behind by pods that died uncleanly              │
         │  • Derivative network policy creation — expands cloud-       │
         │    provider constructs like toGroups into plain CNP/CCNP     │
         │    objects                                                   │
         │  • Ingress & Gateway API support — converts                  │
         │    Ingress/Gateway API objects into CiliumEnvoyConfig for    │
         │    the per-node Envoy                                        │
         │  • Mutual Authentication support — registers each Cilium     │
         │    Identity with the certificate management system           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.2  The Cilium Agent Deep Dive — What Happens at Startup?
         - Boot sequence of the Cilium Agent (the "brain" on each node):
           Step 1: Read ConfigMap for configuration
           Step 2: Detect kernel features (does this kernel support eBPF?)
           Step 3: Compile and load eBPF programs into kernel
           Step 4: Connect to Kubernetes API, start watching for pods/services/policies
           Step 5: For each EXISTING pod → program eBPF maps with identity + policy
           Step 6: For each NEW pod event → update eBPF maps in real-time
           Step 7: Start Hubble flow export
           Step 8: Start health checking (cilium status)
           Step 9: Agent is READY — continuously reconciling!

         [CILIUM AGENT STARTUP — What Happens in Sequence]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  Cilium Agent Pod Starts                                     │
         │       │                                                      │
         │       ▼                                                      │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ 1. Read ConfigMap   │  ← /etc/cilium/config              │
         │  │    (cilium-config)  │                                     │
         │  └─────────┬───────────┘                                     │
         │            ▼                                                 │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ 2. Detect Kernel    │  ← Check kernel version,            │
         │  │    eBPF Support     │     available BPF features          │
         │  │  ✓ CONFIG_BPF=y     │                                     │
         │  └─────────┬───────────┘                                     │
         │            ▼                                                 │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ 3. Compile & Load   │  ← clang/LLVM: compiles C→bytecode │
         │  │    eBPF Programs    │     then loads via bpf() syscall   │
         │  │    into kernel      │     Verifier checks → JIT compiles │
         │  └─────────┬───────────┘                                     │
         │            ▼                                                 │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ 4. Connect to K8s   │  ← Watch API for:                  │
         │  │    API Server       │     • Pods (new/deleted/updated)    │
         │  │                     │     • Services (ClusterIP changes)  │
         │  │                     │     • CiliumNetworkPolicies         │
         │  │                     │     • Nodes, Namespaces, Endpoints  │
         │  └─────────┬───────────┘                                     │
         │            ▼                                                 │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ 5. Sync Existing    │  ← For each pod on this node:       │
         │  │    Pods → eBPF Maps │     • Allocate identity             │
         │  │                     │     • Program IP→ID in eBPF map    │
         │  │                     │     • Apply relevant policies       │
         │  └─────────┬───────────┘                                     │
         │            ▼                                                 │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ 6. Start Hubble     │  ← Export flows to Hubble Relay     │
         │  │    Flow Export      │     via eBPF Ring Buffer            │
         │  └─────────┬───────────┘                                     │
         │            ▼                                                 │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ 7. Agent READY!     │  ← Continuously:                    │
         │  │    Reconciling...   │     Watch K8s API for changes       │
         │  │                     │     Update eBPF maps in real-time   │
         │  │                     │     Report health to cilium status  │
         │  └─────────────────────┘                                     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.3  Cilium CRDs — How Cilium Extends Kubernetes
         - Custom Resource Definitions let Cilium store its state in the K8s API
         - CNP (CiliumNetworkPolicy): namespace-scoped L3/L4/L7 policies
         - CCNP (CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy): cluster-wide policies
         - CiliumNode: per-node IPAM allocation state
         - CiliumIdentity: label → numeric identity mapping
         - CiliumEndpoint: per-endpoint (pod) state
         - 9 total CRDs that Cilium registers:

         [ALL CILIUM CRDs — Complete Reference]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CRD NAME                   SCOPE      PURPOSE               │
         │  ────────────────────────   ─────────  ───────────────────── │
         │                                                               │
         │  CiliumNetworkPolicy        Namespace  L3/L4/L7 policies     │
         │  (CNP)                                 for pod communication  │
         │                                                               │
         │  CiliumClusterwide          Cluster    Policies that apply   │
         │  NetworkPolicy (CCNP)                  across ALL namespaces  │
         │                                                               │
         │  CiliumNode                 Cluster    Per-node IPAM state:   │
         │                                         allocated pod IPs     │
         │                                                               │
         │  CiliumIdentity             Cluster    Label set → Numeric ID │
         │                                         e.g., {app:anihpj}→12345│
         │                                                               │
         │  CiliumEndpoint (CEP)       Namespace  Per-pod Cilium state:  │
         │                                         ID, IP, policies,     │
         │                                         networking state      │
         │                                                               │
         │  CiliumExternalWorkload     Cluster    VM/bare-metal nodes    │
         │                                         running Cilium        │
         │                                                               │
         │  CiliumLocalRedirectPolicy  Namespace  Local traffic redirect │
         │  (CLRP)                                (like DNS intercept)   │
         │                                                               │
         │  CiliumBGPClusterConfig     Cluster    BGP topology (v2 CRD)*│
         │                                                               │
         │  CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy  Cluster    Egress gateway config  │
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 Run: kubectl get crd | grep cilium                        │
         │     to see all Cilium CRDs in your cluster!                   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - * CiliumBGPClusterConfig is 1 of a 4-CRD family (+ CiliumBGP
           PeerConfig, CiliumBGPAdvertisement, CiliumBGPNodeConfigOverride)
           that replaced the single CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy CRD — deprecated
           in Cilium 1.18, REMOVED in 1.19. Full breakdown: Chapter 12.2.

         - Not all 9 CRDs are equal: some are POLICIES a human (or a
           GitOps pipeline) writes and applies, while others are pure
           STATE that the Agent and Operator generate on their own —
           nobody hand-writes a CiliumIdentity or CiliumEndpoint for
           the anihpj pods; the Agent creates and reconciles both
           automatically the instant those pods start, overwriting
           any manual edit right back on the next reconcile

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CRD                            CREATED BY                   │
         │  ─────────────────────────────   ─────────────────────────   │
         │                                                              │
         │  CNP (CiliumNetworkPolicy)     You / GitOps — you write it   │
         │  CCNP (Clusterwide policy)     You / GitOps — you write it   │
         │  CiliumNode                    Cilium Operator — auto (IPAM) │
         │  CiliumIdentity                Cilium Agent — auto (labels)  │
         │  CEP (CiliumEndpoint)          Cilium Agent — auto (per pod) │
         │  CiliumExternalWorkload        You — register external node  │
         │  CLRP (LocalRedirectPolicy)    You / GitOps — you write it   │
         │  CiliumBGPClusterConfig        You / GitOps — you write it   │
         │  CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy     You / GitOps — you write it   │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Policy-shaped CRDs (CNP/CCNP/CLRP/BGP/Egress) are         │
         │     hand-authored. State-shaped CRDs (Node/Identity/CEP)     │
         │     are Agent/Operator output — never hand-edit them; the    │
         │     next reconcile just overwrites your change.              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CiliumEndpointSlice — CiliumEndpoint at Scale]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CiliumEndpointSlice (CES) — CiliumEndpoint at Scale         │
         │                                                              │
         │  One more CRD this chapter's original 9 predates: in large   │
         │  clusters, one CiliumEndpoint (CEP) object PER POD floods    │
         │  the API server with watch events.                           │
         │                                                              │
         │  CiliumEndpointSlice batches many CEPs into one CES object.  │
         │  The Operator watches CEPs and groups slim copies of them    │
         │  into CES objects (default cap: 100 CEPs per CES); every     │
         │  OTHER node's Agent then watches CES instead of raw CEPs.    │
         │                                                              │
         │  Tradeoff: far less API-server/etcd load at scale, at the    │
         │  cost of a slightly longer delay before a brand-new pod's    │
         │  identity propagates to every other node.                    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.4  Identity-Based Networking — Cilium's Secret Sauce (CRITICAL CONCEPT!)
         - Traditional networking: rules based on IP ADDRESSES (fragile!)
           • "Allow 10.0.0.55 → 10.0.0.99 on port 5432"
           • Problem: Pod restarts → new IP → rules break!
         - Cilium networking: rules based on POD LABELS (robust!)
           • "Allow {app: anihpj} → {app: postgres} on port 5432"
           • Pod restart = new IP, SAME labels → rules still work!
         - How it works: Labels → hash → numeric identity → stored in eBPF maps
         - The identity is a uint32 in the datapath, but the space Cilium
           actually hands out by default is far smaller: cluster-local
           identities span only 0x100-0xFFFF (16-bit, max 65,536 live
           label-sets per cluster), and non-CIDR identities are capped at
           24 bits so they still fit on the wire in a VXLAN/Geneve header.
           12345 (0x3039) is a real cluster-local value in that range.
         - eBPF maps store: IP → Identity, Identity → Policy (ALLOW/DENY)
         - See it side-by-side: the exact same anihpj → postgres rule,
           written the fragile IP-address way and then the robust
           identity-based way Cilium actually uses

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  BEFORE: Traditional IP-Based Firewall Rule                  │
         │  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
         │  Rule:   ALLOW 10.0.0.55 → 10.0.0.99 : 5432                  │
         │  anihpj pod restarts → new IP 10.0.0.77 assigned             │
         │  Rule still says "10.0.0.55" — that IP is now DEAD           │
         │  Result: traffic DROPS ❌ — admin must rewrite the rule       │
         │                                                              │
         │  AFTER: Cilium Identity-Based Rule                           │
         │  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
         │  Rule:   ALLOW {app: anihpj} → {app: postgres} : 5432        │
         │  anihpj pod restarts → new IP 10.0.0.77 assigned             │
         │  Labels unchanged → SAME Identity 12345 either way           │
         │  Result: traffic ALLOWED ✅ — zero admin action needed        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [IDENTITY-BASED POLICY — Complete Flow from Label to Enforcement]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 1: LABELS → IDENTITY (Cilium Operator)                 │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │                                                         │ │
         │  │  Pod: anihpj-abc123           Pod: postgres-xyz789     │ │
         │  │  ┌──────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────┐     │ │
         │  │  │ app: anihpj      │         │ app: postgres    │     │ │
         │  │  │ component: django│  hash   │ component: db    │ hash│ │
         │  │  │ env: production  │────────▶│ env: production  │────▶│ │
         │  │  └──────────────────┘         └──────────────────┘     │ │
         │  │           │                            │                │ │
         │  │           ▼                            ▼                │ │
         │  │    IDENTITY: 12345              IDENTITY: 67890         │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2: IDENTITY → eBPF MAP (Cilium Agent per node)        │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │  eBPF Map: IP_TO_IDENTITY                               │ │
         │  │  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────┐               │ │
         │  │  │ 10.0.0.55 → 12345│ 10.0.0.99 → 67890│               │ │
         │  │  │ (anihpj pod IP)  │ (postgres pod IP) │               │ │
         │  │  └──────────────────┴──────────────────┘               │ │
         │  │                                                         │ │
         │  │  eBPF Map: IDENTITY_TO_POLICY                           │ │
         │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │ │
         │  │  │ ALLOW: Identity 12345 → Identity 67890 :5432    │  │ │
         │  │  │ DENY:  Identity * → Identity 67890 (default)    │  │ │
         │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 3: PACKET ARRIVES — eBPF enforces!                    │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │                                                         │ │
         │  │  1. Packet arrives: src=10.0.0.55 dst=10.0.0.99:5432  │ │
         │  │  2. Lookup src IP → ID 12345                           │ │
         │  │  3. Lookup dst IP → ID 67890                           │ │
         │  │  4. Lookup policy: 12345→67890:5432 → ALLOW ✅        │ │
         │  │  5. FORWARD packet to PostgreSQL pod                   │ │
         │  │                                                         │ │
         │  │  💡 Pod IP can change — Identity NEVER changes!        │ │
         │  │     anihpj restart = new IP (10.0.0.77)                │ │
         │  │     But labels are same → Identity 12345 → rules work! │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [RESERVED IDENTITIES — Numbers Already Spoken For]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Reserved Identities — the Numbers Below 12345 Already Mean  │
         │                                                              │
         │  Not every numeric identity comes from hashing a pod's       │
         │  labels. Cilium pre-assigns small, fixed IDs (1-255 range)   │
         │  to special, non-pod traffic sources:                        │
         │                                                              │
         │  • 0 reserved:unknown — identity could not be resolved       │
         │  • 1 reserved:host — traffic from the local node itself      │
         │  • 2 reserved:world — anything OUTSIDE the cluster           │
         │  • 3 reserved:unmanaged — endpoint Cilium isn't managing     │
         │  • 4 reserved:health — the local cilium-health probe         │
         │  • 5 reserved:init — pod exists, labels not resolved yet     │
         │  • 6 reserved:remote-node — a node in this or another        │
         │    cluster                                                   │
         │  • 7 reserved:kube-apiserver — reaching the K8s API          │
         │    server                                                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  Where these live: by default (since Cilium 1.9) identities  │
         │  are CiliumIdentity CRDs in the SAME Kubernetes API-server   │
         │  etcd every other object uses — no separate store needed.    │
         │  Only very large clusters opt into identity-allocation-      │
         │  mode=kvstore, a SEPARATE external etcd used just for        │
         │  identities. The architecture diagram's "etcd (CRDs)" box    │
         │  in 4.1 is this default, K8s-native store — not that         │
         │  optional separate one.                                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.5  Cilium's eBPF Datapath — What Happens to EVERY Packet (DEEP DIVE)
         - This is the "hot path" — executed for every single packet
         - The eBPF programs are JIT-compiled to native code
         - Processing happens at TC hook (Traffic Control layer)

         [CILIUM eBPF DATAPATH — Complete Per-Packet Processing Pipeline]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  📨 PACKET ARRIVES (from pod or network)                     │
         │       │                                                      │
         │       ▼                                                      │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ 1. PRE-FILTER                                        │   │
         │  │    Is this packet for a Cilium-managed endpoint?     │   │
         │  │    • Check destination IP against Cilium IPAM        │   │
         │  │    • If NO → let kernel handle it normally           │   │
         │  │    • If YES → continue Cilium processing             │   │
         │  └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                         │ YES                               │
         │                         ▼                                   │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ 2. IDENTITY LOOKUP (eBPF Map: IP_TO_IDENTITY)        │   │
         │  │    • Hash(src IP) → Source Identity (e.g., 12345)    │   │
         │  │    • Hash(dst IP) → Destination Identity (e.g.,67890)│   │
         │  │                                                       │   │
         │  │    If lookup fails → DROP with "unknown identity"    │   │
         │  └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                         │                                   │
         │                         ▼                                   │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ 3. POLICY ENFORCEMENT (eBPF Map: POLICY_RULES)       │   │
         │  │    • Is there a CiliumNetworkPolicy for this dest?   │   │
         │  │    • Check L3/L4: SrcID→DstID:Port allowed?          │   │
         │  │    • Check L7 (if applicable): HTTP method? path?    │   │
         │  │                                                       │   │
         │  │    ┌─────┐                                            │   │
         │  │    │ALLOW?├──NO──▶ DROP packet 🔴                     │   │
         │  │    └──┬──┘           (Hubble: POLICY_DENIED)          │   │
         │  │       │ YES                                           │   │
         │  └───────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │          │                                                   │
         │          ▼                                                   │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ 4. CONNECTION TRACKING (eBPF Map: CT_MAP)            │   │
         │  │    • Is this an established connection?               │   │
         │  │    • If YES → fast-path forward (skip policy check)  │   │
         │  │    • If NO → create CT entry for future packets      │   │
         │  │                                                       │   │
         │  │    💡 CT dramatically improves performance!          │   │
         │  │       Only FIRST packet does full policy check.      │   │
         │  └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                         │                                   │
         │                         ▼                                   │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ 5. FORWARD / DELIVER                                 │   │
         │  │    • Deliver packet to destination pod's interface   │   │
         │  │    • Or forward to next hop (cross-node)             │   │
         │  │    • Apply service load balancing if ClusterIP       │   │
         │  │    • Apply encryption if WireGuard enabled           │   │
         │  └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                         │                                   │
         │                         ▼                                   │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ 6. HUBBLE EXPORT (eBPF Ring Buffer)                  │   │
         │  │    • Send flow event: {src, dst, port, verdict, bytes}│   │
         │  │    • Hubble Relay reads from Ring Buffer             │   │
         │  │    • Available in Hubble CLI and UI                   │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⏱️ STEPS 1-6 complete in ~microseconds (JIT-native code!)  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [HOW L7 POLICY ACTUALLY RUNS — No Sidecar Required]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  How L7 Policy Actually Runs — no Sidecar Required           │
         │                                                              │
         │  Step 3 of the datapath (L7: HTTP method? path?) can't be    │
         │  decided by eBPF alone — eBPF is fast at L3/L4 header        │
         │  matching but doesn't parse HTTP/gRPC/Kafka payloads.        │
         │                                                              │
         │  When a CiliumNetworkPolicy has L7 rules, eBPF               │
         │  transparently redirects JUST that flow's traffic to a per-  │
         │  node Envoy proxy (embedded in the Agent, not a per-pod      │
         │  sidecar) for the L7 decision, then hands ALLOWED traffic    │
         │  back to the normal eBPF path. Flows with no L7 rule never   │
         │  touch Envoy at all — pure eBPF, full speed.                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.6  How Cilium Routes Cross-Node Traffic
         - Two modes for cross-node pod-to-pod communication:
         - Tunnel mode (default): VXLAN or Geneve encapsulation
           • Pod A packet → encapsulate in UDP → send to Node 2 → decapsulate → Pod B
           • Pro: Works on ANY network. Con: Some overhead (headers)
         - Direct routing mode: No encapsulation! Direct L3 routing
           • Requires the underlying network to know pod CIDRs
           • Pro: FASTER (no headers). Con: Requires network configuration
         - Cilium auto-detects the best mode

         [CROSS-NODE TRAFFIC — Tunnel vs Direct Routing]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  TUNNEL MODE (VXLAN/Geneve) — Default                        │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │ Node 1 (10.0.0.1)              Node 2 (10.0.1.1)       │  │
         │  │ ┌──────────┐                  ┌──────────┐            │  │
         │  │ │ Pod A    │                  │ Pod B    │            │  │
         │  │ │10.0.0.55 │                  │10.0.1.88 │            │  │
         │  │ └────┬─────┘                  └────┬─────┘            │  │
         │  │      │                             │                  │  │
         │  │      │ Original: src=10.0.0.55     │                  │  │
         │  │      │           dst=10.0.1.88     │                  │  │
         │  │      ▼                             │                  │  │
         │  │ ┌──────────────────────────┐      │                  │  │
         │  │ │ ENCAPSULATE in VXLAN     │      │                  │  │
         │  │ │ Outer: src=10.0.0.1      │      │                  │  │
         │  │ │        dst=10.0.1.1      │      │                  │  │
         │  │ │ Inner: src=10.0.0.55     │──────┼──────────────────▶│  │
         │  │ │        dst=10.0.1.88     │      │      │           │  │
         │  │ └──────────────────────────┘      │      ▼           │  │
         │  │                                   │ ┌──────────────┐ │  │
         │  │                                   │ │DECAPSULATE   │ │  │
         │  │                                   │ │Extract inner │ │  │
         │  │                                   │ │→ Pod B       │ │  │
         │  │                                   │ └──────────────┘ │  │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                                              │
         │  DIRECT ROUTING — No Encapsulation! ⚡                       │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │ Pod A (10.0.0.55) ──▶ Route table: "10.0.1.0/24 via   │  │
         │  │                        10.0.1.1" ──▶ Pod B (10.0.1.88) │  │
         │  │                                                         │  │
         │  │  No extra headers! Pure L3 routing. Faster!             │  │
         │  │  Requires: Network knows pod CIDRs (BGP, static routes) │  │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Quick reference: the table below distills the same tunnel-
           vs-native tradeoff into a single glance — anihpj's own
           cluster picks Native Routing, as Section 4.7 confirms

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  FACTOR          TUNNEL (VXLAN)      NATIVE ROUTING          │
         │  ──────────────  ──────────────────  ──────────────────      │
         │                                                              │
         │  Extra headers   Yes (VXLAN/UDP)     None (full MTU)         │
         │  Network setup   None required       Must route Pod CIDRs    │
         │  Raw speed       Good                Faster                  │
         │  Any network?    Yes, always         Needs routes/BGP set up │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 anihpj today: Native Routing — the office network         │
         │     already has static routes for every Pod CIDR (§4.7)      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    4.7  Cilium's Datapath Models — The Official Exam Framing
         - The CCA blueprint's Architecture domain (20% weight) explicitly
           lists "Datapath Models" as a required subtopic — this is the
           exam's own vocabulary, not something this guide invented
         - Section 4.5 showed the per-packet PIPELINE (identity lookup →
           policy → conntrack → forward → Hubble export). Section 4.6
           showed the CROSS-NODE encapsulation choice. Neither was framed
           as a "Datapath Model" the way the exam names it — this section
           is that framing, plus a THIRD axis the exam blueprint implies
           but the guide had not yet covered: pod networking device mode
         - 🎯 A "Datapath Model" = the COMBINATION of independent choices
           Cilium lets you make at install time about HOW packets move.
           This is separate from Network Policy (a different exam domain)
           — Datapath Models never change WHAT traffic is allowed, only
           HOW ALLOWED traffic physically travels through the kernel.
         - Three real, independent axes make up a Cilium Datapath Model:
             (a) Encapsulation Mode  — Tunnel vs. Native Routing
                 (already diagrammed in 4.6 — cross-referenced below)
             (b) Pod Device Mode     — veth pairs vs. netkit
             (c) Host-Routing Mode   — Legacy vs. eBPF Host-Routing

         📖 Key Term: Datapath / Datapath Model
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  DATAPATH = the actual code path a packet takes through the  │
         │  kernel and eBPF programs, from "packet arrives" to "packet  │
         │  delivered." Section 4.5's pipeline diagram IS a datapath.   │
         │                                                              │
         │  DATAPATH MODEL = Cilium's own term for WHICH VARIANT of that│
         │  datapath runs on a given node — the combination of          │
         │  encapsulation mode + pod device mode + host-routing mode.   │
         │  Different models trade performance for kernel-version and   │
         │  network requirements — the SECURITY guarantees (identity,   │
         │  policy enforcement) stay IDENTICAL across every combination.│
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 CCA tests "Datapath Models" as an Architecture-domain     │
         │     topic: know the AXES below, not just one diagram.        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [AXIS (a): ENCAPSULATION MODE — Tunnel vs. Native Routing]
         - Fully diagrammed already in Section 4.6 — see that section
           for the VXLAN encapsulate/decapsulate walkthrough; not
           repeated here
         - This axis governs CROSS-NODE pod-to-pod traffic ONLY —
           same-node traffic never leaves the node, so encapsulation
           never applies to it
         - Tunnel (VXLAN/Geneve): zero network config required, small
           per-packet header overhead, works on ANY underlying network
         - Native Routing: no header overhead (full MTU), but the
           underlying network (or BGP — see the BGP exam domain) must
           already know how to route each node's Pod CIDR

         [AXIS (b): POD DEVICE MODE — veth pairs vs. netkit]
         - veth (virtual Ethernet pair): the traditional Linux model —
           ONE veth pair per pod, one end in the pod's network
           namespace, one end in the host namespace. This is what
           every other CNI (Calico, Flannel) also uses
         - netkit: a newer Linux kernel device type built specifically
           for Cilium, meant to replace veth pairs. Requires Linux
           kernel 6.8 or newer (CONFIG_NETKIT=y)
         - netkit's benefit, in Cilium's own words: it "reduces the
           datapath overhead for network namespaces down to zero,"
           aiming for pod traffic to perform as if the workload ran
           directly in the host namespace
         - netkit supports two operating modes: Layer 3 (recommended —
           skips ARP handling entirely) and Layer 2
         - ⚠️ Exam nuance: you CANNOT hot-swap a running pod's veth for
           netkit — the CNI cannot replace a device after the pod
           already exists. Picking netkit is a per-cluster (or
           per-node-pool) INSTALL-TIME decision, and running both
           flavors side-by-side on the SAME node is not supported
         - If the kernel doesn't support netkit (< 6.8), Cilium falls
           back to veth automatically — no error, just the older device

         [veth vs. netkit — Kernel, Performance & Compatibility]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  PROPERTY         veth (legacy)      netkit (newer)          │
         │  ──────────────   ─────────────      ───────────────         │
         │  Min kernel       any supported      ≥ 6.8                   │
         │  Devices/pod      2 (a pair)         1 (single dev)          │
         │  NS-switch cost   normal             near-zero (goal)        │
         │  ARP handling     needed (L2)        skipped (L3, rec.)      │
         │  Host-Routing     optional, see (c)  ALWAYS eBPF             │
         │  Maturity         default, universal Beta (not GA)           │
         │  Hot migration    n/a (default)      NOT supported           │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ anihpj's cluster runs kernel 5.15, below the 6.8 floor —  │
         │     it falls back to veth automatically. No action needed.   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [AXIS (c): HOST-ROUTING MODE — Legacy vs. eBPF Host-Routing]
         - This axis is about HOW a packet crosses from the HOST
           network namespace into a pod's veth (or netkit) device —
           it is what makes netkit fast, but it ALSO exists as a
           standalone speed-up for plain veth pods
         - Legacy routing: the packet still traverses the regular
           Linux network stack, including iptables hooks, on its way
           in/out of the pod — the safe, universal default
         - eBPF Host-Routing: Cilium's own docs describe this mode as
           designed to "fully bypass iptables and the upper host
           stack" for a faster network-namespace switch — same
           identity/policy decisions, fewer stack hops
         - Cilium auto-enables eBPF Host-Routing whenever the running
           kernel supports it — you typically confirm it's active with
           `cilium status` rather than picking it explicitly
         - 💡 netkit ALWAYS rides the eBPF Host-Routing fast path; veth
           pairs can use EITHER legacy OR eBPF Host-Routing depending
           on kernel support — this is what makes (c) a genuinely
           separate axis from (b), not just another name for it

         [CILIUM DATAPATH MODEL — 3 Independent Axes At a Glance]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  AXIS              OPTION A (safer)    OPTION B (faster)     │
         │  ───────────────   ────────────────    ─────────────────     │
         │  (a) Encapsulation Tunnel (VXLAN/      Native Routing        │
         │                    Geneve)             (direct L3)           │
         │  (b) Pod Device    veth (1/pod)        netkit (≥ 6.8)        │
         │  (c) Host-Routing  Legacy (iptables)   eBPF (bypasses it)    │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 These 3 axes are INDEPENDENT — e.g. anihpj today runs     │
         │     Native Routing + veth + eBPF Host-Routing all at once,   │
         │     because kernel 5.15 clears the eBPF Host-Routing bar     │
         │     (≥ 5.10) but not the netkit bar (≥ 6.8).                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Grounding this in anihpj: the cluster runs kernel 5.15.
           Native Routing was chosen at install (the office network
           already routes Pod CIDRs via static routes — see 4.6).
           Kernel 5.15 is new enough for eBPF Host-Routing (≥ 5.10),
           so anihpj's pod-to-pod traffic already skips iptables on
           its way in and out of the pod. But 5.15 is still below
           netkit's 6.8 floor, so every anihpj/jobpost/postgres pod
           still gets a classic veth pair, not a netkit device — a
           future kernel upgrade to 6.8+ is what would unlock netkit,
           with NO change needed to any CiliumNetworkPolicy already
           written, since policy enforcement is identical either way

    🧪 Practice Questions (4.7):
    ─────────────────────────────
    Q1. What are the three independent axes that make up a Cilium
        "Datapath Model," and which CCA exam domain are they tested
        under?
    Q2. anihpj's cluster runs kernel 5.15. Can it use netkit? Can it
        use eBPF Host-Routing? Explain why the two answers differ.
    Q3. Why is "Host-Routing Mode" a genuinely separate axis from
        "Pod Device Mode," rather than just another name for the
        veth-vs-netkit choice?
    Q4. Which single axis governs CROSS-NODE traffic only, and which
        two axes govern a pod's own network-namespace boundary?

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 4):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Thinking Cilium Agent is a Deployment
            → TRUTH: It's a DaemonSet — one per node. Critical distinction!
            → FIX: Check with: kubectl get ds -n kube-system cilium

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Confusing CiliumIdentity with K8s ServiceAccount
            → TRUTH: CiliumIdentity is for network policy. ServiceAccount is for RBAC.
            → FIX: Identity = networking identity. ServiceAccount = API access identity.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Deleting CRDs without understanding Cilium's dependency
            → TRUTH: Deleting CiliumNode or CiliumIdentity CRDs can break networking!
            → FIX: Never manually delete Cilium CRDs. Use cilium uninstall.

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 4):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. What are the 3 main Cilium components? Where does each run?
    Q2. How does identity-based networking differ from IP-based networking?
        Why is it more robust?
    Q3. Name 5 Cilium CRDs and explain their purpose in one sentence each.
    Q4. Walk through the steps when a packet goes through Cilium's eBPF datapath.
        What happens at each step?
    Q5. Where does the Cilium Agent run? (Hint: DaemonSet vs Deployment)
    Q6. What's the difference between tunnel mode and direct routing mode?
        When would you use each?
    Q7. If a pod restarts and gets a new IP, why do existing CiliumNetworkPolicies
        still work?
    Q8. What is the role of the Cilium Operator vs the Cilium Agent?

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 4):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ The Cilium Agent (DaemonSet, one per node) is the component that
            actually loads eBPF programs and programs the maps — boot
            sequence: read ConfigMap → detect kernel eBPF support →
            compile/load eBPF → watch the K8s API → sync existing pods into
            eBPF maps → export Hubble flows. The Cilium Operator (Deployment)
            instead handles CLUSTER-WIDE bookkeeping: IPAM, identity garbage
            collection, and CiliumNode/CRD cleanup.

         ✅ Cilium registers 9 CRDs to store its state in the K8s API — from
            the namespace-scoped CiliumNetworkPolicy (CNP) and
            CiliumEndpoint, to cluster-scoped resources like
            CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy, CiliumNode (per-node IPAM state),
            and CiliumIdentity (the label-set → numeric-ID map).

         ✅ Identity-based networking is Cilium's secret sauce: pod LABELS
            hash into a numeric identity (a uint32 field, but capped at
            65,536 cluster-local values by default) stored in eBPF maps
            (IP→Identity, Identity→Policy), so when a pod restarts and gets
            a brand-new IP, its labels — and therefore its identity and
            every CiliumNetworkPolicy referencing it — stay exactly the same.

         ✅ Every packet runs Cilium's 6-stage eBPF datapath at the TC hook:
            pre-filter → identity lookup → policy enforcement (ALLOW/DENY) →
            connection tracking → forward/deliver → Hubble export — and
            connection tracking means only the FIRST packet of a flow pays
            the full policy-check cost, while every packet after that takes
            the CT fast path.

         ✅ Section 4.7 named the exam's own "Datapath Model" concept: 3
            INDEPENDENT install-time axes — encapsulation (Tunnel/VXLAN vs.
            Native Routing), pod device (veth vs. netkit, which needs kernel
            ≥ 6.8), and host-routing (Legacy/iptables vs. eBPF Host-Routing)
            — that decide HOW allowed traffic physically moves, never WHAT
            is allowed; anihpj's kernel-5.15 cluster clears the eBPF
            Host-Routing bar (≥5.10) but not netkit's (≥6.8), so it runs
            Native Routing + veth + eBPF Host-Routing today.

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         You now understand every piece of Cilium's architecture and how a
         packet actually travels through it. Chapter 5 puts that theory into
         practice: the kernel and Kubernetes prerequisites Cilium needs, the
         different install methods (Cilium CLI, Helm, and the Operator's own
         bootstrap), and a full CLI walkthrough — from `cilium install` to
         confirming Agent, Operator, and Hubble are all healthy with
         `cilium status`.

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Chapter 5: Installing Cilium on Your Kubernetes Cluster
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ Pre-install system requirements (kernel, K8s version, memory, CPU)
         ✅ Three installation methods and when to use each
         ✅ Complete step-by-step install with cilium CLI for anihpj
         ✅ How to verify everything is working (cilium status, connectivity test)
         ✅ Safe CNI migration from Calico/Flannel to Cilium (zero downtime!)
         ✅ What actually changes in your cluster when Cilium is installed

    5.1  Prerequisites Check — Is Your Cluster Ready for Cilium?
         - Linux kernel version ≥ 5.10 (for full eBPF feature support)
         - Kubernetes version ≥ 1.16 (older versions lack required API features)
         - Cilium requires eBPF support in the kernel: /sys/fs/bpf must exist
         - Existing CNI must be REMOVED before Cilium installation
         - kube-proxy can be replaced by Cilium (optional but recommended!)

         [SYSTEM REQUIREMENTS VISUAL — What Cilium Needs]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  LINUX KERNEL         │    │  KUBERNETES               │   │
         │  │  ┌──────────────────┐ │    │  ┌──────────────────────┐ │   │
         │  │  │ ≥ 5.10 (eBPF)    │ │    │  │ ≥ 1.16              │ │   │
         │  │  │ Recommended:     │ │    │  │ Recommended: 1.27+   │ │   │
         │  │  │ 5.15+ or 6.1+   │ │    │  └──────────────────────┘ │   │
         │  │  └──────────────────┘ │    └──────────────────────────┘   │
         │  └──────────────────────┘                                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  HARDWARE PER NODE    │    │  NETWORK REQUIREMENTS     │   │
         │  │  ┌──────────────────┐ │    │  ┌──────────────────────┐ │   │
         │  │  │ RAM: ≥ 4GB       │ │    │  │ No existing CNI!     │ │   │
         │  │  │ CPU: ≥ 2 cores   │ │    │  │ (remove Calico/     │ │   │
         │  │  │ Disk: ≥ 10GB     │ │    │  │  Flannel/Weave      │ │   │
         │  │  └──────────────────┘ │    │  │  first!)            │ │   │
         │  └──────────────────────┘    │  └──────────────────────┘ │   │
         │                               └──────────────────────────┘   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [PRE-INSTALL CHECKLIST for anihpj cluster — Interactive style]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ☐ CHECK 1: Kernel version                                   │
         │     $ uname -r                                               │
         │     Expected: 5.10.0+ or higher                              │
         │     Yours: ______                                            │
         │                                                              │
         │  ☐ CHECK 2: Kubernetes version                                │
         │     $ kubectl version --short                                │
         │     Expected: v1.16.0+                                       │
         │     Yours: ______                                            │
         │                                                              │
         │  ☐ CHECK 3: eBPF filesystem mounted                          │
         │     $ ls /sys/fs/bpf                                         │
         │     Expected: directory exists (may be empty)                │
         │     Yours: ______                                            │
         │                                                              │
         │  ☐ CHECK 4: NO existing CNI configs                          │
         │     $ ls /etc/cni/net.d/                                     │
         │     Expected: EMPTY (or doesn't exist)                       │
         │     ⚠️  If files exist → you need to remove old CNI first!   │
         │                                                              │
         │  ☐ CHECK 5: kube-proxy detection                             │
         │     $ kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep kube-proxy     │
         │     Note: Cilium CAN replace kube-proxy (recommended!)      │
         │                                                              │
         │  ☐ CHECK 6: Node resources                                   │
         │     $ free -h          (RAM: ≥ 4GB)                          │
         │     $ nproc            (CPU: ≥ 2 cores)                     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.2  Installation Methods Compared — Choose Your Path
         - Method 1: cilium CLI — wraps Helm, simplest, auto-detects environment
         - Method 2: Helm chart — full control, GitOps-friendly, version pinning
         - Method 3: Cloud marketplace — EKS add-on, GKE Dataplane V2, AKS BYOCNI

         [INSTALLATION METHOD COMPARISON — Decision Matrix]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                               │
         │  METHOD         BEST FOR           PROS            CONS       │
         │  ─────────────  ─────────────────  ──────────────  ────────── │
         │                                                               │
         │  ┌──────────┐                                               │
         │  │ cilium   │  First-time users,  One command!     Less      │
         │  │ CLI      │  learning, quick    Auto-detects     control   │
         │  │ 🟢 EASY  │  setups             best defaults    over      │
         │  └──────────┘                                           │
         │                                                               │
         │  ┌──────────┐                                               │
         │  │ Helm     │  Production,         Full control,    More     │
         │  │ Chart    │  GitOps (ArgoCD),    version pinning, complex,  │
         │  │ 🟡 MED   │  repeatable deploys  values.yaml      100+     │
         │  └──────────┘                                        options  │
         │                                                               │
         │  ┌──────────┐                                               │
         │  │ Cloud    │  EKS/GKE/AKS         Managed by       Vendor   │
         │  │ Market-  │  users, no           cloud provider,  lock-in, │
         │  │ place    │  CLI needed          integrated       less     │
         │  │ 🔴 EASY  │                      with platform     control │
         │  └──────────┘                                               │
         │                                                               │
         │  💡 RECOMMENDATION: Start with cilium CLI for learning.       │
         │     Move to Helm for production.                              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.3  Hands-On: Installing Cilium with cilium CLI — Full Walkthrough
         - We'll install Cilium WITH all features enabled for maximum learning
         - Features enabled: kube-proxy replacement, Hubble (CLI+Relay+UI), WireGuard encryption

         [CILIUM INSTALL — What Actually Happens Under the Hood]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  "cilium install" does ALL of this automatically:            │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ 1. Creates namespace or uses kube-system                │ │
         │  │ 2. Creates Cilium ConfigMap (cilium-config)             │ │
         │  │ 3. Creates Cilium DaemonSet (Agent on every node!)      │ │
         │  │ 4. Creates Cilium Operator Deployment                   │ │
         │  │ 5. Creates Cilium CRDs (9 Custom Resource Definitions)  │ │
         │  │ 6. Creates RBAC (ServiceAccount, ClusterRole, Binding)  │ │
         │  │ 7. Creates Hubble Relay + UI Deployments                │ │
         │  │ 8. Creates Hubble Peer Service (for Relay↔Agent comm)  │ │
         │  │ 9. Waits for all pods to be Ready                       │ │
         │  │ 10. Runs cilium connectivity test (21+ tests!)          │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [INSTALL CILIUM FOR anihpj — Complete Command Sequence with Annotations]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  # ═══ STEP 1: Download cilium CLI ═══                       │
         │  CILIUM_CLI_VERSION=$(curl -s https://raw.githubusercontent.│
         │  com/cilium/cilium-cli/main/stable.txt)                      │
         │  curl -L --remote-name-all https://github.com/cilium/        │
         │  cilium-cli/releases/download/${CILIUM_CLI_VERSION}/         │
         │  cilium-linux-amd64.tar.gz                                   │
         │  sudo tar xzvfC cilium-linux-amd64.tar.gz /usr/local/bin    │
         │  # ↑ Downloads the cilium binary to /usr/local/bin           │
         │                                                              │
         │  # ═══ STEP 2: Install Cilium (ALL features ON!) ═══         │
         │  cilium install \                                            │
         │    --namespace kube-system \                                 │
         │    --set kubeProxyReplacement=true \    # ← Replace kube-    │
         │    --set hubble.enabled=true \          #    proxy! No more  │
         │    --set hubble.relay.enabled=true \    #    iptables rules! │
         │    --set hubble.ui.enabled=true \       # ← Hubble Web UI   │
         │    --set encryption.enabled=true \      # ← Transparent     │
         │    --set encryption.type=wireguard      #    WireGuard enc. │
         │                                                              │
         │  # This single command replaces:                             │
         │  #   ❌ Old CNI (Calico/Flannel)                             │
         │  #   ❌ kube-proxy (iptables burden)                         │
         │  #   ❌ Istio sidecar (service mesh proxy)                   │
         │  #   ✅ With ONE unified eBPF layer!                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  # ═══ STEP 3: Wait for Cilium to be READY ═══               │
         │  cilium status --wait                                        │
         │  # ↑ Blocks until all Cilium pods are healthy                │
         │  #   (typically 1-2 minutes)                                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  # ═══ STEP 4: Run connectivity tests ═══                    │
         │  cilium connectivity test                                    │
         │  # ↑ Runs 21+ tests: pod-to-pod, service, DNS,               │
         │  #   network policy, encryption, Hubble, etc.               │
         │  #   All should show ✅ green checkmarks!                    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.4  Verifying Your Installation — What Success Looks Like
         - cilium status — the health dashboard in your terminal
         - kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep cilium — should show all Running
         - cilium endpoint list — should list every pod on every node
         - cilium connectivity test — 21+ green checkmarks = everything works!

         [VERIFICATION — What to Look For After Install]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  $ cilium status                                             │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │    /¯¯\                                              │ │
         │  │   /¯¯\__/¯¯\    Cilium:         OK                  │ │
         │  │   \__/¯¯\__/    Operator:       OK                  │ │
         │  │   /¯¯\__/¯¯\    Envoy DaemonSet:disabled (not used) │ │
         │  │   \__/¯¯\__/    Hubble Relay:   OK                  │ │
         │  │   \__/          ClusterMesh:    disabled            │ │
         │  │                 Encryption:     OK (WireGuard)      │ │
         │  │                                                     │ │
         │  │  DaemonSet         cilium      Desired: 3, Ready: 3│ │
         │  │  Deployment        cilium-operator   Ready: 1/1    │ │
         │  │  Deployment        hubble-relay      Ready: 1/1    │ │
         │  │  Deployment        hubble-ui         Ready: 1/1    │ │
         │  │  Containers:       cilium            Running: 3    │ │
         │  │  Cluster Pods:     12/12 connected successfully     │ │
         │  │  Image versions    cilium             v1.16.0       │ │
         │  │                    cilium-operator    v1.16.0       │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ GREEN CHECKMARKS: Everything is healthy!                  │
         │  🔴 RED Xs: Something is wrong — check cilium status --verbose│
         │  🟡 YELLOW WARNINGS: Minor — still functional but not optimal│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [WHAT cilium connectivity test CHECKS — 21+ Tests Visualized]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  TEST CATEGORY       SPECIFIC TESTS (21+)                    │
         │  ──────────────────  ─────────────────────────────────────── │
         │  📦 Pod-to-Pod        [✅] Same node communication            │
         │                       [✅] Cross-node communication          │
         │                       [✅] Pod-to-pod (no service)            │
         │                                                              │
         │  🌐 Services           [✅] ClusterIP (internal LB)            │
         │                       [✅] NodePort (external access)         │
         │                                                              │
         │  🔍 DNS               [✅] CoreDNS resolution                 │
         │                       [✅] FQDN resolution                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  🛡️ Network Policy    [✅] Allow all (default)                │
         │                       [✅] Deny all + specific allow          │
         │                       [✅] L7 HTTP policy                     │
         │                                                              │
         │  🔒 Encryption        [✅] WireGuard encrypted traffic        │
         │                       [✅] Cross-node encrypted               │
         │                                                              │
         │  🔭 Hubble            [✅] Hubble Relay functional            │
         │                       [✅] Hubble UI accessible               │
         │                       [✅] Hubble CLI shows flows             │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚡ Performance       [✅] Bandwidth test                      │
         │                       [✅] Latency test                       │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 If ANY test fails → Cilium has a problem to fix!         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.5  Uninstalling an Old CNI — Safe Migration from Calico/Flannel
         - Why you can't run two CNIs at once: they both try to assign IPs → conflicts!
         - Safe migration: backup everything → drain node → remove old CNI → install Cilium
         - Migration strategy diagram:

         [CNI MIGRATION — Safe Migration Step-by-Step Flowchart]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  PHASE 1: PREPARE                                            │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ ① kubectl get pods -A -o wide > pods-backup.txt        │ │
         │  │ ② kubectl get svc,deploy,ds -A -o yaml > k8s-backup/   │ │
         │  │ ③ IDENTIFY current CNI:                                │ │
         │  │    ls /etc/cni/net.d/  →  "10-calico.conflist"         │ │
         │  │    → Current CNI is Calico                              │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │            │                                                 │
         │            ▼                                                 │
         │  PHASE 2: DRAIN                                              │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ ④ kubectl drain <node> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-   │ │
         │  │    emptydir-data                                       │ │
         │  │    → All pods move to other nodes                       │ │
         │  │    → Node is now empty (except DaemonSets)              │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │            │                                                 │
         │            ▼                                                 │
         │  PHASE 3: REMOVE OLD CNI                                     │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ ⑤ kubectl delete -f <old-cni-manifest>                 │ │
         │  │    OR helm uninstall calico / flannel                   │ │
         │  │ ⑥ sudo rm -f /etc/cni/net.d/*                          │ │
         │  │ ⑦ sudo ip link delete cni0 2>/dev/null                 │ │
         │  │ ⑧ sudo ip link delete flannel.1 2>/dev/null            │ │
         │  │    → Node has NO CNI — pods can't get network!          │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │            │                                                 │
         │            ▼                                                 │
         │  PHASE 4: INSTALL CILIUM + UNCORDON                           │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ ⑨ cilium install ... (as shown in 5.3)                 │ │
         │  │ ⑩ cilium status --wait                                 │ │
         │  │ ⑪ kubectl uncordon <node>                              │ │
         │  │    → Node is back! Cilium now manages networking        │ │
         │  │ ⑫ Repeat steps ④-⑪ for each node!                     │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 TIP: In production, drain ONE node at a time.            │
         │     Your apps stay available on other nodes!                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [BEFORE/AFTER — Cluster CNI Migration Visualization]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  BEFORE MIGRATION                  AFTER MIGRATION           │
         │  ┌────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────┐    │
         │  │   CALICO CLUSTER    │           │   CILIUM CLUSTER    │    │
         │  │                    │           │                    │    │
         │  │ Node1: Calico pod  │           │ Node1: Cilium pod  │    │
         │  │ Node2: Calico pod  │  ──────▶  │ Node2: Cilium pod  │    │
         │  │ Node3: Calico pod  │           │ Node3: Cilium pod  │    │
         │  │                    │           │                    │    │
         │  │ CNI: iptables      │           │ CNI: eBPF ⚡       │    │
         │  │ NetPol: IP-based   │           │ NetPol: Identity   │    │
         │  │ No Hubble          │           │ Hubble enabled     │    │
         │  │ No encryption      │           │ WireGuard on       │    │
         │  └────────────────────┘           └────────────────────┘    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 5):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Installing Cilium without removing old CNI
            → RESULT: Two CNIs fight over IP allocation — pods stay Pending!
            → FIX: Always remove old CNI before installing Cilium.

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Forgetting --wait flag on cilium status
            → RESULT: You think it's installed but pods are still starting.
            → FIX: Always cilium status --wait before proceeding.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Not running connectivity test after install
            → RESULT: Everything looks green but something is silently broken.
            → FIX: Always cilium connectivity test after ANY Cilium change.

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 5):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. What is the minimum Linux kernel version for full Cilium eBPF support?
    Q2. What single flag replaces kube-proxy with Cilium's eBPF load balancer?
    Q3. What does `cilium connectivity test` verify? Name 5 categories it tests.
    Q4. Can you run Cilium and Calico simultaneously on the same cluster? Why or why not?
    Q5. How do you enable Hubble (CLI + Relay + UI) during Cilium installation?
    Q6. In the CNI migration process, why do you drain nodes one at a time?
    Q7. What happens to running pods when you delete the old CNI config from /etc/cni/net.d/?
    Q8. After running `cilium install`, what 10 things happen behind the scenes?

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 5):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ Cilium needs a Linux kernel ≥ 5.10 (for full eBPF support) and
            Kubernetes ≥ 1.16 — Section 5.1's six-point checklist confirms
            both, plus that any existing CNI is fully removed, before you run
            cilium install.

         ✅ Three install paths exist: the cilium CLI (auto-detects defaults,
            best for first-time/learning setups), the Helm chart (full
            control, version-pinned, GitOps/ArgoCD-friendly), and a cloud
            marketplace add-on (EKS/GKE/AKS, managed but vendor-locked).

         ✅ The Section 5.3 walkthrough — cilium install --set
            kubeProxyReplacement=true --set hubble.enabled=true --set
            encryption.type=wireguard — silently performs 10 steps (ConfigMap,
            DaemonSet, Operator, 9 CRDs, RBAC, Hubble Relay/UI) before cilium
            status --wait unblocks.

         ✅ A healthy install shows green checkmarks in cilium status
            (Cilium/Operator/Hubble Relay all OK) and 21+ passing categories
            in cilium connectivity test — pod-to-pod, Services, DNS, network
            policy, encryption, and Hubble.

         ✅ Migrating off Calico/Flannel is a 4-phase, one-node-at-a-time
            process — back up manifests, drain the node, delete the old CNI's
            configs/interfaces, then install Cilium and uncordon — since two
            CNIs can never safely share a node.
