    ─────────────────────────
         With Cilium installed and verified, Chapter 6 hands you the cilium
         CLI as your daily command center — the health dashboard in cilium
         status, the full command reference for endpoints/policies/services,
         and cilium monitor for watching packets drop or forward in real time.
         You'll also learn cilium config, the workflow for safely reading and
         durably changing Cilium's runtime configuration through Helm.


Chapter 6: Cilium CLI — Your Command Center for Daily Operations
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ Master cilium status — the health dashboard
         ✅ Essential daily commands (endpoint, policy, service, identity)
         ✅ cilium monitor — real-time packet visibility
         ✅ cilium config — viewing and changing configuration
         ✅ How to debug: "Is my pod networked? Is my policy active?"

    6.1  cilium status — Your First Command Every Morning
         - The "health dashboard" that tells you everything in one view
         - Sections: Component Health (Cilium/Operator/Hubble Relay/Hubble
           UI/ClusterMesh/Encryption — 6 lines, answers Q1), Deployment
           Rollout (DaemonSet/Deployment/Containers/Cluster Pods), Image
           Versions
         - Key indicators: green check = healthy, red X = broken, yellow = degraded

         [cilium status — ANNOTATED OUTPUT BREAKDOWN]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  $ cilium status                                             │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │    /¯¯\                                               │ │
         │  │   /¯¯\__/¯¯\    Cilium:          OK  ← Main agent     │ │
         │  │   \__/¯¯\__/    Operator:        OK  ← Cluster tasks │ │
         │  │   /¯¯\__/¯¯\    Hubble Relay:    OK  ← Flow aggregator│ │
         │  │   \__/¯¯\__/    Hubble UI:       OK  ← Web dashboard │ │
         │  │   \__/          ClusterMesh:  disabled  ← Multi-clstr│ │
         │  │                 Encryption:      OK  ← WireGuard     │ │
         │  │                                                      │ │
         │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ │
         │  │  │ DaemonSet    cilium      Desired: 3, Ready: 3  │ │ │
         │  │  │ Deployment   cilium-op   Ready: 1/1            │ │ │
         │  │  │ Deployment   hubble-rly  Ready: 1/1            │ │ │
         │  │  │ Deployment   hubble-ui   Ready: 1/1            │ │ │
         │  │  │ Containers:  cilium      Running: 3             │ │ │
         │  │  │ Cluster Pods: 12/12 connected successfully      │ │ │
         │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ │
         │  │                                                      │ │
         │  │  Image versions:                                     │ │
         │  │    cilium             quay.io/cilium/cilium:v1.16.0 │ │
         │  │    cilium-operator    quay.io/cilium/operator:v1.16 │ │
         │  │    hubble-relay       quay.io/cilium/hubble-relay   │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  🔴 RED FLAGS to watch for:                                 │
         │    • "Cilium: Warning" → Agent degraded                      │
         │    • "Desired: 3, Ready: 1" → 2 agents not starting          │
         │    • "Cluster Pods: 8/12" → 4 pods not managed by Cilium    │
         │    • "Encryption: Disabled" → WireGuard not working          │
         │    • Errors section at bottom → specific failure messages    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.2  Essential cilium Commands — Your Daily Toolkit
         - Organized by what you actually need to do day-to-day

         [COMPLETE cilium COMMAND REFERENCE — Organized by Task]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  🔍 INSPECT & VERIFY                                         │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ cilium status          Overall health of Cilium         │ │
         │  │ cilium status --wait    Block until all healthy          │ │
         │  │ cilium version          Client + daemon versions        │ │
         │  │ cilium node list        All nodes + Cilium status       │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  📦 POD MANAGEMENT                                           │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ cilium endpoint list    Every pod Cilium manages         │ │
         │  │ cilium endpoint health <id>  View endpoint health        │ │
         │  │ cilium endpoint get <id> Get specific endpoint by ID     │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  🛡️ POLICY MANAGEMENT                                        │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ kubectl apply -f <file>  Create/update a CNP or CCNP    │ │
         │  │ kubectl get cnp / ccnp   List policies (ns / cluster)    │ │
         │  │ kubectl delete cnp <name>  Delete a policy               │ │
         │  │ cilium-dbg policy get    Agent view (DEPRECATED)         │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚖️ SERVICE & LB MANAGEMENT                                  │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ cilium service list     All Services (eBPF LB view)      │ │
         │  │ cilium bpf lb list      Low-level eBPF LB entries        │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  🏷️ IDENTITY MANAGEMENT                                      │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ cilium identity list    All identities in cluster        │ │
         │  │ cilium identity get <id> Get details of identity        │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  🔍 REAL-TIME MONITORING                                     │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ cilium monitor          Live packet-level view            │ │
         │  │ cilium metrics list     Available Prometheus metrics     │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  🔒 ENCRYPTION                                               │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ cilium encrypt status   WireGuard/IPSec status           │ │
         │  │ cilium encrypt flush    Refresh all encryption keys      │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  🌍 CLUSTER MESH                                             │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ cilium clustermesh status   Mesh connection status       │ │
         │  │ cilium clustermesh connect  Connect two clusters         │ │
         │  │ cilium clustermesh disconnect Disconnect clusters        │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  🩺 DEBUG & COLLECT                                          │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ cilium-dbg debuginfo    Text/JSON dump (agent-side)    │ │
         │  │ cilium-bugtool          Full diag tarball (own binary) │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [cilium vs cilium-dbg — Which Binary Actually Runs Each Command]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TWO SEPARATE BINARIES BOTH GET CALLED "cilium":             │
         │                                                              │
         │  1) The CLIENT tool (Cilium CLI, github.com/cilium/          │
         │     cilium-cli) — installed on YOUR laptop/bastion.          │
         │     It talks to the K8s API to manage the install:           │
         │     status, version, install, upgrade, uninstall,            │
         │     connectivity test, hubble ui, sysdump.                   │
         │                                                              │
         │  2) cilium-dbg — the debug CLI baked into the Agent          │
         │     container itself. Everything this chapter calls          │
         │     "cilium endpoint/policy/service/identity/                │
         │     monitor/config/debuginfo/node" is ACTUALLY               │
         │     cilium-dbg, reached only via kubectl exec:               │
         │                                                              │
         │     $ kubectl -n kube-system exec ds/cilium -- \             │
         │         cilium-dbg endpoint list                             │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Typing "cilium endpoint list" on your laptop              │
         │     fails with "unknown command" — that binary only          │
         │     knows install/status/lifecycle commands. The             │
         │     per-Agent internals need cilium-dbg + kubectl exec.      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.3  cilium monitor — Your Real-Time Packet Detective
         - See every packet DROP, FORWARD, and AUDIT in real-time
         - Filter by: pod, namespace, verdict, protocol

         [cilium monitor — ANNOTATED OUTPUT EXAMPLES]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  $ cilium monitor --type drop                                │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ Listening for events on 4 CPUs with 64x4096 of shared  │ │
         │  │ memory                                                  │ │
         │  │                                                        │ │
         │  │ ⬇ DROP:                                                 │ │
         │  │   FROM: anihpj/anihpj-abc123 (ID:12345)                │ │
         │  │   TO:   kube-system/coredns-xyz (ID:1)                 │ │
         │  │   PORT: 53/UDP                                          │ │
         │  │   REASON: Policy denied                                 │ │
         │  │   ↑ "anihpj pod is trying DNS but policy blocks it!"   │ │
         │  │                                                        │ │
         │  │ ⬇ DROP:                                                 │ │
         │  │   FROM: default/hacker-pod (ID:99999)                  │ │
         │  │   TO:   anihpj/postgres-0 (ID:67890)                   │ │
         │  │   PORT: 5432/TCP                                        │ │
         │  │   REASON: Policy denied                                 │ │
         │  │   ↑ "Unknown pod trying to reach the database —        │ │
         │  │      BLOCKED! ✅"                                       │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 USEFUL FILTERS (add to cilium monitor):                  │
         │    --type drop             Only dropped packets              │
         │    --type policy-verdict   Only policy decisions             │
         │    --type trace            Packet forwarding trace events    │
         │    --type l7               L7 (HTTP/DNS/Kafka) visibility    │
         │    --from <endpoint-id>    Only FROM that source endpoint    │
         │    --to <endpoint-id>      Only TO that destination endpoint │
         │    --related-to <ep-id>    Traffic to/from that endpoint     │
         │    --json                  Machine-readable JSON output      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.4  cilium config — Viewing and Modifying Runtime Configuration
         - Full command: kubectl -n kube-system exec ds/cilium -- cilium-dbg config
           (this guide shortens it to "cilium config" below, matching 6.1-6.3's style)
         - Config is NOT invented per-query — it's read from the cilium-config
           ConfigMap in kube-system, generated ONCE at install/upgrade time from
           your Helm values (the same cilium install / cilium upgrade from Ch5)
         - Two views: bare "cilium config" = the small set of LIVE-toggleable
           debug flags; "cilium config -a" = the full dump, including feature
           flags fixed at install time (kube-proxy-replacement, hubble.enabled...)
         - The ONLY supported way to permanently change a feature flag: edit a
           Helm value, then run cilium upgrade — never patch the ConfigMap directly
         - ⚠️  cilium upgrade rolls every Agent pod to apply Helm changes — treat
           it like any DaemonSet rollout and schedule it on a real cluster

         [cilium config — Two Views: Live Toggles vs. Full Dump]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  VIEW 1 — bare: the small set of flags you CAN flip on       │
         │  a live Agent, no restart required:                          │
         │                                                              │
         │  $ cilium config                                             │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │  Debug                    Disabled                      │ │
         │  │  ConntrackAccounting      Enabled                       │ │
         │  │  PolicyTracing            Disabled                      │ │
         │  │  MonitorAggregationLevel  Medium                        │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 cilium config Debug=true flips THIS flag live — it        │
         │     is NOT written to the ConfigMap and resets on the        │
         │     next Agent pod restart.                                  │
         │                                                              │
         │  VIEW 2 — with -a/--all: EVERY current setting, incl.        │
         │  the feature flags fixed at install/upgrade time:            │
         │                                                              │
         │  $ cilium config -a | grep -E "kube-proxy|hubble"            │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │  kube-proxy-replacement    true                         │ │
         │  │  enable-hubble             true                         │ │
         │  │  enable-hubble-open-metrics false                       │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 These three did NOT come from a live toggle — they        │
         │     were baked into the cilium-config ConfigMap at           │
         │     install/upgrade time. -a only lets you READ them         │
         │     here; changing them needs Helm (see below).              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [Changing Cilium Config — The Supported Path vs. Two Risky Ones]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ✅ SUPPORTED: edit a Helm value, then cilium upgrade         │
         │     --set <key>=<value>. Re-renders the SAME Helm            │
         │     release — ConfigMap AND every Agent pod get              │
         │     recreated from the new values. Durable, tracked.         │
         │                                                              │
         │  ❌ RISKY #1: cilium config <option>=enable|disable           │
         │     Only affects the small live-toggle set (Debug,           │
         │     PolicyTracing, etc). Cannot touch kube-proxy-            │
         │     replacement or hubble.enabled at all, and whatever       │
         │     it DOES change vanishes on the next pod restart.         │
         │                                                              │
         │  ❌ RISKY #2: kubectl edit configmap cilium-config -n         │
         │     kube-system. Persists — but bypasses Helm entirely.      │
         │     Needs a manual rollout to take effect, and the NEXT      │
         │     cilium upgrade / helm upgrade can silently revert        │
         │     it back to whatever Helm's tracked values say.           │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 EXAM ANGLE: the blueprint tests whether you know          │
         │     Helm-value-then-upgrade is the supported path — not      │
         │     just that "a command to change config" exists.           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [WORKED EXAMPLE — Durably Turning On Hubble Open Metrics]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  STEP 1 — Check what's on right now:                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  $ cilium config -a | grep -E "kube-proxy|hubble"            │
         │  kube-proxy-replacement       true                           │
         │  enable-hubble                true                           │
         │  enable-hubble-open-metrics   false                          │
         │                                                              │
         │  ↑ kube-proxy-replacement and Hubble are ON already.         │
         │    Open metrics (Prometheus scraping of Hubble flow          │
         │    data, for anihpj's Grafana dashboards) is OFF.            │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2 — ❌ this does NOT work — don't reach for it:         │
         │                                                              │
         │  $ cilium config enable-hubble-open-metrics=true             │
         │  Error: unknown configuration option                         │
         │  # Open-metrics isn't in the small live-toggle set —         │
         │  # it's fixed at agent start. Helm is the only lever.        │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 3 — ✅ the supported way — change it via Helm:          │
         │                                                              │
         │  $ cilium upgrade \                                          │
         │      --set hubble.metrics.enableOpenMetrics=true             │
         │  # (hubble.metrics.enabled is a separate list-valued flag —  │
         │  #  e.g. {dns,drop,http} — left untouched here so this       │
         │  #  example stays focused on ONE flag's ConfigMap round-trip)│
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 4 — Confirm, and confirm it STICKS:                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  $ cilium config -a | grep open-metrics                      │
         │  enable-hubble-open-metrics   true                           │
         │                                                              │
         │  ↑ Now recorded in the anihpj cluster's Helm release         │
         │    values, so the NEXT cilium upgrade or helm upgrade        │
         │    won't silently revert it.                                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         📖 Key Term: cilium config vs. cilium status vs. the ConfigMap
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  cilium config (this section; real name cilium-dbg           │
         │  config, run via kubectl exec into the Agent pod) shows      │
         │  the Agent's RUNTIME FEATURE FLAGS and live debug            │
         │  toggles — kube-proxy-replacement, Hubble, IPv4/IPv6,        │
         │  Debug, PolicyTracing, and more.                             │
         │                                                              │
         │  cilium status (Section 6.1) shows HEALTH — is the           │
         │  Agent up? Are all pods connected? It answers "is it         │
         │  running," not "how is it configured."                       │
         │                                                              │
         │  kubectl edit configmap cilium-config -n kube-system         │
         │  opens the SAME data cilium config reads — but raw and       │
         │  unvalidated, and edits made this way are NOT reflected      │
         │  back into your Helm release's recorded values.              │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Memory hook: status = is it ALIVE. config = HOW           │
         │     it's set up. The ConfigMap = the raw file underneath     │
         │     both views.                                              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.5  cilium-health and preflight validate-cnp — Two Real Gaps Filled
         - cilium-health is a separate binary from cilium-dbg — it checks
           NODE-to-node reachability, one layer below policy/endpoint state
         - There is no "cilium policy validate" — real validation is
           kubectl --dry-run=server (per-file) or cilium-dbg preflight
           validate-cnp (cluster-wide, run before a Cilium upgrade)

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  cilium-health is a SEPARATE binary from cilium-dbg —        │
         │  it measures NODE-to-NODE connectivity, not per-pod          │
         │  policy or endpoint state.                                   │
         │                                                              │
         │  $ kubectl -n kube-system exec ds/cilium -- \                │
         │      cilium-health status --succinct                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  Node1 (localhost): Reachable                                │
         │    ICMP to stack: OK, primary: OK                            │
         │    HTTP to API:   OK, primary: OK                            │
         │  Node2:             Reachable                                │
         │    ICMP to stack: OK, primary: OK                            │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 cilium-health runs its OWN tiny agent on every            │
         │     node and probes every other node over ICMP + an          │
         │     HTTP health endpoint — it answers "can node A            │
         │     even REACH node B," one layer below "is my pod           │
         │     policy correct" (cilium-dbg monitor/policy get).         │
         │                                                              │
         │  Related subcommands (per official cmdref):                  │
         │    status  Connectivity to other nodes (shown above)         │
         │    get     Display local cilium agent status                 │
         │    ping    Check whether the health API is up                │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  There is NO "cilium policy validate" command — that         │
         │  was this guide's error. Two REAL validation tools:          │
         │                                                              │
         │  BEFORE you apply ONE file (schema-check a CNP/CCNP          │
         │  YAML against the CRD, client- or server-side):              │
         │                                                              │
         │  $ kubectl apply --dry-run=server -f policy.yaml             │
         │  # catches typos in field names, bad selectors, etc.         │
         │  # --dry-run=client only checks local YAML syntax;           │
         │  # =server also validates against the live CRD schema        │
         │                                                              │
         │  BEFORE a Cilium UPGRADE, to sanity-check EVERY CNP          │
         │  already deployed in the cluster (not just one file):        │
         │                                                              │
         │  $ kubectl -n kube-system exec ds/cilium -- \                │
         │      cilium-dbg preflight validate-cnp                       │
         │  # exits 1 if ANY deployed policy is invalid — the           │
         │  # official pre-upgrade sanity check.                        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 6):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ cilium status is the first command every morning — a single-view
            health dashboard covering Cilium, Operator, Hubble Relay/UI,
            ClusterMesh, and Encryption, where "Desired: 3, Ready: 1" means 2
            Agents aren't starting.

         ✅ Section 6.2's command reference is organized by task: cilium
            endpoint for pods, cilium policy for network policy, cilium
            service/bpf lb for load balancing, cilium identity for security
            identities, and cilium debuginfo/bugtool for support bundles.

         ✅ cilium monitor shows every DROP/FORWARD/AUDIT verdict live, with
            --type drop, --type policy-verdict, --from-pod, and --to-pod
            filters — turning a silent policy denial into a readable
            FROM/TO/PORT/REASON line.

         ✅ The real command behind "cilium config" is cilium-dbg config (run
            via kubectl exec into the Agent pod): bare cilium config shows
            only the small live-toggleable debug set, while cilium config -a
            dumps every setting, including feature flags like
            kube-proxy-replacement fixed at install/upgrade time.

         ✅ The only supported way to durably change a feature flag is edit the
            Helm value then run cilium upgrade — cilium config <flag>=... only
            flips the small live-toggle set and resets on restart, and editing
            the cilium-config ConfigMap directly bypasses Helm and can be
            silently reverted.

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         You now know how to inspect, monitor, and configure a running Cilium
         install — but so far every pod has been free to talk to every other
         pod. Chapter 7 opens Part 4 by turning on the firewall:
         CiliumNetworkPolicy, the identity-based rules that decide exactly
         which pods may talk to which, on which ports, and over which
         protocols.

PART 4: NETWORK POLICY — SECURING YOUR CLUSTER                         🟡

Chapter 7: Network Policies — The Firewall for Your Pods
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    7.1  Why Network Policies Matter — The "Zero Trust" Mentality
         - Default Kubernetes: ALL pods can talk to ALL pods (dangerous!)
         - Network Policies = firewall rules for pod-to-pod communication
         - Why anihpj needs network policies (isolate DB, restrict admin, etc.)
         [DIAGRAM: Before/After — wide-open cluster vs policy-protected cluster]

         [ZERO TRUST — Before vs After Network Policies]
         ┌───────────────────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────────────────┐
         │  WITHOUT POLICIES (DEFAULT)   │  │  WITH CILIUM POLICIES         │
         │                               │  │                               │
         │  ┌─────┐    ┌─────┐          │  │  ┌─────┐      ┌─────┐        │
         │  │ ANY │◀──▶│ ANY │  😱      │  │  │anihpj│────▶│ DB  │ ✅     │
         │  │ POD │    │ POD │  All     │  │  └──┬──┘      └──┬──┘        │
         │  └─────┘    └─────┘  talk!   │  │     │            │            │
         │     │          │             │  │     │       ┌────▼────┐       │
         │     └────┬─────┘             │  │     │       │FRONTEND│        │
         │          ▼                   │  │     │       │(nginx) │ ✅     │
         │     ┌─────────┐             │  │     │       └────────┘        │
         │     │ HACKER  │ 😱          │  │     │                          │
         │     │ POD     │  Can        │  │  ┌──▼─────┐                   │
         │     │ injected│  access     │  │  │ HACKER │────▶ 🚫 DROP!    │
         │     └─────────┘  anything!  │  │  │  POD   │     BLOCKED!    │
         │                               │  └────────┘                   │
         │  ❌ No isolation              │  ✅ Full isolation              │
         │  ❌ No segmentation           │  ✅ Least privilege             │
         │  ❌ Attackers roam free       │  ✅ Attackers blocked           │
         └───────────────────────────────┘  └──────────────────────────────┘

    7.2  Standard Kubernetes NetworkPolicy vs CiliumNetworkPolicy
         - K8s native NetworkPolicy: L3/L4 only (IPs and ports)
         - CiliumNetworkPolicy: L3/L4/L7 (HTTP paths, gRPC methods, DNS, Kafka!)
         - Why CiliumCNP is more powerful: "Block POST /admin, but allow GET /"
         [COMPARISON TABLE: K8s NetPol vs CiliumNetPol features matrix]

         [K8s NetworkPolicy vs CiliumNetworkPolicy Feature Comparison]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  FEATURE                  K8s NetPol    Cilium CNP/CCNP     │
         │  ───────────────────────  ────────────  ─────────────────── │
         │  IP/CIDR Rules            ✅            ✅                   │
         │  Pod Selector             ✅            ✅                   │
         │  Namespace Selector       ✅            ✅                   │
         │  Port-Based Rules         ✅            ✅                   │
         │  HTTP Method (GET/POST)   ❌            ✅ 🔥                │
         │  HTTP Path (/api, /admin) ❌            ✅ 🔥                │
         │  HTTP Headers             ❌            ✅ 🔥                │
         │  gRPC Method              ❌            ✅ 🔥                │
         │  Kafka Topic              ❌            ✅ 🔥                │
         │  DNS FQDN Rules           ❌            ✅ 🔥                │
         │  ICMP Rules               ❌            ✅                   │
         │  Identity-Based           ❌            ✅ (core design!)    │
         │  Policy Audit Mode        ❌            ✅                   │
         │  Deny by Default          Manual        ✅ (easier!)        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    7.3  Your First CiliumNetworkPolicy — Protect the Database
         - Scenario: Only anihpj pods should talk to PostgreSQL
         - YAML walkthrough: every field explained
         - Apply, verify, and test
         [YAML BLOCK: Full CNP example for anihpj→PostgreSQL, annotated]

         [CILIUM NETWORK POLICY — anihpj → PostgreSQL Example]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind: CiliumNetworkPolicy                                   │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: protect-postgres    # Name of this rule             │
         │    namespace: anihpj         # Which namespace               │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    endpointSelector:         # WHICH PODS does this apply to?│
         │      matchLabels:                                            │
         │        app: postgres         # → PostgreSQL pods             │
         │        component: database                                   │
         │    ingress:                  # INCOMING traffic rules        │
         │    - fromEndpoints:          # ALLOW from these pods:        │
         │      - matchLabels:                                          │
         │          app: anihpj         # → Only anihpj Django pods     │
         │          component: backend                                  │
         │      toPorts:                # ALLOW on these ports:         │
         │      - ports:                                                │
         │        - port: "5432"        # → Only PostgreSQL port        │
         │          protocol: TCP                                       │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Everything ELSE is DENIED by default!                    │
         │     (If no rule allows it, Cilium drops the packet)          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    7.4  Layer 7 Policies — HTTP-Aware Rules That Understand Your App
         - Block POST to Django /admin from external IPs
         - Allow GET to /jobs/ for everyone
         - Rate limit API endpoints
         [YAML BLOCK: L7 CNP for Django anihpj — HTTP method + path rules]

         [L7 CILIUM NETWORK POLICY — anihpj HTTP Protection]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind: CiliumNetworkPolicy                                   │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: protect-django-admin                                │
         │    namespace: anihpj                                         │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    endpointSelector:                                         │
         │      matchLabels:                                            │
         │        app: anihpj                                           │
         │    ingress:                                                  │
         │    # RULE 1: Allow GET /jobs/ from anywhere                  │
         │    - fromEndpoints:                                          │
         │      - {}                     # "{}" = ANY source            │
         │      toPorts:                                                │
         │      - ports:                                                │
         │        - port: "8000"                                        │
         │        rules:                                                │
         │          http:                                               │
         │          - method: "GET"     # ⬅ L7! Cilium understands HTTP│
         │            path: "/jobs/"    # ⬅ Only this path!            │
         │                                                              │
         │    # RULE 2: Block POST to /admin EXCEPT from internal IPs   │
         │    - fromCIDR:                                               │
         │      - "10.0.0.0/8"          # Only internal network         │
         │      toPorts:                                                │
         │      - ports:                                                │
         │        - port: "8000"                                        │
         │        rules:                                                │
         │          http:                                               │
         │          - method: "POST"                                    │
         │            path: "/admin/"   # Admin login page              │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Any POST to /admin/ from the internet? → DROP! 🔴        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    7.5  DNS-Based Policies — "Allow Pods to Talk to api.github.com"
         - FQDN-based rules: allow egress to specific domain names
         - How it works: Cilium watches DNS requests, caches resolved IPs
         - Example: Allow anihpj to call external payment API
         [YAML BLOCK: toFQDNs rule example]

    7.6  Policy Audit Mode — Test Before You Enforce
         - What is audit mode? (It LOGS violations but doesn't BLOCK)
         - How to safely roll out policies: Audit → Monitor → Enforce
         - Example: Audit anihpj traffic for a week, then enforce
         [STEP-BY-STEP: Audit → Enforce workflow with timeline]

    7.7  Complete Network Policy Strategy for anihpj
         - Default deny all ingress
         - Allow frontend → backend (port 8000)
         - Allow backend → database (port 5432)
         - Allow monitoring tools → all (port 9090)
         - Block external access to /admin
         [VISUAL DIAGRAM: anihpj security perimeter with all rules]

         [COMPLETE NETWORK POLICY STRATEGY — anihpj Security Perimeter]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  INTERNET ───▶ INGRESS CONTROLLER (nginx)                    │
         │                    │                                         │
         │                    │ ALLOW :80, :443                         │
         │                    ▼                                         │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐                    │
         │  │         FRONTEND (nginx)             │                    │
         │  │         Label: app=anihpj,           │                    │
         │  │                tier=frontend         │                    │
         │  └────────────────┬────────────────────┘                    │
         │                   │ ALLOW :8000                             │
         │                   ▼                                         │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐                    │
         │  │         BACKEND (Django)             │                    │
         │  │         Label: app=anihpj,           │                    │
         │  │                tier=backend          │                    │
         │  │                                       │                    │
         │  │  ┌──── GET /jobs/ ────────────── ✅  │                    │
         │  │  └──── POST /admin/ (external) ── 🔴  │                    │
         │  └────────────────┬────────────────────┘                    │
         │                   │ ALLOW :5432                             │
         │                   ▼                                         │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐                    │
         │  │         DATABASE (PostgreSQL)        │                    │
         │  │         Label: app=postgres          │                    │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────┘                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  DEFAULT RULE: DENY ALL (everything else blocked!)          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [HOW CILIUM EVALUATES NETWORK POLICIES — Rule Processing Order]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  For EACH packet, Cilium checks in this order:               │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ 1. CNP (CiliumNetworkPolicy) — namespaced policies      │ │
         │  │    • Most specific — checked FIRST                       │ │
         │  │    • If CNP has a rule → USE IT (ALLOW or DENY)         │ │
         │  │                                                          │ │
         │  │ 2. CCNP (CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy) — cluster-wide │ │
         │  │    • Checked if NO matching CNP rule                    │ │
         │  │    • If CCNP has a rule → USE IT                         │ │
         │  │                                                          │ │
         │  │ 3. Default DENY (Cilium's secure default)                │ │
         │  │    • If NO policy matches → DENY the traffic 🔴          │ │
         │  │    • This is the "zero trust" default                    │ │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 CNP OVERRIDES CCNP! Namespace policies take priority.   │
         │     Rule of thumb: more specific wins over more general.     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [NETWORK POLICY YAML — The 4 Essential Fields Visualized]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ apiVersion: cilium.io/v2  ← MUST be cilium.io/v2        │ │
         │  │ kind: CiliumNetworkPolicy ← CNP or CCNP                  │ │
         │  │ metadata:                                                │ │
         │  │   name: my-policy          ← Unique name in namespace    │ │
         │  │   namespace: anihpj        ← Which namespace             │ │
         │  │ spec:                                                    │ │
         │  │   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ │
         │  │   │ endpointSelector:   ← WHICH PODS does this apply? │ │ │
         │  │   │   matchLabels:                                     │ │ │
         │  │   │     app: postgres   ← Apply to pods with this     │ │ │
         │  │   │                       label (the TARGET)           │ │ │
         │  │   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ │
         │  │   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ │
         │  │   │ ingress:            ← INCOMING traffic rules      │ │ │
         │  │   │   - fromEndpoints:  ← WHO can send traffic IN?    │ │ │
         │  │   │     toPorts:        ← WHICH ports are allowed?    │ │ │
         │  │   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ │
         │  │   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ │
         │  │   │ egress:             ← OUTGOING traffic rules      │ │ │
         │  │   │   - toEndpoints:    ← WHERE can pods send traffic? │ │ │
         │  │   │     toPorts:        ← WHICH ports are allowed?    │ │ │
         │  │   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 MEMORY AID: endpointSelector = "WHO", ingress/egress =   │
         │     "CAN DO WHAT", fromEndpoints/toEndpoints = "FROM/TO WHO" │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [POLICY AUDIT MODE — The Safe Rollout Strategy]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚠️  NEVER enforce a policy immediately in production!        │
         │                                                              │
         │  THE 3-STEP SAFE ROLLOUT:                                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 1: AUDIT (1-7 days)                                    │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ Add: enableAudit: true   ← LOGS but doesn't BLOCK    │   │
         │  │ Monitor: hubble observe --verdict AUDIT               │   │
         │  │ Goal: See what traffic WOULD be blocked               │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │            │                                                 │
         │            ▼                                                 │
         │  STEP 2: MONITOR                                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ Review audit logs in Hubble for 1-7 days              │   │
         │  │ Check: Any legitimate traffic being flagged?          │   │
         │  │ Tune policy if needed (add missing allows)            │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │            │                                                 │
         │            ▼                                                 │
         │  STEP 3: ENFORCE                                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ Remove: enableAudit  (or set to false)                │   │
         │  │ Now policy actually DENIES traffic 🔴                 │   │
         │  │ Monitor closely for first 24 hours                    │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Pro tip: Use audit mode for 1 WEEK before enforcing.     │
         │     It saves you from "I just broke production!" moments.    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    7.8  Policy Enforcement Modes — Default, Always, and Never
         - A SEPARATE axis from "does a rule allow this traffic?" (7.1-7.7)
         - Enforcement mode = does the endpoint check policy AT ALL?
         - Three real, literal mode values: default, always, never
         - Set cluster-wide (Helm → ConfigMap), inspected per-pod (CLI)
         - Distinct from — but often confused with — Policy Audit Mode (7.6)
         [DIAGRAM: Same postgres pod, 3 modes, 3 different outcomes]

         [POLICY ENFORCEMENT MODES — The Three Real Cilium Modes]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  DEFAULT  (Cilium's factory setting)                         │
         │    Endpoint allows ALL traffic until a policy selects it.    │
         │    Once selected — PER DIRECTION — only allowed traffic      │
         │    passes for that direction; the other direction stays      │
         │    open until its OWN matching rule shows up.                │
         │                                                              │
         │  ALWAYS  (paranoid / zero-trust-everywhere mode)             │
         │    EVERY endpoint enforces policy, even with ZERO rules      │
         │    selecting it. Zero rules = implicit deny-all, always.     │
         │                                                              │
         │  NEVER  (fire-drill / cluster bring-up mode)                 │
         │    NO endpoint enforces policy, even if rules DO select      │
         │    it. Every CNP/CCNP stays loaded but fully INERT.          │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Literal values Cilium expects — nothing else is valid:    │
         │     default  |  always  |  never                             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [HOW TO CONFIGURE — Helm Value → ConfigMap → Live Endpoint Check]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  1. SET CLUSTER-WIDE — Helm value, at install/upgrade:       │
         │     --set policyEnforcementMode=always                       │
         │                                                              │
         │     → Templates into the cilium-config ConfigMap key:        │
         │       enable-policy: "always"        # value is lowercased   │
         │                                                              │
         │  2. CHECK ONE ENDPOINT'S LIVE STATUS — cilium CLI:           │
         │     $ cilium endpoint list                                   │
         │     (or cilium-dbg endpoint list on newer agent images)      │
         │                                                              │
         │     ENDPOINT  POLICY (ingress)  POLICY (egress)  LABELS      │
         │     1928      Disabled          Disabled         postgres    │
         │     (real output also shows IDENTITY, IPv4, STATUS cols)     │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 POLICY (ingress/egress) = Enabled means THAT direction    │
         │     is enforcing RIGHT NOW. Disabled means wide open.        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [WORKED EXAMPLE — postgres Under Each Enforcement Mode]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  SETUP: postgres pod (app=postgres, component=database).     │
         │         Zero CiliumNetworkPolicy selects it — yet.           │
         │                                                              │
         │  MODE = default, 0 policies match postgres:                  │
         │    → postgres ACCEPTS traffic from ANYWHERE. Nothing has     │
         │      selected it yet, so nothing is enforced. ✅(open)        │
         │                                                              │
         │  MODE = always, 0 policies match postgres:                   │
         │    → postgres REJECTS traffic from EVERYWHERE. Zero          │
         │      rules = implicit deny-all, nothing is allowed. 🔴        │
         │                                                              │
         │  Now apply protect-postgres (7.3): only anihpj may           │
         │  reach :5432.                                                │
         │                                                              │
         │  MODE = default, 1+ policy matches postgres:                 │
         │    → Ingress flips to Enabled. Only anihpj:5432 passes —     │
         │      default now CONVERGES with always. ✅                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  MODE = always, 1+ policy matches postgres:                  │
         │    → Identical enforced result — same CNP, same outcome.     │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 default and always are IDENTICAL once ANY policy          │
         │     selects the endpoint. The gap only exists at ZERO.       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [DEFAULT vs ALWAYS vs NEVER — Side-by-Side Comparison]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  MODE      0 POLICIES MATCH    1+ POLICIES MATCH             │
         │  ───────   ─────────────────   ───────────────────           │
         │  DEFAULT   ALLOW all traffic    ENFORCE (allow-listed only)  │
         │  ALWAYS    DENY all traffic     ENFORCE (allow-listed only)  │
         │  NEVER     ALLOW all traffic    ALLOW all traffic (ignored)  │
         │                                                              │
         │  REAL-WORLD USE CASE:                                        │
         │    DEFAULT — normal day-to-day operations (recommended)      │
         │    ALWAYS  — strict zero-trust bring-up, no gaps allowed     │
         │    NEVER   — fresh cluster install / active troubleshooting  │
         │            (⚠️ dangerous to leave on in production)          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         📖 Key Term: Policy Enforcement Mode vs. Policy Audit Mode
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Policy Enforcement Mode decides WHETHER an endpoint         │
         │  checks policy AT ALL (default / always / never — set        │
         │  cluster-wide via policyEnforcementMode).                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  Policy Audit Mode (7.6) is layered ON TOP of an ALREADY     │
         │  enforcing endpoint: it decides whether a matching DENY      │
         │  actually blocks the packet, or just logs a "would-deny"     │
         │  verdict to Hubble while still letting the packet through.   │
         │  Toggle: Helm policyAuditMode: true (cluster-wide), or       │
         │  cilium-dbg endpoint config <ID> PolicyAuditMode=Enabled.    │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Enforcement = "is there a wall at all?"                   │
         │     Audit      = "is the wall's alarm silent or armed?"      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 7):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Forgetting to allow DNS (port 53 to CoreDNS!)
            → SYMPTOM: Pods work then suddenly can't resolve any hostnames
            → FIX: Always add an egress rule allowing UDP 53 to kube-system

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Using the wrong selector labels
            → SYMPTOM: Policy seems to have no effect
            → FIX: kubectl get pods --show-labels and verify exact label names

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Confusing ingress and egress
            → TRUTH: ingress = traffic COMING INTO the selected pod
            → TRUTH: egress = traffic GOING OUT FROM the selected pod
            → FIX: For DB protection, use ingress on the DB pod selector

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 7):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. What's the difference between K8s NetworkPolicy and CiliumNetworkPolicy?
        Name 4 capabilities Cilium has that K8s NetPol doesn't.
    Q2. Write a CiliumNetworkPolicy that allows ONLY GET requests to /api/v1/.
    Q3. What does "{}" mean in fromEndpoints? When would you use it?
    Q4. How does DNS-based policy (toFQDNs) work? What does Cilium do under the hood?
    Q5. What is policy audit mode and why should you ALWAYS use it before enforcing?
    Q6. If CNP and CCNP both have rules for the same traffic, which one takes priority?
    Q7. Why does blocking all egress without allowing DNS break your applications?
    Q8. Write a policy that allows anihpj to call api.stripe.com (HTTPS) but blocks all
        other egress. Include DNS allowance.

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 7):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ K8s NetworkPolicy is allow-all by default and L3/L4-only; Cilium's zero-
            trust CiliumNetworkPolicy (CNP) defaults to deny and adds real L7
            superpowers — HTTP, gRPC, Kafka, and DNS.

         ✅ Your first CNP (protect-postgres) put the 4 core fields to work:
            endpointSelector = WHO it targets, fromEndpoints = WHO may connect,
            toPorts = which port — everything else denied.

         ✅ L7 rules read the actual HTTP request — allow GET /jobs/ for everyone,
            block POST /admin/ from outside 10.0.0.0/8 — while toFQDNs lets egress
            target a domain like api.stripe.com.

         ✅ Policy Audit Mode's 3-step rollout (audit → monitor Hubble → enforce) is
            the safe way to ship a new policy; Cilium always evaluates CNP before CCNP
            before falling back to default-deny.

         ✅ NEW in 7.8: Policy Enforcement Mode (default/always/never) decides whether
            an endpoint checks policy AT ALL — a different axis from Policy Audit Mode
            (7.6), which decides whether an already-enforcing DENY actually blocks the
            packet or just logs it.

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         You've now built a complete namespace-scoped defense for anihpj — default-
         deny, L7-aware, DNS-based, and audit-tested — plus a clear picture of how
         enforcement mode decides whether any of it even gets checked. Chapter 8 lifts
         every one of those same building blocks cluster-wide with
         CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy (CCNP): one CRD, no metadata.namespace, and a
         nodeSelector that can firewall entire NODES instead of just pods.


Chapter 8: CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy (CCNP) — Cluster-Level Rules
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ CNP is namespace-scoped; CCNP is cluster-scoped — same rules, wider reach
         ✅ CCNP's real apiVersion/kind: cilium.io/v2 / CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy
         ✅ Why CCNP has NO metadata.namespace field (and what breaks if you add one)
         ✅ How nodeSelector targets NODES instead of PODS — Cilium's host firewall
         ✅ Writing a CCNP that blocks every pod from reaching the cloud metadata IP
         ✅ Writing a CCNP that lets Prometheus scrape /metrics from every namespace
         ✅ Why CCNP needs ClusterRole RBAC permissions, not just a namespaced Role

    8.1  When You Need Cluster-Wide Policies
         - CNP vs CCNP: namespace-scoped vs cluster-scoped
         - Example: Block all external traffic to kube-system namespace
         - Example: Allow monitoring namespace to scrape all pods
         - Real CRD: apiVersion cilium.io/v2, kind CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy
         - CCNP has NO metadata.namespace field — cluster-scoped by definition
         - nodeSelector lets a CCNP target NODES instead of PODS (host firewall)

         [CNP vs CCNP — Field-by-Field Comparison]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CNP vs CCNP — Field-by-Field Comparison                     │
         │                                                              │
         │  FIELD             CNP (namespaced)  CCNP (cluster)          │
         │  ─────────────     ────────────────  ───────────────         │
         │  kind:             CiliumNetwork     CiliumClusterwide       │
         │                    Policy            NetworkPolicy           │
         │  metadata.         REQUIRED          DOES NOT EXIST          │
         │    namespace       (1 namespace)     (cluster-scoped!)       │
         │  endpointSelector  own ns only       ANY ns (or nodes)       │
         │  nodeSelector      not available     targets NODES only      │
         │  Typical RBAC      Role/RoleBinding  ClusterRoleBinding      │
         │  Typical use       "anihpj->DB"      "block IMDS"            │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Internally, Cilium enforces CNP and CCNP with the SAME    │
         │     eBPF rule engine — CCNP just skips the namespace filter. │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         📖 Key Term: CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy (CCNP)
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy is a CLUSTER-SCOPED custom   │
         │  resource (no metadata.namespace) that applies its rules to  │
         │  every namespace in the cluster at once.                     │
         │                                                              │
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind:       CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy                  │
         │                                                              │
         │  • Same spec fields as CiliumNetworkPolicy: endpointSelector,│
         │    ingress, egress, ingressDeny, egressDeny                  │
         │  • PLUS an extra option CNP doesn't have: nodeSelector       │
         │    (select NODES by label instead of PODS — used for host    │
         │    firewalling, e.g. "lock down SSH on every worker node")   │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Analogy: CNP is a rule posted on ONE office door.         │
         │     CCNP is a rule posted on the building's front gate —     │
         │     it applies no matter which office (namespace) you enter. │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CCNP EXAMPLE — Monitoring Namespace Scraping ALL Namespaces]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind: CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy                        │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: allow-monitoring-scrape-all  # no 'namespace:' here!│
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    description: "Let Prometheus scrape metrics cluster-wide" │
         │    endpointSelector: {}    # "{}" = ALL pods, ALL namespaces │
         │    ingress:                                                  │
         │    - fromEndpoints:        # ALLOW incoming traffic FROM:    │
         │      - matchLabels:                                          │
         │          # k8s: prefix = Cilium's reserved namespace label   │
         │          k8s:io.kubernetes.pod.namespace: monitoring         │
         │          app: prometheus                                     │
         │      toPorts:               # ALLOW on these ports ONLY:     │
         │      - ports:                                                │
         │        - port: "9090"       # anihpj /metrics exporter port  │
         │          protocol: TCP                                       │
         │        - port: "8000"       # Django app port (if instru-    │
         │          protocol: TCP      #  mented w/ django-prometheus)  │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 endpointSelector: {} + no metadata.namespace = this       │
         │     rule's ALLOW applies to every namespace, cluster-wide.   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    8.2  Real-World CCNP Scenarios for anihpj
         - Policy 1: Block all pods from accessing metadata.cloud services
         - Policy 2: Allow Prometheus to scrape metrics from ALL namespaces
         - Policy 3: DNS allowlist for entire cluster (block DNS exfiltration)

         [CCNP POLICY 1 — Block Cloud Metadata / IMDS Access Cluster-Wide]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind: CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy                        │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: block-cloud-metadata-access                         │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    description: "Block every pod from reaching the cloud     │
         │                  instance-metadata service (SSRF / credential│
         │                  theft prevention)"                          │
         │    endpointSelector: {}      # Applies to ALL pods, cluster- │
         │                              # wide (every namespace)        │
         │    egressDeny:                 # ⛔ explicit DENY (not just   │
         │    - toCIDR:                   #    'no allow rule exists')  │
         │      - "169.254.169.254/32"    # AWS/GCP/Azure metadata IP   │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 egressDeny ALWAYS wins, even if some other CNP/CCNP has   │
         │     an 'allow all egress' rule. Deny beats allow — always.   │
         │  ⚠️  169.254.169.254 serves cloud IAM credentials. A pod that│
         │     can reach it can often steal node/cluster-level secrets! │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CCNP POLICY 2 — Prometheus Scrapes Metrics From ALL Namespaces]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind: CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy                        │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: allow-prometheus-scrape-all-namespaces              │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    description: "Prometheus in monitoring/ may scrape        │
         │                  /metrics on every pod in the cluster"       │
         │    endpointSelector: {}        # Target: ALL pods everywhere │
         │    ingress:                                                  │
         │    - fromEndpoints:                                          │
         │      - matchLabels:                                          │
         │          k8s:io.kubernetes.pod.namespace: monitoring         │
         │          app.kubernetes.io/name: prometheus                  │
         │      toPorts:                                                │
         │      - ports:                                                │
         │        - port: "9090"         # anihpj metrics port          │
         │          protocol: TCP                                       │
         │        rules:                                                │
         │          http:                                               │
         │          - method: "GET"      # L7! Only GET is allowed —    │
         │            path: "/metrics"   #    scraping can't POST/DELETE│
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Combine with Ch7's L7 rules: Prometheus gets GET          │
         │     /metrics ONLY — it still can't POST to /admin/ on Django.│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CCNP POLICY 3 — Cluster-Wide DNS Allowlist (Anti-Exfiltration)]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind: CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy                        │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: cluster-wide-dns-allowlist                          │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    description: "Every pod may resolve DNS via CoreDNS, but  │
         │                  may only ever RESOLVE an allow-listed set of│
         │                  external FQDNs (blocks DNS exfiltration)"   │
         │    endpointSelector: {}          # ALL pods, cluster-wide    │
         │    egress:                                                   │
         │    - toEndpoints:                # RULE 1: allow DNS lookups │
         │      - matchLabels:              #  to CoreDNS itself        │
         │          k8s:io.kubernetes.pod.namespace: kube-system        │
         │          k8s-app: kube-dns          # from Cilium's own docs │
         │      toPorts:                                                │
         │      - ports:                                                │
         │        - port: "53"                                          │
         │          protocol: ANY                                       │
         │        rules:                                                │
         │          dns:                                                │
         │          - matchPattern: "*.anihpj.internal"                 │
         │          - matchName: "api.stripe.com"     # payments (Ch7)  │
         │          - matchName: "pypi.org"           # pip installs    │
         │    - toFQDNs:                  # RULE 2: once resolved, only │
         │      - matchPattern: "*.anihpj.internal"   # THESE IPs may be│
         │      - matchName: "api.stripe.com"         # dialed on egress│
         │      - matchName: "pypi.org"                                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 toPorts.rules.dns restricts WHICH NAMES may be resolved;  │
         │     toFQDNs restricts WHICH RESOLVED IPs may be connected to.│
         │     Use both together — that's what actually blocks exfil.   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    8.3  Multi-Tenancy, RBAC, and Change-Control for Cluster-Wide Policies
         - CCNP's blast radius is the WHOLE cluster — write access must stay
           with a small platform/security team, unlike namespaced CNP
         - Real RBAC: a ClusterRole + ClusterRoleBinding scopes
           ciliumclusterwidenetworkpolicies verbs to platform-admins only
         - App teams keep a namespaced Role/RoleBinding scoped to
           ciliumnetworkpolicies (CNP) — never the CCNP resource itself
         - Change control: PR review + cilium policy validate (App. A.3) +
           staged Policy Audit Mode rollout (Ch7.6) BEFORE merging a CCNP
         - What goes wrong: one team's "small" CCNP edit can silently widen
           access for every other namespace in the cluster at once

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # PLATFORM TEAM — may write CCNP (cluster-wide reach)       │
         │  apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                    │
         │  kind: ClusterRole                                           │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: ccnp-platform-admin                                 │
         │  rules:                                                      │
         │  - apiGroups: ["cilium.io"]                                  │
         │    resources: ["ciliumclusterwidenetworkpolicies"]           │
         │    verbs: ["get","list","watch","create","update",           │
         │            "delete"]                                         │
         │  ---                                                         │
         │  apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                    │
         │  kind: ClusterRoleBinding                                    │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: ccnp-platform-admin-binding                         │
         │  subjects:                                                   │
         │  - kind: Group                                               │
         │    name: platform-admins     # SSO/OIDC group claim          │
         │  roleRef:                                                    │
         │    kind: ClusterRole                                         │
         │    name: ccnp-platform-admin                                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  # APP TEAM — may write CNP, but ONLY in their own ns        │
         │  apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                    │
         │  kind: Role                                                  │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: anihpj-cnp-editor                                   │
         │    namespace: anihpj             # scoped to ONE ns          │
         │  rules:                                                      │
         │  - apiGroups: ["cilium.io"]                                  │
         │    resources: ["ciliumnetworkpolicies"]                      │
         │    verbs: ["get","list","watch","create","update",           │
         │            "delete"]                                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Same verbs, two different resources, two different        │
         │     RBAC kinds. ciliumclusterwidenetworkpolicies needs       │
         │     a ClusterRole; ciliumnetworkpolicies can stay on a       │
         │     namespaced Role. Never grant the app team access         │
         │     to the CCNP resource name.                               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Because endpointSelector: {} on a CCNP reaches EVERY        │
         │  namespace, treat any CCNP change like a cluster-admin       │
         │  change, not an ordinary app-team pull request:              │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 1 — PR + REQUIRED REVIEWER                             │
         │    Require approval from platform-admins (the same           │
         │    group bound above) before ANY CCNP merge.                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2 — STATIC VALIDATION                                  │
         │    $ cilium policy validate ccnp-change.yaml                 │
         │    Catches YAML/schema mistakes (Appendix A.3) — it          │
         │    does NOT catch a selector that is merely too broad.       │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 3 — STAGED ROLLOUT, POLICY AUDIT MODE (Ch7.6)          │
         │    Apply the CCNP with enableAudit: true FIRST, then         │
         │    watch hubble observe --verdict AUDIT cluster-wide         │
         │    for 1-7 days before flipping audit off to enforce.        │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 4 — MERGE / ENFORCE                                    │
         │    Only after Steps 1-3 pass does the CCNP go live —         │
         │    with real enforcement, cluster-wide.                      │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 A namespaced CNP mistake breaks one team's day. A         │
         │     CCNP mistake merged without this workflow can            │
         │     break ingress/egress for every namespace at once.        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  SCENARIO: anihpj's app team is mistakenly granted the       │
         │  platform-admin ClusterRole (an RBAC misconfiguration).      │
         │                                                              │
         │  1. They edit the existing cluster-wide-dns-allowlist        │
         │     CCNP (Ch8.2, Policy 3) to add their own vendor:          │
         │       - toFQDNs:                                             │
         │           - matchPattern: "*.some-saas-vendor.com"           │
         │                                                              │
         │  2. They tested it against the anihpj namespace only —       │
         │     it looked like a small, safe, one-line addition.         │
         │                                                              │
         │  3. But the CCNP still has endpointSelector: {} and NO       │
         │     metadata.namespace — so the new toFQDNs rule is          │
         │     now resolvable AND dialable from EVERY namespace,        │
         │     including a compliance-scoped billing namespace          │
         │     meant to reach ONLY its payment processor.               │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚠️  A change scoped in the author's head to 'just my        │
         │     namespace' silently widened egress for a namespace       │
         │     under stricter compliance rules — no error, no           │
         │     warning, no namespace boundary to stop it.               │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 This is exactly the class of bug that looks like a        │
         │     namespace-local policy issue (Appendix C.2) but is       │
         │     actually cluster-wide — which is precisely why CCNP      │
         │     write access must stay platform-admins-only.             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 8):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Adding a metadata.namespace field to a CCNP manifest
            → TRUTH: CCNP is cluster-scoped by definition — it has NO namespace field.
            → FIX: Never add metadata.namespace to a CCNP. Need one namespace? Use a CNP.

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Treating "CNP overrides CCNP" as a strict priority order
            → TRUTH: Cilium MERGES rules from every CNP/CCNP that selects a pod (additive).
            → FIX: Think "union of all rules, deny beats allow" — not "CNP always wins."

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Assuming a namespaced RoleBinding can create a CCNP
            → TRUTH: CCNP is cluster-scoped — only a ClusterRoleBinding grants access to it.
            → FIX: Ask your admin for a ClusterRole on ciliumclusterwidenetworkpolicies.

         ❌ MISTAKE 4: Giving an app team CCNP edit access "to move faster"
            → TRUTH: A CCNP's endpointSelector: {} has no namespace boundary — one team's edit can silently reach ingress/egress rules in every OTHER namespace too, not just their own.
            → FIX: Keep ciliumclusterwidenetworkpolicies write access in a ClusterRole bound only to platform-admins; give app teams a namespaced Role scoped to ciliumnetworkpolicies (CNP) instead.

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 8):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. When would you use CCNP instead of CNP?
    Q2. Can a CNP override a CCNP? Which takes precedence?
    Q3. Which field is INVALID in a CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy manifest?
        A) spec.endpointSelector    B) metadata.namespace
        C) spec.nodeSelector        D) spec.ingress

    Q4. Write a CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy that blocks EVERY pod in the
        cluster from reaching 169.254.169.254, regardless of namespace.

    Q5. Which field lets a CCNP target NODES (for host firewalling) instead
        of PODS?
