        A) hostSelector    B) nodeSelector
        C) endpointSelector    D) machineSelector

    Q6. Why does a cluster-wide DNS allowlist need BOTH a toPorts.rules.dns
        rule AND a toFQDNs rule? What does each one actually restrict?

    Q7. A user has a namespaced RoleBinding granting "create" on
        ciliumnetworkpolicies in the anihpj namespace. Can that user also
        create a CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy?
        A) Yes, RBAC permissions for CNP automatically extend to CCNP
        B) No — CCNP is cluster-scoped and requires a ClusterRoleBinding
        C) Yes, but only inside the kube-system namespace
        D) No — CCNP support must first be enabled via a Helm feature flag

    Q8. Your team wants Prometheus (in the monitoring namespace) to scrape
        /metrics on port 9090 from every pod in the cluster, but NOT be
        able to call any other HTTP path or port. Write the CCNP that
        accomplishes this.

    Q9. Your platform team wants every CCNP change reviewed BEFORE it goes
        live, given that Ch8.2's Policy 3 (cluster-wide-dns-allowlist)
        already grants broad toFQDNs reach. Describe the change-control
        steps you'd require between a pull request that edits that CCNP
        and that CCNP actually enforcing in the cluster.

    Q10. An app team is accidentally granted a ClusterRole with create/
         update/delete on ciliumclusterwidenetworkpolicies. They edit an
         existing CCNP to add a toFQDNs entry meant only for their own
         namespace. What is the MOST likely real-world consequence?
         A) Nothing — CCNP edits still respect metadata.namespace boundaries
         B) The API server rejects it because they lack a CNP Role
         C) The new toFQDNs rule applies cluster-wide, potentially loosening
            egress for every other namespace too
         D) Cilium automatically converts their edit into a namespaced CNP

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 8):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ CCNP shares CNP's apiVersion (cilium.io/v2) and spec fields, but is
            cluster-scoped: no metadata.namespace, plus an optional nodeSelector.

         ✅ Cilium MERGES rules from every CNP/CCNP selecting a pod (additive union) —
            the only hard precedence rule is that explicit DENY always beats ALLOW.

         ✅ Real-world CCNP use cases are cluster-wide guardrails: blocking the cloud
            metadata IP, letting Prometheus scrape everywhere, DNS anti-exfiltration.

         ✅ CCNP is cluster-scoped, so creating one always requires a ClusterRole bound
            via a ClusterRoleBinding — a namespaced Role/RoleBinding is never enough.

         ✅ Reach for CCNP when a rule must apply cluster-wide (or to nodes); reach for
            CNP when a rule belongs to one application's own namespace, like anihpj's.

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         You've now written rules at both the namespace and cluster level — but real
         clusters don't always behave the way your YAML says they should. Chapter 9
         turns to Network Policy Debugging: a systematic, step-by-step method (DNS →
         policy verdict → routing → application) for answering the question every
         Cilium operator eventually asks out loud: "Why can't my pod connect?"


Chapter 9: Network Policy Debugging — "Why Can't My Pod Connect?"
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ The systematic 4-step debugging order every Cilium operator uses:
            DNS → policy verdict → routing → application
         ✅ How to read real `cilium monitor --type policy-verdict` output:
            action allow/deny/audit and match L3-L4/none
         ✅ Why a Cilium policy DENY causes a silent timeout, never a
            "connection refused" error
         ✅ The 5 most common CiliumNetworkPolicy mistakes — and the
            one-line fix for each
         ✅ Why endpoint IDs are ephemeral and node-local, and how that
            trips up cilium monitor filters
         ✅ Why cilium monitor is scoped to a single node, and when to
            reach for Hubble instead
         ✅ How to confirm a label-selector mismatch with --show-labels
            before blaming the policy YAML

    9.1  The Systematic Debugging Approach
         - Step 1: Is it a DNS issue? (nslookup from pod)
         - Step 2: Is it a policy issue? (cilium monitor --type policy-verdict)
         - Step 3: Is it a routing issue? (cilium endpoint inspect)
         - Step 4: Is it the application? (check app logs)
         [DECISION FLOWCHART: Pod can't connect — debug path]

         [DEBUGGING FLOWCHART — "My Pod Can't Connect!"]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  🔴 "anihpj pod can't reach PostgreSQL!"                     │
         │       │                                                      │
         │       ▼                                                      │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ Can pod resolve DNS? │──NO──▶ Check CoreDNS + toFQDNs    │
         │  │ nslookup postgres   │          policy.                    │
         │  └─────────┬───────────┘                                     │
         │            │ YES                                             │
         │            ▼                                                │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ Is packet DROP-ed?  │──YES──▶ cilium monitor shows       │
         │  │ cilium monitor -t   │          POLICY_DENIED?             │
         │  │ policy-verdict      │          Check policy selectors!   │
         │  └─────────┬───────────┘                                     │
         │            │ NO (packet forwarded)                           │
         │            ▼                                                │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ Can pod reach IP?   │──NO──▶ Check routes, tunnel mode   │
         │  │ ping <DB pod IP>    │          cilium endpoint inspect   │
         │  └─────────┬───────────┘                                     │
         │            │ YES                                             │
         │            ▼                                                │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ Port open?          │──NO──▶ Is PostgreSQL listening?    │
         │  │ nc -zv <IP> 5432    │          kubectl exec into DB pod  │
         │  └─────────┬───────────┘                                     │
         │            │ YES                                             │
         │            ▼                                                │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                     │
         │  │ App-level issue!    │──▶ Check Django settings,          │
         │  │ Check app logs      │    DATABASE_URL, credentials       │
         │  └─────────────────────┘                                     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [DEBUGGING STEP OUTPUTS — What Each Flowchart Check Returns]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  STEP-BY-STEP: What Each Flowchart Check Actually Returns    │
         │                                                              │
         │  Step 1 (DNS) — nslookup from inside the anihpj pod:         │
         │  $ kubectl exec -it anihpj-7d9f6 -- nslookup postgres        │
         │  Server:    10.244.0.10                                      │
         │  Name:      postgres.anihpj.svc.cluster.local                │
         │  Address 1: 10.244.2.9 postgres-0.postgres.anihpj.svc        │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ Resolved — an IP came back, DNS itself is fine.           │
         │  Move on to Step 2 (policy verdict) — see the 9.2 box.       │
         │                                                              │
         │  Step 3 (routing) — ping the resolved pod IP directly:       │
         │  $ kubectl exec -it anihpj-7d9f6 -- ping -c 2 10.244.2.9     │
         │  PING 10.244.2.9: 56 data bytes                              │
         │  2 packets transmitted, 0 received, 100% packet loss         │
         │                                                              │
         │  ❌ 100% loss even though policy-verdict showed allow —       │
         │  that's a ROUTING problem. Check cilium endpoint inspect     │
         │  and cilium node list for the tunnel/native routing state.   │
         │                                                              │
         │  Step 4 (app) — port answers, but the app itself fails:      │
         │  $ kubectl exec -it anihpj-7d9f6 -- nc -zv 10.244.2.9 5432   │
         │  Connection to 10.244.2.9 5432 port [tcp/postgresql] ok!     │
         │  $ kubectl logs anihpj-7d9f6 --tail=3                        │
         │  django.db.utils.OperationalError: FATAL: password           │
         │  authentication failed for user "anihpj"                     │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Port 5432 answers — Cilium and routing are both           │
         │  innocent. This is Step 4: an app/credentials bug only.      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    9.2  Using cilium monitor to See Policy Decisions in Real Time
         - Filtering by pod, namespace, verdict
         - Understanding the output: action allow vs action deny vs action audit
         - Example: Watching anihpj→postgres traffic with live monitor

         [cilium monitor --type policy-verdict — ANNOTATED OUTPUT]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  $ cilium monitor --type policy-verdict                      │
         │                                                              │
         │  Policy verdict log: flow 0x9f3a2c17 local EP ID             │
         │  3902, remote ID 1848, dst port 5432, proto 6,               │
         │  ingress true, action allow, match L3-L4,                    │
         │  10.244.1.7:51122 -> 10.244.2.9:5432 tcp SYN                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ ALLOWED: anihpj pod (EP 3902) -> postgres pod             │
         │     (EP 1848) on 5432 — a real L3-L4 rule matched.           │
         │                                                              │
         │  Policy verdict log: flow 0x0 local EP ID 3902,              │
         │  remote ID 4, dst port 53, proto 17, ingress                 │
         │  false, action deny, match none,                             │
         │  10.244.1.7:57991 -> 10.244.0.10:53 udp                      │
         │                                                              │
         │  ❌ DENIED: anihpj pod (EP 3902) -> CoreDNS (EP 4)            │
         │     on port 53. match none = NO rule allows this —           │
         │     pod has zero DNS egress rules configured!                │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 READING THE VERDICT FIELDS:                               │
         │    action allow  = permitted by a matching rule              │
         │    action deny   = blocked by an explicit rule               │
         │    action audit  = would be denied, but policy is            │
         │                    in audit mode — logged only               │
         │    match L3-L4   = a real selector/port matched              │
         │    match none    = no rule matched this flow                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 FILTER FLAGS THAT ACTUALLY EXIST ON THIS CMD:             │
         │    -t, --type policy-verdict   only verdict events           │
         │    --from <endpoint-id>        only FROM that EP             │
         │    --to <endpoint-id>          only TO that EP               │
         │    --related-to <endpoint-id>  either side = that EP         │
         │    -v, -vv                     more verbose output           │
         │    -j, --json                  machine-readable JSON         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [VERDICT ACTIONS AT A GLANCE — allow / deny / audit]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  VERDICT ACTIONS AT A GLANCE — allow / deny / audit          │
         │                                                              │
         │  ACTION  MEANING                  NEXT STEP                  │
         │  ──────  ───────────────────────  ─────────────────────────  │
         │  allow   rule permits this flow   nothing — flow passes      │
         │  deny    rule blocks this flow    fix selectors, see 9.3     │
         │  audit   WOULD deny, but the      edit the rule NOW before   │
         │          policy is audit-only     flipping enforcement on    │
         │                                                              │
         │  Example — the SAME anihpj→postgres flow, but the CNP is     │
         │  still in audit mode (Ch7.6), not enforcing yet:             │
         │                                                              │
         │  Policy verdict log: flow local EP ID 3902, remote ID        │
         │  1848, dst port 5432, proto 6, ingress true, action          │
         │  audit, match none, 10.244.1.7:51125 -> 10.244.2.9:5432      │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚠️  action audit + match none means this flow is STILL      │
         │  let through — audit mode never actually blocks. Don't       │
         │  mistake this line for a real deny (see Ch7.6 / Ch15.1).     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    9.3  Common Policy Mistakes and How to Fix Them
         - Mistake 1: Label selector doesn't match (check with --show-labels)
         - Mistake 2: Namespace not included (policies are namespace-scoped!)
         - Mistake 3: Forgot to allow DNS (pods need port 53 to CoreDNS!)
         - Mistake 4: Policy applied to wrong direction (ingress vs egress)
         [TABLE: Common mistakes → Symptoms → Fix]

         [TOP 5 NETWORK POLICY MISTAKES]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  MISTAKE              SYMPTOM             FIX                │
         │  ───────────────────  ──────────────────  ─────────────────  │
         │  Label mismatch       Policy has no       Check labels with  │
         │  (typo in selector)   effect              kubectl get pods   │
         │                                           --show-labels      │
         │  Forgot DNS allow     Pods can't resolve  Allow egress to    │
         │  (port 53 blocked)    any hostnames       kube-system/CoreDNS│
         │  Wrong namespace      Policy doesn't      Verify -n flag     │
         │                       apply to pods       matches            │
         │  Ingress vs Egress    Blocked in wrong    Check direction:   │
         │  confusion            direction           ingress=inbound    │
         │                                           egress=outbound    │
         │  Too restrictive      Everything breaks!  Start with audit   │
         │                       😱                  mode first!        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [MISTAKE 3 IN PRACTICE — anihpj's Missing DNS Egress Rule]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  MISTAKE 3 IN PRACTICE — anihpj's Missing DNS Egress Rule    │
         │                                                              │
         │  ❌ BEFORE — egress locks anihpj to postgres only, no DNS:    │
         │                                                              │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    endpointSelector:                                         │
         │      matchLabels:                                            │
         │        app: anihpj                                           │
         │    egress:                                                   │
         │    - toEndpoints:                                            │
         │      - matchLabels:                                          │
         │          app: postgres                                       │
         │      toPorts:                                                │
         │      - ports:                                                │
         │        - port: "5432"                                        │
         │          protocol: TCP                                       │
         │                                                              │
         │  Result: nslookup postgres.anihpj.svc.cluster.local hangs —  │
         │  port 53 to CoreDNS was never allowed, so every DNS query    │
         │  from anihpj is silently dropped before it leaves the pod.   │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ AFTER — add a SECOND egress rule just for DNS (port 53):  │
         │                                                              │
         │  egress:                                                     │
         │  - toEndpoints:                                              │
         │      - matchLabels:                                          │
         │          app: postgres                                       │
         │    toPorts:                                                  │
         │      - ports:                                                │
         │        - port: "5432"                                        │
         │          protocol: TCP                                       │
         │  - toEndpoints:                    # ADDED: DNS egress rule  │
         │      - matchLabels:                                          │
         │          k8s:io.kubernetes.pod.namespace: kube-system        │
         │          k8s-app: kube-dns                                   │
         │    toPorts:                                                  │
         │      - ports:                                                │
         │        - port: "53"                                          │
         │          protocol: ANY                                       │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Every CiliumNetworkPolicy with ANY egress rule needs its  │
         │     OWN explicit DNS rule — Kubernetes' default "allow all   │
         │     DNS" disappears the moment one egress rule exists at all.│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    9.4  Debugging DNS-Based and Cross-Node Policy Issues
         - Two categories that break the systematic 9.1 flow: DNS-based
           (toFQDNs) policy hides its real matching logic behind a
           dynamic IP cache; cross-node traffic hides a same-node "fast
           path" that never exercises the real distributed path at all
         - Scenario A: anihpj's egress to api.stripe.com is denied even
           though nslookup succeeds from inside the pod
         - Scenario B: a CiliumNetworkPolicy that works when anihpj and
           postgres land on the same node fails once the scheduler
           splits them across two different nodes
         - Appendix C.2 already tables the SYMPTOMS for both of these —
           this section is the step-by-step, command-by-command walk-
           through that neither C.2 nor 9.1-9.3 actually spells out

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Symptom: anihpj's payment code calls api.stripe.com (the    │
         │  toFQDNs rule from Ch7.5/Ch8.2). nslookup api.stripe.com     │
         │  from inside the anihpj pod succeeds and prints an IP — but  │
         │  the actual HTTPS request to Stripe just hangs and times out.│
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 1 — Don't trust nslookup alone; see what Cilium's own  │
         │  DNS proxy actually observed with hubble observe:            │
         │  $ hubble observe --pod anihpj/anihpj-7d9f6 \                │
         │      --protocol dns --last 5 -o json                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  {                                                           │
         │    "time": "2026-07-10T14:22:07.118Z",                       │
         │    "flow": { "l7": { "dns": {                                │
         │      "query": "api.stripe.com.",                             │
         │      "rcode": 0,                                             │
         │      "ips": ["151.101.1.2"],                                 │
         │      "cnames": ["stripe-gateway.map.fastly.net."],           │
         │      "ttl": 45                                               │
         │    }}}                                                       │
         │  }                                                           │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ DNS itself is fine — rcode 0 = NOERROR, and an IP came    │
         │     back. But look at "cnames": Stripe's answer is a CNAME   │
         │     chain, not a direct A record for api.stripe.com.         │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2 — Confirm the policy verdict for the actual TCP      │
         │  connect (not the DNS lookup) to that IP on :443:            │
         │  $ cilium monitor --type policy-verdict --related-to 4471    │
         │                                                              │
         │  Policy verdict log: flow local EP ID 4471, remote ID 0,     │
         │  dst port 443, proto 6, egress, action deny, match none,     │
         │  10.244.1.19:44210 -> 151.101.1.2:443 tcp SYN                │
         │                                                              │
         │  ❌ action deny, match none — no toFQDNs entry covers         │
         │     151.101.1.2, even though the pod DID just resolve        │
         │     api.stripe.com and DID get that exact IP back.           │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 3 — Why: Cilium's FQDN proxy caches a resolved IP      │
         │  under the NAME it saw in that DNS answer segment. With a    │
         │  CNAME chain, that's the CNAME's own target — NOT            │
         │  automatically the name the app originally queried. Confirm: │
         │  $ cilium-dbg fqdn cache list                                │
         │  ENDPOINT  FQDN                            TTL  IP           │
         │  4471      stripe-gateway.map.fastly.net.  45   151.101.1.2  │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚠️  THE TRAP: toFQDNs' matchName: api.stripe.com matches the│
         │     name the APP queried — but the cached IP is filed under  │
         │     stripe-gateway.map.fastly.net, a name NO rule mentions.  │
         │     "The pod resolved fine" and "a rule covers that IP" are  │
         │     two different questions — this scenario answers YES/NO.  │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 4 — Cilium DOES keep re-evaluating toFQDNs dynamically │
         │  as DNS answers change (Ch7.5, Ch20.2) — that part isn't     │
         │  broken here; the rule is just missing a name. FIX: add the  │
         │  CNAME target to BOTH toPorts.rules.dns and toFQDNs, the     │
         │  same pairing Ch8.2 already requires:                        │
         │      - matchName: "api.stripe.com"                           │
         │      - matchPattern: "*.map.fastly.net"                      │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 nslookup only proves the pod CAN resolve a name — it      │
         │     says nothing about which name Cilium's proxy filed the   │
         │     IP under. Always check hubble observe --protocol dns     │
         │     (or cilium-dbg fqdn cache list) before editing any YAML. │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Symptom: the CNP allowing anihpj -> postgres:5432 hasn't    │
         │  changed in weeks. After a node drain reschedules postgres   │
         │  onto a DIFFERENT node than anihpj, connections hang and     │
         │  time out — but forcing both pods back onto the SAME node    │
         │  (as a quick test) makes the problem disappear completely.   │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚠️  DON'T treat that same-node test as proof the policy is  │
         │     fine: same-node traffic never leaves the node at all     │
         │     (Ch4.7 axis (a)) — it skips cross-node routing ENTIRELY, │
         │     so it only rules out a pure selector bug, nothing more.  │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 1 — Check policy-verdict on the SOURCE node (anihpj's  │
         │  node) first, same as any single-node debug session:         │
         │  $ cilium-dbg monitor --type policy-verdict --from 5218      │
         │                                                              │
         │  Policy verdict log: flow local EP ID 5218, remote ID 9034,  │
         │  dst port 5432, proto 6, egress, action allow, match L3-L4,  │
         │  10.244.1.19:51230 -> 10.244.2.41:5432 tcp SYN               │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ action allow — the SOURCE node's Cilium agent already     │
         │     approved this flow and released it to the datapath.      │
         │     This node's policy engine is NOT the problem here.       │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2 — Now check the DESTINATION node's agent too — Ch9's │
         │  own Mistake 1: a verdict only shows on whichever node is    │
         │  actually enforcing that flow, and for a cross-node flow     │
         │  that could be EITHER side:                                  │
         │  $ cilium-dbg monitor --type drop --to 9034                  │
         │  (run against postgres's OWN node's cilium-agent pod)        │
         │  ...(no output at all — not one line for this flow)          │
         │                                                              │
         │  ❌ NOTHING — not a DROP, not a policy-verdict, no record of  │
         │     this flow ever reaching postgres's node. Cilium there    │
         │     never got a chance to allow or deny it — because the     │
         │     packet itself never arrived at that node to begin with.  │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 3 — ALLOWED on the source's policy engine but NEVER    │
         │  SEEN at the destination's Cilium agent = a routing problem, │
         │  not a policy problem. Check the network, not the YAML:      │
         │  $ cilium node list                                          │
         │  (confirms postgres's new node and its Pod CIDR, e.g.        │
         │  10.244.2.0/24 — then check the office router's own route    │
         │  table for that exact CIDR, per Ch4.6/4.7's Native Routing)  │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 anihpj's cluster runs Native Routing (Ch4.7): the office  │
         │     network's router — not Cilium — must already know how    │
         │     to route every node's Pod CIDR. A node added without a   │
         │     matching route entry looks IDENTICAL to a policy DENY:   │
         │     no RST, no ICMP, just a SYN that silently disappears.    │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚠️  KEY INSIGHT: these two silent drops are different bugs  │
         │     that present IDENTICALLY to anihpj — "it just hangs."    │
         │     The only way to tell them apart is checking BOTH nodes'  │
         │     monitor output: allow-on-source + nothing-on-destination │
         │     means routing; a DROP with a reason on either node means │
         │     policy. Never diagnose a cross-node flow from one side.  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Tunnel-mode variant: if a cluster runs Tunnel mode instead of
           anihpj's Native Routing (Ch4.6/4.7), the equivalent cross-node
           silent failure is usually a per-node MTU mismatch — a NIC that
           leaves no room for the VXLAN/Geneve header truncates or
           silently drops the encapsulated packet, with the exact same
           no-RST, no-ICMP symptom as a policy DENY

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 9):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Assuming cilium monitor shows verdicts for the whole cluster
            → TRUTH: cilium monitor only reports events from the ONE cilium-agent it's
              attached to. A verdict shows up on whichever node is actually enforcing
              that flow — which may not be the node you happened to exec into.
            → FIX: kubectl get pod <pod> -o wide to find the pod's node, then exec into
              the Cilium agent pod on THAT node. For a cluster-wide view, use Hubble.

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Treating a connection that just "hangs" and times out as an
            application bug instead of a policy problem
            → TRUTH: a Cilium policy DENY is a silent packet drop — no TCP RST, no ICMP
              "port unreachable." A blocked connection looks exactly like a slow app or
              a network black hole; it never announces itself as "connection refused."
            → FIX: before touching anihpj/Django code, run cilium monitor --type
              policy-verdict (or check Hubble) for that exact source/destination/port
              and look for action deny.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Reusing an endpoint ID from an earlier debugging session to
            filter cilium monitor --related-to
            → TRUTH: endpoint IDs are ephemeral and node-local — the cilium-agent
              assigns a NEW one every time a pod is created, restarted, or rescheduled
              to another node.
            → FIX: re-run cilium endpoint list immediately before filtering monitor
              output; never assume yesterday's ID still matches today's anihpj pod.

         ❌ MISTAKE 4: Assuming a toFQDNs rule naming the domain your app calls
            (matchName: api.stripe.com) automatically covers every IP behind it
            → TRUTH: when the DNS answer is a CNAME chain, Cilium's FQDN proxy
              caches the resolved IP under the CNAME's OWN target name — not
              automatically under the name your matchName rule lists (see 9.4).
            → FIX: run cilium-dbg fqdn cache list (or hubble observe --protocol
              dns -o json) to see which name the IP is actually filed under, then
              add that name too, in BOTH toPorts.rules.dns and toFQDNs.

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 9):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. What's the first thing to check when a pod can't connect?
    Q2. How do you see policy verdicts in real time?
    Q3. Why does blocking all egress break DNS?
    Q4. What happens if a label selector doesn't match any pods?

    Q5. In cilium monitor --type policy-verdict output, a flow shows
        "action deny, match none". What does match none mean?
        A) The flow matched zero bytes of payload
        B) No CiliumNetworkPolicy selector matched this flow at all —
           there is no rule permitting it
        C) The policy YAML is corrupted and must be reapplied
        D) The destination pod does not exist

    Q6. A developer says: "My curl to the database just hangs for 30
        seconds and then times out — I never see 'connection refused',
        so it can't be a network policy." Explain why this reasoning is
        wrong, and name the exact command you'd run to confirm or rule
        out a policy DENY.

    Q7. You exec into the Cilium agent pod on Node A and run cilium
        monitor --type policy-verdict, but you see NO output at all for
        a failing connection between a pod on Node A and a pod on Node
        B. What is the MOST likely explanation?
        A) cilium monitor cannot show policy-verdict events, only drops
        B) The relevant verdict is being logged on Node B's cilium-agent,
           not Node A's
        C) Policy verdict logging is disabled by default and must be
           turned on via a Helm value
        D) The two pods must be in the same namespace for cilium monitor
           to report anything

    Q8. Walk through the debugging flow from 9.1 for this scenario: the
        anihpj Django pod resolves postgres.anihpj.svc.cluster.local via
        DNS successfully, but cilium monitor --type policy-verdict shows
        "action deny, match none" for its traffic to the postgres pod on
        port 5432. Which category of problem is this (DNS, policy,
        routing, or application), and which two things in the
        CiliumNetworkPolicy would you check first to fix it?

    Q9. A hubble observe --protocol dns -o json event for anihpj's
        egress shows "query": "api.stripe.com.", "ips":
        ["151.101.1.2"], and "cnames": ["stripe-gw.map.fastly.net."].
        The toFQDNs rule only lists matchName: api.stripe.com, and
        the connection to 151.101.1.2 is denied. Why?
        A) Because rcode was non-zero, meaning the DNS lookup itself
           failed
        B) Because Cilium ignores CNAME records entirely and never
           permits the connection
        C) Because Cilium's FQDN proxy filed the resolved IP under
           the CNAME's target name, which no toFQDNs rule covers
        D) Because toFQDNs rules cannot be combined with any domain
           that uses a CNAME, under any circumstances

    Q10. A CiliumNetworkPolicy allowing anihpj -> postgres:5432 works
         perfectly whenever both pods land on the same node, but hangs
         and times out whenever the scheduler splits them across two
         different nodes. cilium-dbg monitor --type policy-verdict on
         anihpj's own node shows action allow for the flow. cilium-dbg
         monitor on postgres's node shows NO output at all for it — no
         drop, no policy-verdict, nothing. Is this a policy problem?
         Name the single concept from Ch4.6/4.7 you'd check next, and
         explain why the same-node test can't prove the policy itself
         is correct.

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 9):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ Debug pod connectivity in a fixed order — DNS, then policy verdict,
            then routing, then the application layer — never skip straight to
            guessing at app code.

         ✅ cilium monitor --type policy-verdict reports action allow/deny/audit
            and match L3-L4/none per flow — read the endpoint IDs and match
            field, not just "did it get blocked."

         ✅ A Cilium policy DENY is a SILENT drop (no RST, no ICMP) — a "hanging"
            or timed-out connection is a classic policy symptom, not proof the
            application itself is broken.

         ✅ Nearly every real-world CiliumNetworkPolicy bug traces back to a
            SELECTOR problem — label typos, a missing namespace match, forgotten
            DNS egress, or the wrong direction.

         ✅ cilium monitor is scoped to the ONE node it's attached to, and
            endpoint IDs are ephemeral — check cilium endpoint list and pick the
            right node before filtering.

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         You now have a repeatable playbook for answering "why can't my pod
         connect" — DNS, policy verdict, routing, application, in that order.
         Chapter 10 shifts from defense to architecture: Cilium's built-in
         service mesh, which delivers mTLS, L7 traffic management, and
         observability between anihpj's services WITHOUT injecting an Envoy
         sidecar into every pod — less RAM, less latency, and one fewer moving
         part to debug next time something "just hangs."

Chapter 10: Cilium's Built-In Service Mesh — No Sidecar Required!
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ Why a service mesh exists at all — the observability/security
            problem it solves between services (recap from 10.1)
         ✅ Why "sidecar-less" is NOT the same claim as "proxy-less" — the
            precise distinction the CCA exam tests
         ✅ How eBPF rewrites a pod's own socket at connect() time so plain
            L3/L4 traffic (like anihpj → postgres) never touches a proxy
         ✅ Why L7 features (HTTP routing, canaries) still use Envoy — but
            ONE shared instance per NODE, never injected per pod
         ✅ How Cilium's Mutual Authentication (mTLS) proves workload
            identity via SPIFFE/SPIRE — and why that is NOT the same as
            encrypting the traffic
         ✅ How to require mutual auth on anihpj → postgres traffic with a
            CiliumNetworkPolicy's authentication.mode field
         ✅ How to configure a 90/10 canary split between anihpj-v1 and
            anihpj-v2 using the CiliumEnvoyConfig CRD
         ✅ How Ingress and Gateway API get external traffic INTO the mesh
            via the same per-node Envoy (Path B), and which of Gateway
            API's advantages over Ingress are real, verifiable mechanisms

    10.1 What is a Service Mesh? (And Why Traditional Ones Are Heavy)
         - The problem a service mesh solves: observability + security between services
         - Traditional approach: Istio/Linkerd with sidecar proxies (Envoy)
         - The sidecar problem: Every pod gets an extra container (200MB+ RAM each!)
         [DIAGRAM: Sidecar mesh vs Cilium sidecar-less mesh]

         [SIDECAR vs SIDECAR-LESS MESH — Resource Comparison]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────┐  ┌────────────────────────────┐
         │  ISTIO (Sidecar Mesh)              │  │  CILIUM (Sidecar-less Mesh)│
         │                                    │  │                            │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐  │  │  ┌──────────────────────┐  │
         │  │          POD                  │  │  │  │        POD           │  │
         │  │                              │  │  │  │                      │  │
         │  │  ┌─────────┐ ┌────────────┐  │  │  │  │  ┌─────────┐        │  │
         │  │  │  App    │ │  Envoy     │  │  │  │  │  │  App    │        │  │
         │  │  │(anihpj) │ │  Sidecar   │  │  │  │  │  │(anihpj) │        │  │
         │  │  │ 100 MB  │ │  200 MB    │  │  │  │  │  │ 100 MB  │        │  │
         │  │  └────┬────┘ └─────┬──────┘  │  │  │  │  └────┬────┘        │  │
         │  │       │            │         │  │  │  │       │             │  │
         │  │       └──localhost─┘         │  │  │  │       │ eBPF kernel │  │
         │  │          extra hop!          │  │  │  │       │ (0 MB extra)│  │
         │  └──────────────────────────────┘  │  │  │       │ ⚡ native!  │  │
         │                                    │  │  └──────────────────────┘  │
         │  ❌ 200MB overhead per pod         │  │                            │
         │  ❌ Extra network hop (latency)    │  │  ✅ 0 MB overhead per pod │
         │  ❌ Complex to manage + upgrade   │  │  ✅ No extra network hop   │
         │                                    │  │  ✅ Kernel-native speed    │
         └────────────────────────────────────┘  └────────────────────────────┘

    10.2 How Cilium Service Mesh Works Under the Hood
         - Most "service mesh" traffic — plain TCP/UDP, like anihpj → postgres —
           never touches a proxy at all. eBPF rewrites the destination directly.
         - eBPF intercepts the connect() syscall at the SOCKET layer (a cgroup
           hook) and rewrites the destination INSIDE THE KERNEL, before a
           single packet is built
         - L7 features (HTTP path routing, canary weighting, retries) DO still
           use Envoy — but Cilium runs ONE Envoy instance per NODE, shared by
           every pod on it, never one injected per pod
         - That "per-node, not per-pod" distinction is what "sidecar-less"
           actually means — it is not a claim that no proxy exists anywhere

         [CILIUM SERVICE MESH — TWO DATA PATHS, ONE AGENT]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  PATH A: L3/L4 (plain TCP) — anihpj → postgres               │
         │    1. anihpj calls connect(postgres-svc-ip, 5432)            │
         │    2. eBPF cgroup/connect4 hook intercepts INSIDE the kernel │
         │    3. eBPF looks up the real backend: postgres-0 pod IP      │
         │    4. Socket destination REWRITTEN before any packet exists  │
         │    5. Packet leaves already addressed to postgres-0 — 1 hop  │
         │                                                              │
         │  PATH B: L7 (HTTP) — jobpost API canary, header-based routes │
         │    1. anihpj calls the jobpost Service (HTTP)                │
         │    2. eBPF redirects the socket to a LOCAL Envoy proxy       │
         │    3. Envoy (ONE instance per NODE, not per pod!) reads the  │
         │       HTTP request and applies routing/canary rules          │
         │    4. Envoy forwards to anihpj-v1 or anihpj-v2 as configured │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 "Sidecar-less" = no proxy container injected INTO a pod.  │
         │     Path A never touches a proxy. Path B still uses Envoy —  │
         │     just ONE per node, shared by every pod that needs L7.    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         📖 Key Term: Socket-Level Load Balancing
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Socket-level load balancing is Cilium choosing a Service's  │
         │  backend pod by rewriting a POD'S OWN SOCKET at connect()    │
         │  time — inside the kernel, via an eBPF cgroup hook — before  │
         │  a single network packet is ever constructed.                │
         │                                                              │
         │  • Traditional kube-proxy: builds the packet FIRST, then     │
         │    rewrites it in netfilter/iptables (or IPVS) — extra work  │
         │    per packet, on every packet, in the data path.            │
         │  • Cilium socket-level LB: rewrites ONCE, at connect() time. │
         │    Every packet after that flows directly, pod-to-pod.       │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Analogy: kube-proxy is a mail-forwarding sticker slapped  │
         │     on every single envelope. Cilium's socket LB is telling  │
         │     the sender the CORRECT address before they seal anything.│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [L3/L4 eBPF-ONLY PATH vs L7 ENVOY-ASSISTED PATH]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ASPECT           L3/L4 (eBPF-only)   L7 (Envoy-assisted)    │
         │  ───────────      ─────────────────   ────────────────────   │
         │  Proxy involved?  NONE                Envoy (1 per NODE)     │
         │  Enforced where   Kernel (socket)     Userspace (per node)   │
         │  Example traffic  anihpj → postgres   jobpost REST API       │
         │  Sees HTTP paths? No                  Yes                    │
         │  Used for         Plain connectivity  Canary, header route,  │
         │                   + L3/L4 policy      retries, mTLS authN    │
         │  Exam keyword     "socket LB"         "CiliumEnvoyConfig"    │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Every anihpj request touches Path A. Only requests that   │
         │     need HTTP-aware smarts (canary, path routing) add Path B.│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    10.3 Enabling Mutual TLS (mTLS) for anihpj
         - What mutual auth does: both sides cryptographically PROVE their
           identity before a connection is allowed through — not just encrypt
         - Cilium's real feature name is Mutual Authentication (Beta) — it is
           built on SPIFFE/SPIRE, not a custom home-grown PKI
         - Cilium automates SVID issuance and rotation via SPIRE — you never
           hand-manage a per-pod certificate yourself
         - Step-by-step: require mutual auth on anihpj → postgres traffic by
           adding an authentication block to a CiliumNetworkPolicy
         - Critical distinction the exam loves: mutual auth ≠ encryption —
           pair it with WireGuard or IPsec if the traffic itself must be
           encrypted, not merely authenticated

         📖 Key Term: SPIFFE / SPIRE (Cilium's mTLS Identity Backbone)
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  SPIFFE (Secure Production Identity Framework For Everyone)  │
         │  is the STANDARD Cilium's Mutual Authentication feature uses │
         │  to issue every workload a verifiable identity — an SVID     │
         │  (SPIFFE Verifiable Identity Document, an X.509 cert).       │
         │                                                              │
         │  SPIRE is the production implementation of SPIFFE that       │
         │  actually ISSUES those SVIDs. Cilium's mutual-auth feature   │
         │  has only been validated against SPIRE (Beta feature).       │
         │                                                              │
         │  • SPIRE Server — the root of trust for the whole cluster    │
         │  • SPIRE Agent  — runs on EVERY node, hands each pod its SVID│
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Analogy: SPIRE Server is the passport office. The per-node│
         │     SPIRE Agent is the local clerk who checks you're really  │
         │     you (node + labels) before handing over your passport.   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [MUTUAL AUTHENTICATION — anihpj → postgres HANDSHAKE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  1. SPIRE Server issues the cluster's root of trust          │
         │  2. SPIRE Agent (runs on every node) verifies a pod's own    │
         │     node + labels are legit, then requests that pod's SVID   │
         │  3. anihpj pod and postgres pod each hold their own SVID     │
         │     (an X.509 cert with identity spiffe://<domain>/<path>)   │
         │  4. anihpj tries to connect to postgres                      │
         │  5. Cilium agent intercepts and runs an OUT-OF-BAND mTLS     │
         │     handshake — both sides present their SVID to prove WHO   │
         │     they are — BEFORE the connection is allowed through      │
         │  6. CiliumNetworkPolicy's authentication: mode: required is  │
         │     what makes step 5 mandatory for this traffic             │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 The handshake proves WHO is connecting. Whether the bytes │
         │     on the wire are ENCRYPTED is a separate concern (below). │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CILIUMNETWORKPOLICY — Requiring Mutual Auth, anihpj → postgres]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind: CiliumNetworkPolicy                                   │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: anihpj-require-mutual-auth-to-postgres              │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    endpointSelector:                                         │
         │      matchLabels:                                            │
         │        app: postgres        # policy applies TO postgres pods│
         │    ingress:                                                  │
         │    - fromEndpoints:          # ALLOW incoming traffic FROM:  │
         │      - matchLabels:                                          │
         │          app: anihpj         # ...only the anihpj backend    │
         │      authentication:                                         │
         │        mode: "required"     # ⬅ enforces the SPIFFE/SPIRE    │
         │                              #   mTLS handshake before this  │
         │                              #   ingress traffic is allowed  │
         │      toPorts:                                                │
         │      - ports:                                                │
         │        - port: "5432"                                        │
         │          protocol: TCP                                       │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 authentication.mode: "required" lives INSIDE an ingress/  │
         │     egress rule, next to fromEndpoints/toPorts — not a top-  │
         │     level spec field. Beta feature (Cilium 1.19/1.20).       │
         │  ⚠️  This block only AUTHENTICATES — it does NOT encrypt the │
         │     connection. Pair with WireGuard/IPsec for that (Ch 16).  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    10.4 Traffic Management — Canary Deployments with Cilium
         - What is a canary deployment? Run anihpj-v1 (stable) and anihpj-v2
           (canary) at the same time, and shift a SMALL % of live traffic to
           v2 first — e.g., 10% to v2, 90% staying on v1
         - Cilium's real mechanism is the CiliumEnvoyConfig CRD, not a
           dedicated "canary" resource — it hands Cilium's per-node Envoy
           raw xDS config that defines weighted_clusters
         - This is an L7 (HTTP) mechanism: it inspects real HTTP requests to
           split them, so it does NOT apply to non-HTTP backends like a raw
           PostgreSQL connection
         - Real example: shift 10% of jobpost API traffic to anihpj-v2, watch
           Hubble for error rate/latency, then ramp the weight up gradually

         📖 Key Term: CiliumEnvoyConfig
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CiliumEnvoyConfig is the CRD that lets you hand Cilium's    │
         │  built-in, per-node Envoy proxy a slice of RAW Envoy xDS     │
         │  config (Listeners, Routes, Clusters) — the same config      │
         │  format Istio/Envoy users already know.                      │
         │                                                              │
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind:       CiliumEnvoyConfig                               │
         │              (or CiliumClusterwideEnvoyConfig, cluster-wide) │
         │                                                              │
         │  • spec.services        — which K8s Service(s) this hijacks  │
         │  • spec.backendServices  — the real backend Services it can  │
         │                            route to (e.g. anihpj-v1/-v2)     │
         │  • spec.resources        — the literal Envoy xDS resources   │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 This is Cilium's escape hatch for anything eBPF alone     │
         │     can't do at L7: weighted canaries, header-based routing, │
         │     retries/timeouts, regex rewrites — Envoy-grade power,    │
         │     without an Envoy sidecar in every single pod.            │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CANARY TRAFFIC SPLIT — jobpost API, anihpj-v1 vs anihpj-v2]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                   ┌─────────────────────┐                    │
         │    All jobpost    │  Envoy (per node)   │   90% ─▶ anihpj-v1 │
         │    API requests──▶│  weighted_clusters  │          (stable)  │
         │                   │  90 / 10 split      │   10% ─▶ anihpj-v2 │
         │                   └─────────────────────┘          (canary)  │
         │                                                              │
         │  ██████████████████████████████████████░░░░  90%  anihpj-v1  │
         │  ████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  10%  anihpj-v2 │
         │                                                              │
         │  Both anihpj-v1 and anihpj-v2 run as SEPARATE Deployments +  │
         │  Services at the same time — the split is decided by ENVOY'S │
         │  ROUTING WEIGHT, not by how many replicas each version has.  │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Watch anihpj-v2's error rate/latency via Hubble (Ch13).   │
         │     Confident? Raise its weight. Bad numbers? Set it to 0 —  │
         │     no redeploy needed, just re-apply the CiliumEnvoyConfig. │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CILIUMENVOYCONFIG — 90/10 Canary Split for the anihpj jobpost API]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind: CiliumEnvoyConfig                                     │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: anihpj-jobpost-canary                               │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    services:                 # the Service clients still call│
         │      - name: anihpj                                          │
         │        namespace: anihpj                                     │
         │    backendServices:          # the REAL versioned backends   │
         │      - name: anihpj-v1                                       │
         │        namespace: anihpj                                     │
         │      - name: anihpj-v2                                       │
         │        namespace: anihpj                                     │
         │    resources:                # raw Envoy xDS config, verbatim│
         │      - "@type": .../Listener                                 │
         │        name: anihpj-canary-listener                          │
         │        # ...HttpConnectionManager -> RouteConfiguration...   │
         │      - "@type": .../RouteConfiguration                       │
         │        virtual_hosts:                                        │
         │        - routes:                                             │
         │          - match: { prefix: "/" }                            │
         │            route:                                            │
         │              weighted_clusters:                              │
         │                clusters:                                     │
         │                - name: "anihpj/anihpj-v1"                    │
         │                  weight: 90   # ⬅ stable version, 90% of hits│
         │                - name: "anihpj/anihpj-v2"                    │
         │                  weight: 10   # ⬅ canary version, 10% of hits│
         │      # ...+ 2 Cluster (EDS) resources, one per backend...    │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Cilium ships these Envoy resources AS-IS to its per-node  │
         │     Envoy — K8s does NOT validate them. A typo only surfaces │
         │     in the Cilium agent's logs, not kubectl apply errors.    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    10.5 Ingress and Gateway API — Routing External Traffic Into the Mesh
         - Sections 10.1-10.4 covered traffic BETWEEN services inside the mesh
           (east-west); this section covers traffic getting INTO the mesh from
           OUTSIDE the cluster (north-south) — a different problem, same Path B
         - Kubernetes Ingress: the original, minimal API — ONE Ingress resource,
           path/host-based routing only, limited to HTTP/HTTPS
         - Cilium ships a REAL Ingress controller: set ingressClassName: cilium
           and traffic is handed to the SAME per-node Envoy from 10.2 (Path B) —
           eBPF intercepts it (via the TPROXY kernel facility) and forwards it;
           no separate Ingress-only proxy Deployment is ever created
         - Gateway API: the newer, Kubernetes-SIG-standard replacement — a
           FAMILY of role-split resources (GatewayClass, Gateway, HTTPRoute,
           GRPCRoute, TLSRoute) instead of one do-everything object
         - Cilium implements Gateway API too, with the same gatewayClassName:
           cilium and the same per-node Envoy — Ingress and Gateway API are
           BOTH Path B, never a new "Path C"
         - The CCA exam explicitly names "benefits of Gateway API over
           Ingress" as a graded topic — every benefit below is grounded in a
           real, verifiable mechanism, not a marketing claim

         [EXTERNAL TRAFFIC INTO THE MESH — Both APIs Ride Path B]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Client Browser ──HTTPS──▶ LoadBalancer/NodePort Service     │
         │    (jobpost.example.com)      (Cilium-managed, L4 only)      │
         │                                                              │
         │  LoadBalancer/NodePort ──▶ eBPF (TPROXY) ──▶ per-node        │
         │                                    Envoy (Path B, 10.2)      │
         │                                                              │
         │  Envoy reads the HTTP Host header + path, matches the        │
         │  Ingress or HTTPRoute rule, forwards to the anihpj Svc       │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Same shared per-node Envoy as the L7 canary routing       │
         │     in 10.4 — Ingress/Gateway API do not add a NEW           │
         │     proxy, they add a NEW way to configure Path B's one.     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Kubernetes Ingress in practice: a single Ingress object mixes
           infrastructure concerns (which controller: ingressClassName) with
           application routing (host/path/backend) in ONE manifest
         - Cilium's Ingress supports Exact/Prefix/ImplementationSpecific path
           types, host-based rules, TLS termination, and a TLS Passthrough
           mode for SNI-based backend selection — but nothing beyond
           HTTP(S), and no native traffic-splitting field at all
         - Below: the anihpj-jobpost Ingress, routing jobpost.example.com to
           the anihpj Service

         [INGRESS — jobpost.example.com → anihpj, HTTP-Only Routing]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                            │
         │  kind: Ingress                                               │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: anihpj-jobpost-ingress                              │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    ingressClassName: cilium     # ⬅ Cilium owns this object  │
         │    rules:                                                    │
         │    - host: jobpost.example.com  # host-based routing         │
         │      http:                                                   │
         │        paths:                                                │
         │        - path: /                # path-based routing         │
         │          pathType: Prefix                                    │
         │          backend:                                            │
         │            service:                                          │
         │              name: anihpj        # the K8s Service, not Pod  │
         │              port:                                           │
         │                number: 80                                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 ONE object does infra (ingressClassName), routing         │
         │     (host/path), AND app wiring (backend.service) at once.   │
         │  ⚠️  No native field for weighting/splitting traffic here —  │
         │     that needs a CONTROLLER-SPECIFIC annotation (e.g.        │
         │     nginx's canary-weight). Cilium's Ingress has none;       │
         │     drop to 10.4's CiliumEnvoyConfig to split HTTP traffic.  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Gateway API fixes exactly what Ingress conflates: it splits ONE
           object into role-scoped resources matching Gateway API's own
           three official personas — Infrastructure Provider, Cluster
           Operator, and Application Developer
         - Real, verifiable benefits over Ingress:
             1) Role split — GatewayClass/Gateway (platform team) vs
                HTTPRoute/GRPCRoute (app team) are separate objects with
                separate RBAC, not annotations bolted onto one manifest
             2) Native weighted traffic splitting — HTTPRoute's backendRefs
                list has a real weight field in the spec itself; a 90/10
                anihpj-v1/v2 split needs NO vendor annotation and no
                hand-written Envoy xDS (contrast with 10.4's
                CiliumEnvoyConfig, which existed BECAUSE Ingress had no
                such field)
             3) Native header/query/method matching — HTTPRoute's matches
                block supports headers, queryParams, and method natively;
                Ingress can only approximate this via controller-specific
                annotations
             4) Protocol extensibility — Ingress is HTTP(S)-only by design;
                Gateway API adds purpose-built kinds: GRPCRoute (native
                gRPC routing) and TLSRoute (route by SNI without
                terminating TLS)
             5) Cross-namespace safety — ReferenceGrant is a native Gateway
                API object that must explicitly allow a Route in one
                namespace to reference a backend in another; Ingress has
                no equivalent guardrail
         - Below: the SAME jobpost.example.com route as a Gateway +
           HTTPRoute pair — compare its shape to the Ingress above

         [GATEWAY + HTTPROUTE — jobpost.example.com → anihpj, Role-Split]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # 1) Gateway — owned by the PLATFORM/infra team             │
         │  apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1                    │
         │  kind: Gateway                                               │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: anihpj-jobpost-gateway                              │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    gatewayClassName: cilium   # ⬅ same controller as Ingress │
         │    listeners:                                                │
         │    - name: http                                              │
         │      protocol: HTTP                                          │
         │      port: 80                                                │
         │      hostname: jobpost.example.com                           │
         │      allowedRoutes:                                          │
         │        namespaces:                                           │
         │          from: Same           # cross-ns needs ReferenceGrant│
         │  ---                                                         │
         │  # 2) HTTPRoute — owned by the anihpj APP team               │
         │  apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1                    │
         │  kind: HTTPRoute                                             │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: anihpj-jobpost-route                                │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    parentRefs:                                               │
         │    - name: anihpj-jobpost-gateway  # binds to Gateway above  │
         │    hostnames:                                                │
         │    - jobpost.example.com                                     │
         │    rules:                                                    │
         │    - matches:                                                │
         │      - path:                                                 │
         │          type: PathPrefix                                    │
         │          value: /                                            │
         │      backendRefs:                                            │
         │      - name: anihpj-v1                                       │
         │        port: 80                                              │
         │        weight: 90             # ⬅ native split, no annotation│
         │      - name: anihpj-v2                                       │
         │        port: 80                                              │
         │        weight: 10             # ⬅ same 90/10 split as Ch10.4 │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Two role-scoped objects instead of ONE do-everything      │
         │     Ingress — and weight: 90/10 is a REAL HTTPRoute field,   │
         │     the same split 10.4 hand-fed Envoy via CiliumEnvoyConfig │
         │     for. GRPCRoute/TLSRoute exist too; Ingress has neither.  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         📖 Key Term: Gateway API
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Gateway API is the Kubernetes-SIG-standard, role-oriented   │
         │  successor to Ingress — a FAMILY of CRDs (GatewayClass,      │
         │  Gateway, HTTPRoute, GRPCRoute, TLSRoute...) instead of      │
         │  one single Ingress object.                                  │
         │                                                              │
         │  • GatewayClass — WHICH controller implements it (Cilium's   │
         │    is literally named "cilium", same as ingressClassName)    │
         │  • Gateway — WHICH listeners/ports/protocols are exposed     │
         │  • HTTPRoute/GRPCRoute/TLSRoute — the actual routing rules,  │
         │    owned by the app team, not the platform team              │
         │                                                              │
         │  Cilium implements Gateway API with the SAME mechanism as    │
         │  Ingress and the L7 canary routing from 10.2/10.4: eBPF      │
         │  intercepts the connection and hands it to Cilium's          │
         │  per-node Envoy (Path B) — Gateway API is not a new          │
         │  "Path C", it is Path B with a richer, role-split front door.│
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Ingress = one door, one key, HTTP only. Gateway API =     │
         │     a lobby (Gateway) with labeled per-tenant mailslots      │
         │     (Routes) — and doors built for more than just HTTP mail. │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [INGRESS vs GATEWAY API — Feature Comparison]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ASPECT           INGRESS            GATEWAY API             │
         │  ──────────       ───────            ───────────             │
         │  Resource model   1 object, mixed    GatewayClass+Gateway+   │
         │                   roles              xRoute, split roles     │
         │                                                              │
         │  Who owns what    infra+app in ONE   infra owns Gateway,     │
         │                   manifest           app owns the xRoute     │
         │                                                              │
         │  Protocols        HTTP/HTTPS only    HTTP, gRPC, TLS(SNI),   │
         │                                      TCP/UDP (dev-only,1.20) │
         │                                                              │
         │  Traffic split    none native —      native: backendRefs     │
         │                   vendor annotation  weight: field           │
         │                                                              │
         │  Header/query     vendor annotation  native: HTTPRoute       │
         │  match            only               matches block           │
         │                                                              │
         │  Cross-ns safety  none built in      ReferenceGrant (native) │
         │                                                              │
         │  Cilium support   Supported, stable  Supported — Core        │
         │                   (ingressClassName) conformance passed      │
         │                                      (v1.4.1, Cilium 1.19);  │
         │                                      TLSRoute experimental   │
         │                                                              │
         │  Exam keyword     ingressClassName   GatewayClass / weight:  │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 TCPRoute/UDPRoute are only experimental in Cilium's       │
         │     newest dev line (1.20.0-dev, Gateway API v1.5.1) — NOT   │
         │     yet in the current stable release. Don't assume raw      │
         │     TCP-passthrough routing is GA on the exam.               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 10):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Assuming Cilium's mesh is "100% proxy-free," even for L7 canaries
            → TRUTH: Only L3/L4 (plain TCP/UDP) is truly proxy-free — eBPF rewrites the
              socket directly. L7 (HTTP routing, canary weighting) still routes through
              Envoy.
            → FIX: Say "no PER-POD sidecar," not "no proxy." Cilium runs ONE shared Envoy
              per NODE for L7 — that's the real innovation, not a total absence of proxies.

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Believing authentication: mode: "required" encrypts anihpj ↔ postgres traffic
            → TRUTH: Cilium's Mutual Authentication only PROVES identity via SPIFFE/SPIRE
              SVIDs — it does not itself encrypt the connection.
            → FIX: Pair it with WireGuard or IPsec transparent encryption (Ch16) if the bytes
              on the wire must actually be encrypted, not just authenticated.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Assuming a CiliumEnvoyConfig canary split works for ANY protocol
            → TRUTH: Weighted traffic shifting via CiliumEnvoyConfig is an L7 (HTTP)
              mechanism built on Envoy's weighted_clusters — it can't see or split plain
              TCP connections like a raw PostgreSQL session.
            → FIX: Use CiliumEnvoyConfig canaries for HTTP APIs like jobpost's REST
              endpoints; for non-HTTP backends, canary by shifting replica ratios instead.

         ❌ MISTAKE 4: Assuming Cilium's Ingress supports vendor canary/weight
            annotations like nginx's, or that Gateway API's TCPRoute is already GA
            → TRUTH: Cilium's Ingress has no native or vendor-annotation weighting
              field at all; TCPRoute/UDPRoute are experimental only in Cilium's
              newest dev line, not yet in the current stable release.
            → FIX: Use 10.4's CiliumEnvoyConfig or Gateway API's HTTPRoute weight
              field to split HTTP traffic; don't assume raw TCP-passthrough
              routing is GA when studying for the exam.

    ────────────────────────────────

        L7 HTTP-based traffic splitting like a canary release.

    Q6. Which Custom Resource does Cilium use to implement a 90/10 canary
        traffic split between anihpj-v1 and anihpj-v2?
        A) CiliumNetworkPolicy              B) CiliumEnvoyConfig
        C) CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy    D) CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy

    Q7. What identity framework issues each workload's X.509 SVID under
        Cilium's Mutual Authentication feature, and which production
        implementation is it currently validated against?

    Q8. A teammate adds authentication: mode: "required" to the
        CiliumNetworkPolicy governing anihpj → postgres traffic and tells you
        the connection is "now fully encrypted." Are they right? If not, what
        is actually happening, and what else must be enabled?

        over a Kubernetes Ingress for splitting traffic 90/10 between
        anihpj-v1 and anihpj-v2.
