    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 10):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ Cilium's service mesh is sidecar-FREE, not proxy-free: plain L3/L4 traffic
            (anihpj → postgres) is routed purely by eBPF at the socket layer, while L7
            HTTP features still use Envoy — just ONE instance shared per NODE.

         ✅ Socket-level load balancing rewrites a pod's destination at connect() time,
            inside the kernel, before a single packet is built — this is what eliminates
            the extra proxy hop and iptables/IPVS chain kube-proxy requires (see Ch11).

         ✅ Cilium's Mutual Authentication (mTLS) is a Beta feature: it authenticates
            workload identity via SPIFFE/SPIRE-issued SVIDs and is enabled per-policy
            with authentication: mode: "required" — but it does NOT encrypt traffic.

         ✅ Canary/traffic-shifting deployments (e.g., 90% anihpj-v1 / 10% anihpj-v2) run
            through the CiliumEnvoyConfig CRD, which hands Cilium's per-node Envoy raw
            xDS config with weighted_clusters — an L7-only mechanism, not raw TCP.

         ✅ Because L7 routing, mutual authentication, and canaries all still lean on
            Envoy and SPIRE, "sidecar-less" means "no per-pod proxy container" — not
            "zero moving parts." The CCA exam tests exactly this distinction.

         ✅ Ingress and Gateway API both route external traffic into the mesh through
            the SAME per-node Envoy (Path B) — Gateway API adds role-split resources
            and a native weight field that replaces what CiliumEnvoyConfig hand-rolls.

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         You just watched eBPF rewrite a pod's destination socket instead of routing
         through a proxy — and that very same socket-layer trick is how Cilium replaces
         kube-proxy entirely. Chapter 11 opens the eBPF load balancer up in full: why a
         shared iptables/IPVS conntrack table causes mysterious connection drops, how
         Maglev consistent hashing keeps sessions stable while backends scale up and
         down, and how to prove — for real, and for the exam — that kube-proxy is
         completely gone from your cluster.


Chapter 11: Cilium as kube-proxy Replacement — Faster Load Balancing
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ Why iptables/IPVS share ONE finite conntrack table per node —
            and how that causes mysterious, unrelated connection drops
         ✅ How Cilium's eBPF load balancer replaces kube-proxy at the
            socket, TC, and XDP hooks — no shared conntrack bottleneck
         ✅ Maglev consistent hashing: why adding/removing backends only
            reshuffles ~1% of connections instead of nearly all of them
         ✅ How Cilium's eBPF datapath handles ClusterIP, NodePort, and
            ExternalIPs differently — each has its own hook and path
         ✅ How to enable kubeProxyReplacement=true and verify kube-proxy
            is truly gone (not just scaled down)
         ✅ Session affinity internals: why in-cluster clients are pinned
            by a network-namespace cookie, not by source IP
         ✅ DSR vs SNAT vs Hybrid mode — the real latency/complexity
            tradeoff behind Cilium's load-balancing forwarding modes

    11.1 How kube-proxy Works (and Why It's Slow)
         - iptables mode: chain traversal, O(n) rules
         - IPVS mode: faster hash lookup, but still netfilter
         - Connection tracking limitations
         - NOTE: Ch2.5 already compared iptables/IPVS/eBPF raw lookup
           speed — this section covers a DIFFERENT angle: the shared,
           finite conntrack table both kube-proxy modes depend on
         [DIAGRAM: iptables chain for a single K8s Service]

         [CONNTRACK TABLE LIMITS — kube-proxy's Hidden Bottleneck]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ iptables AND IPVS both depend on ONE shared kernel table:    │
         │ nf_conntrack — used by EVERY connection on the node, not     │
         │ just Kubernetes Services (also raw pod traffic, node-to-     │
         │ internet, health checks... everything shares it).            │
         │                                                              │
         │ SYMPTOM                       ROOT CAUSE                     │
         │ ───────────────────────────  ─────────────────────────       │
         │ "nf_conntrack: table full,   net.netfilter.nf_conntrack_max  │
         │  dropping packet" in dmesg    reached — table is FIXED size  │
         │ Unrelated pods lose new       One noisy pod (port scan,      │
         │  connections randomly          burst of short conns) can     │
         │                                 starve the WHOLE node        │
         │ Restarting kube-proxy pod     The table lives in the kernel  │
         │  does NOT free any space       itself, not in kube-proxy     │
         │                                                              │
         │ 💡 Cilium's eBPF LB keeps its OWN per-service BPF CT maps,    │
         │    and socket-level LB (11.2) skips conntrack/DNAT entirely  │
         │    for many ClusterIP flows — no single table to exhaust.    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    11.2 Cilium's eBPF Load Balancer — How It Replaces kube-proxy
         - Direct eBPF socket-level load balancing
         - Maglev consistent hashing (even distribution, connection affinity)
         - How Cilium handles NodePort, ClusterIP, and ExternalIPs
         [DIAGRAM: eBPF load balancer data path vs iptables path]

         [MAGLEV HASHING — Scaling Backends Without Breaking Connections]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ NAIVE HASHING: hash(connection) % N   (N = backend count)    │
         │   3 backends → conn #47 lands on backend 2                   │
         │   Add a 4th backend (N: 3→4) → hash(47)%4 = backend 0!       │
         │   Nearly EVERY connection remaps to a new backend            │
         │                                                              │
         │ MAGLEV: one big lookup table (M slots, M prime, M >> N),     │
         │ built from each backend's own hash-derived permutation.      │
         │   slot:    0    1    2    3    4 ... 16380                   │
         │   backend: pod-A pod-C pod-B pod-A ... pod-A                 │
         │   connection hash → pick a slot → that backend               │
         │                                                              │
         │ Remove pod-C (scale 3→2): ONLY pod-C's slots move;           │
         │ pod-A's and pod-B's slots (and live conns) untouched.        │
         │ Cilium docs: at most ~1% of entries move per backend         │
         │ change — recommended M > 100 × N backends.                   │
         │                                                              │
         │ 💡 anihpj: 3 Django pods behind django-web Service.           │
         │    Scaling to 4 pods during a spike reassigns only           │
         │    ~25% of the table — not all live connections.             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [ClusterIP vs NodePort vs ExternalIPs — eBPF Paths]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ TYPE        ENTRY HOOK        BACKEND PICK   NAT DIR.        │
         │ ──────────  ────────────────  ─────────────  ───────         │
         │ ClusterIP   socket hooks:     Maglev among    none —         │
         │  (internal)  connect(),        ready           dest          │
         │              sendmsg(),        backends        set in        │
         │              recvmsg()                         socket        │
         │                                                              │
         │ NodePort    TC/XDP hook on    Maglev over     SNAT           │
         │  (external)  node's external   node's own      (default;     │
         │              interface         backend set     node =        │
         │                                                 return hop)  │
         │                                                              │
         │ ExternalIPs same path as      same as         same as        │
         │  (external)  NodePort, plus    NodePort        NodePort,     │
         │              CVE-2020-8554                     plus MITM     │
         │              MITM mitigation                   mitigation    │
         │                                                              │
         │ 💡 ClusterIP traffic from a POD never touches a packet-       │
         │    level NAT rule — the kernel socket connects directly      │
         │    to the chosen backend's real IP before data is sent.      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    11.3 Enabling kube-proxy Replacement
         - The flag: --set kubeProxyReplacement=true
         - Verification: kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep kube-proxy
         - (Spoiler: there should be NO kube-proxy pods anymore!)
         [COMMAND BLOCK: Enable, verify, and test kube-proxy replacement]

         [KUBE-PROXY REPLACEMENT — Before vs After]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  BEFORE: WITH KUBE-PROXY                     AFTER: CILIUM   │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌────────────────────┐                     ┌──────────────┐ │
         │  │  $ kubectl get pods │                     │ $ kubectl get │ │
         │  │  -n kube-system    │                     │ pods -n kube- │ │
         │  │                    │                     │ system        │ │
         │  │  kube-proxy-abcde  │  ← iptables rules   │               │ │
         │  │  kube-proxy-fghij  │     on every node!  │               │ │
         │  │  kube-proxy-klmno  │                     │ (no kube-proxy│ │
         │  └────────────────────┘                     │  pods!) ⚡     │ │
         │                                             └──────────────┘ │
         │  $ iptables -t nat -L    ← 5000+ rules!     $ cilium service │
         │  (takes 30 seconds)                          list (instant!) │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    11.4 Session Affinity and Direct Server Return (DSR)
         - Session affinity: pinning a client to the same backend
         - In-cluster affinity key: the client's network-namespace cookie
         - External affinity key: the client's source IP address
         - DSR mode vs SNAT mode vs Hybrid mode — latency/MTU tradeoffs

         [SESSION AFFINITY — Two Different Identifiers, Same Goal]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ CLIENT LOCATION    AFFINITY KEY         WHY                  │
         │ ─────────────────  ───────────────────  ──────────────────   │
         │ In-cluster pod      network-namespace    socket-LB runs at   │
         │  (socket-LB path)    cookie (per netns)   connect() — no IP  │
         │                                           packet exists yet  │
         │ External client      client source IP    traffic arrives as  │
         │  (NodePort/Ext-IP)                        real packets first │
         │                                                              │
         │ apiVersion: v1                                               │
         │ kind: Service                                                │
         │ metadata:                                                    │
         │   name: django-web      # anihpj's jobpost frontend Service  │
         │ spec:                                                        │
         │   sessionAffinity: ClientIP                                  │
         │   sessionAffinityConfig:                                     │
         │     clientIP:                                                │
         │       timeoutSeconds: 10800  # 3 hours, Django's default     │
         │                                                              │
         │ 💡 3 django-web pods each cache job listings in local memory; │
         │    ClientIP affinity keeps one browser session on one pod so │
         │    its cache stays warm instead of missing on every request. │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [DSR vs SNAT vs HYBRID — Return-Traffic Forwarding Modes]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ MODE     RETURN PATH            TRADEOFF                     │
         │ ───────  ─────────────────────  ───────────────────────      │
         │ SNAT      backend replies via   simplest, no MTU change,     │
         │  (default) the SAME node it      but +1 hop on the return    │
         │            arrived through                                   │
         │                                                              │
         │ DSR        backend replies       preserves client source     │
         │            DIRECTLY to client,   IP, but needs IP option/    │
         │            skipping ingress      Geneve header room (MTU     │
         │            node entirely         impact)                     │
         │                                                              │
         │ Hybrid     DSR for TCP,          best of both: low TCP       │
         │            SNAT for UDP         latency, no UDP MTU hit      │
         │                                                              │
         │ 💡 DSR encodes the original service IP/port into an IP        │
         │    option (or Geneve header) so the backend can reply        │
         │    as if it were the Service — client sees no change.        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    11.5 Performance Tuning and Capacity Planning for the eBPF Load Balancer
         - bpf-lb-map-max / bpf.lbMapMax: the ceiling on Service+backend
           entries in cilium_lb4/6_services_v2, and what a growing
           cluster looks like once it quietly outgrows the default
         - bpf-lb-maglev-table-size / maglev.tableSize: revisiting 11.2's
           M with Cilium's own ">100 x N backends" sizing rule
         - The real memory tradeoff: every one of these maps is KERNEL
           memory, created ONCE PER NODE — bigger isn't free
         - IPv6/dual-stack kube-proxy replacement is real, with real
           caveats worth verifying before you rely on them
         - Reading live Maglev/LB map utilization before it becomes
           an outage, not after

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ KNOB (--flag)               HELM VALUE          DEFAULT      │
         │ ──────────────────────────  ──────────────────  ─────────    │
         │ bpf-lb-map-max               bpf.lbMapMax          65536     │
         │ bpf-lb-maglev-table-size     maglev.tableSize      16381     │
         │                                                              │
         │ bpf-lb-map-max caps cilium_lb4/6_services_v2 — one entry     │
         │ per (backend × port/protocol) pair, summed across EVERY      │
         │ Service. maglev.tableSize is 11.2's M (must stay prime,      │
         │ and > 100 x N backends per Service).                         │
         │                                                              │
         │ FORMULA: Σ (backends × ports/protocols) over every Service.  │
         │ Budget 2-3× current usage as headroom, and re-check after    │
         │ every major rollout — a new Service, a scale-up, or an       │
         │ added port all consume slots.                                │
         │                                                              │
         │ ⚠️  A full bpf-lb-map-max does NOT crash Cilium — it just    │
         │    can't reconcile NEW Services/backends. Existing, already- │
         │    programmed traffic keeps flowing; only new rollouts stall.│
         │                                                              │
         │ 💡 anihpj today: django-web (3 backends × 2 ports) +          │
         │    postgres-0 (1 backend × 1 port) ≈ 7 entries — nowhere     │
         │    near 65536. Plan for what the cluster looks like after    │
         │    the next few Service rollouts, not today's footprint.     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ Every map above lives in KERNEL memory, created ONCE PER     │
         │ NODE at agent startup — it is NOT shared cluster-wide, and   │
         │ every node pays its own copy's memory cost independently.    │
         │                                                              │
         │ ⚠️  Bigger maps cost real RAM on EVERY node, forever,        │
         │    whether that space is ever used or not — "just size it    │
         │    generously" on a 200-node cluster is 200× the bill.       │
         │                                                              │
         │ Cilium can also auto-size this (and CT/NAT/neighbor) maps    │
         │ via bpfMapDynamicSizeRatio (--bpf-map-dynamic-size-ratio),   │
         │ a ratio of each node's OWN RAM — default 0.0025 (0.25%)      │
         │ — instead of a fixed entry count.                            │
         │                                                              │
         │ ⚠️  Growing bpf-lb-map-max/maglev.tableSize AFTER an agent's │
         │    first start needs a cilium-agent restart: the old map is  │
         │    torn down and the new, larger one is repopulated from     │
         │    scratch — a brief connectivity gap while it refills.      │
         │    Size it right the FIRST time; don't wait for a "map       │
         │    full" failure to react.                                   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ Cilium's eBPF Service LB genuinely supports dual-stack:      │
         │ cilium_lb4_services_v2 and cilium_lb6_services_v2 run        │
         │ side by side, each Maglev-balancing its own address          │
         │ family — IPv6 is NOT a bolt-on iptables fallback path.       │
         │                                                              │
         │ ⚠️  REAL CAVEATS (verify against your exact Cilium           │
         │    version before relying on these in production):           │
         │    • v4 and v6 each get their OWN map, both sized by the     │
         │      SAME bpf-lb-map-max / maglev.tableSize value —          │
         │      running dual-stack roughly DOUBLES the LB map's         │
         │      per-node memory cost versus single-stack alone.         │
         │    • DSR mode (11.4) encodes Service IP/port into a          │
         │      Cilium-specific IPv4 IP option OR an IPv6 Destination   │
         │      Option extension header — either can be dropped by a    │
         │      cloud provider's underlying network fabric, so test     │
         │      DSR explicitly on IPv6 before trusting it in prod.      │
         │                                                              │
         │ This is a fast-moving corner of Cilium — treat the two       │
         │ caveats above as "go verify against your version," not       │
         │ as a permanently-fixed final word.                           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ cilium-dbg bpf lb list          (Appendix A.6)               │
         │   raw entry COUNT ≈ current bpf-lb-map-max usage —           │
         │   pipe to `wc -l` and compare against the configured max     │
         │                                                              │
         │ cilium-dbg bpf lb maglev list                                │
         │   dumps the ACTUAL Maglev lookup table (11.2's M slots)      │
         │   for a live Service — confirms the table is populated       │
         │   and roughly even, not just "configured"                    │
         │                                                              │
         │ cilium_bpf_map_pressure  (Prometheus gauge, OFF by default)  │
         │   fill percentage per map, BY NAME — includes                │
         │   cilium_lb4_maglev / cilium_lb6_maglev explicitly, so       │
         │   you can alert BEFORE a map fills instead of after          │
         │                                                              │
         │ 💡 None of these replace capacity planning — they tell you    │
         │    when today's estimate was wrong, not what to set it to.   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 11):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Setting kubeProxyReplacement=true but leaving the existing
            kube-proxy DaemonSet (and ConfigMap) running
            → TRUTH: Cilium's docs warn the two mechanisms run fully independent
              of each other — you get TWO separate NAT paths fighting over the
              same Services.
            → FIX: Delete the kube-proxy DaemonSet + ConfigMap and flush leftover
              KUBE-* iptables chains BEFORE installing Cilium with
              kubeProxyReplacement=true.

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Assuming "any modern-ish kernel" is good enough
            → TRUTH: Full socket-LB support depends on specific backported kernel
              commits (e.g. present in Ubuntu 5.4.0-187+, RHEL 8.10+/9.4+); an
              older kernel can silently run a degraded or partial replacement
              instead of failing loudly.
            → FIX: After install, run cilium-dbg status and confirm
              "KubeProxyReplacement: True" — don't just trust the Helm flag
              was accepted.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Bootstrapping a kubeadm cluster with kube-proxy replacement
            and forgetting k8sServiceHost/k8sServicePort
            → TRUTH: kube-proxy normally provisions the in-cluster kubernetes
              Service implicitly; with it removed, Cilium has no way to find
              the API server unless told directly.
            → FIX: Always set k8sServiceHost and k8sServicePort explicitly in
              the Helm values on kubeadm-based installs.

         ❌ MISTAKE 4: Waiting until a "Service not reconciling" incident to
            raise bpf-lb-map-max/maglev.tableSize, then resizing on a live cluster
            → TRUTH: Resizing either map after an agent's first start requires
              restarting cilium-agent — the old map is torn down and the new
              one is repopulated from scratch, producing a real connectivity
              gap on that node, right in the middle of the incident.
            → FIX: Treat bpf-lb-map-max and maglev.tableSize as capacity-
              planning inputs, not forgettable defaults — size them with
              2-3x headroom BEFORE a major Service/Endpoint rollout, not during one.

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 11):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. What flag enables kube-proxy replacement?
    Q2. How does eBPF load balancing differ from iptables?
    Q3. What is Maglev hashing?

    Q4. Why does adding a 4th backend to a naive hash(conn) % N load
        balancer reshuffle nearly every existing connection, while Maglev
        reassigns only about 1% of the lookup table?

    Q5. A team enables kubeProxyReplacement=true but never deletes the
        original kube-proxy DaemonSet. What goes wrong, and why doesn't
        Cilium simply detect and override kube-proxy's rules automatically?

    Q6. On a kubeadm cluster with kube-proxy replaced, which TWO Helm values
        must be set explicitly, and why were they never needed before?
        A) k8sServiceHost / k8sServicePort
        B) apiServerHost / apiServerPort
        C) kubeProxyReplacement / kubeProxyMode
        D) clusterDNS / clusterDomain

    Q7. For a Service with sessionAffinity: ClientIP, what identifier does
        Cilium use to pin an IN-CLUSTER pod's connections to the same
        backend, and why can't it just reuse the client's source IP the
        way it does for external NodePort clients?

    Q8. Your team needs backend replies to preserve the original client
        source IP address for compliance logging, but wants to avoid the
        MTU impact of encoding service metadata on every UDP packet. Which
        Cilium forwarding mode fits, and why?
        A) SNAT for everything    B) DSR for everything
        C) Hybrid (DSR for TCP, SNAT for UDP)    D) IPVS passthrough

    Q9. Your cluster's Service count triples after a major microservices
        rollout, and some new Deployments intermittently fail to receive
        traffic while all pre-existing Services keep working fine.
        cilium-dbg status still reports healthy and nothing has crashed.
        What's the most likely root cause, and which value should you
        check and raise first?

    Q10. A team enables dual-stack (IPv4+IPv6) kube-proxy replacement and
         notices Cilium's LB map memory footprint per node roughly doubles
         versus their old single-stack cluster. Why?
         A) Cilium duplicates conntrack entries for every TCP retransmit
         B) cilium_lb4_services_v2 and cilium_lb6_services_v2 are separate
            maps, each sized by the same bpf-lb-map-max value
         C) IPv6 requires kube-proxy to remain installed alongside Cilium
         D) Maglev tables are only used for IPv6, doubling CPU, not memory

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 11):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ iptables/IPVS both depend on ONE shared, finite nf_conntrack table
            per node — exhausting it drops unrelated connections, a distinct
            failure mode from the raw O(n) lookup cost covered in Ch2.5.

         ✅ Cilium's eBPF load balancer intercepts traffic at three points
            (socket hooks, TC, XDP) and keeps its own per-service BPF CT maps —
            no dependency on the kernel's shared conntrack table.

         ✅ Maglev consistent hashing builds a large lookup table (M much
            bigger than N backends) so scaling up or down reassigns roughly
            1% of connections, not nearly all of them like naive modulo hashing.

         ✅ ClusterIP, NodePort, and ExternalIPs each take a different path
            through Cilium's datapath — ClusterIP rewrites at connect() with
            no NAT, while NodePort/ExternalIPs SNAT (or DSR) at the node's
            ingress hook.

         ✅ Session affinity is keyed differently by traffic origin (netns
            cookie in-cluster, source IP externally), and DSR/SNAT/Hybrid
            forwarding modes trade off latency against MTU and complexity.

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         You've replaced kube-proxy entirely inside the cluster — but what about
         traffic that needs to reach your Services from OUTSIDE the cluster, on
         bare metal or on-premise hardware with no cloud load balancer to
         provision one for you? Chapter 12 introduces BGP: how Cilium announces
         Service IPs directly to your physical network's routers, turning any
         router into a Kubernetes-aware load balancer.


Chapter 12: BGP and External Load Balancing
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ Why bare-metal / on-prem Cilium clusters need BGP at all — there is
            no cloud LoadBalancer API to lean on out here
         ✅ How Cilium acts as a real BGP speaker, peering with a physical
            router over a plain TCP session on port 179
         ✅ CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy's real fields: nodeSelector, virtualRouters,
            localASN, neighbors, exportPodCIDR, serviceSelector
         ✅ Why Cilium's BGP configuration CRDs changed across versions —
            and which one this guide's v1.16.0 reference version uses
         ✅ How LB IPAM's CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool assigns real external IPs
            to LoadBalancer Services with zero cloud provider involved
         ✅ Why LB IPAM and BGP are two DIFFERENT jobs (assigning an IP vs.
            announcing a route to it) that both have to be configured
         ✅ How to diagnose a BGP session stuck in Idle/Connect, or an
            exhausted LB IPAM pool leaving Services stuck at <pending>

    12.1 What is BGP? (Border Gateway Protocol — The Internet's Routing Protocol)
         - Simple analogy: BGP is a GPS for network packets — it doesn't move
           packets itself, it just tells routers which way to send them
         - How BGP lets Cilium announce Service IPs AND Pod CIDRs to your
           physical network — turning Cilium's agent into a real router
         - When you need BGP: bare-metal clusters, on-premise data centers,
           anywhere there's no cloud provider API to hand out LoadBalancer IPs
         - Contrast: on AWS/GCP, `type: LoadBalancer` calls a cloud API that
           provisions a real load balancer for you — bare metal has no such API

         [DIAGRAM: BGP — Cilium Announces Service & Pod CIDR Routes to the Router]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  BGP — Cilium Announces Routes to the Physical Network       │
         │                                                              │
         │  EXTERNAL CLIENT (office LAN / the internet)                 │
         │         │                                                    │
         │         ▼                                                    │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐        │
         │  │ PHYSICAL ToR ROUTER — 10.0.0.1  (AS 64512)       │        │
         │  │ Runs standard router BGP software (FRR, etc.)    │        │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘        │
         │                    │                                         │
         │                    │  eBGP session, TCP port 179             │
         │                    ▼                                         │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐        │
         │  │ K8S WORKER NODE — Cilium Agent (BGP speaker)     │        │
         │  │ Local AS 64513                                   │        │
         │  │                                                  │        │
         │  │ ANNOUNCES via BGP UPDATE messages:               │        │
         │  │   • anihpj Service (LoadBalancer) IP:            │        │
         │  │       192.168.1.240/32                           │        │
         │  │   • this node's Pod CIDR:  10.244.1.0/24         │        │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘        │
         │  (Every BGP-enabled node repeats this — the router ends      │
         │   up with a route to every node that can reach that Svc)     │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Result: the router's routing table now has a REAL         │
         │     entry for 192.168.1.240 — no cloud LoadBalancer, no      │
         │     MetalLB, no manually-typed static route required.        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         📖 Key Term: BGP Peering Vocabulary — ASN, Speaker, eBGP
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  BGP announces ROUTES (how to reach an IP); it does not      │
         │  create or assign the IP itself — that's LB IPAM's job       │
         │  (Section 12.3).                                             │
         │                                                              │
         │  • AS (Autonomous System): a network under one org's         │
         │    control, identified by an ASN (e.g. AS 64512). Private    │
         │    ASNs live in the 64512-65534 range — safe for labs.       │
         │  • BGP speaker: any device running BGP — here, both the      │
         │    physical router AND the Cilium agent on each node.        │
         │  • Peering: two BGP speakers forming a TCP session (port     │
         │    179) and exchanging routes. Cilium's side = eBGP          │
         │    ("external" BGP — the two sides use different ASNs).      │
         │  • Advertisement: a route a BGP speaker tells its peer       │
         │    about, e.g. "I can reach 192.168.1.240/32."               │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Analogy: BGP is two neighboring post offices telling      │
         │     each other "send anything addressed to this street       │
         │     to me — I'll deliver it from here."                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.2 Cilium BGP Configuration
         - CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy: the CRD this guide's v1.16.0 reference
           version uses to configure BGP (apiVersion cilium.io/v2alpha1)
         - Advertising ClusterIP, LoadBalancer, and Pod CIDRs via
           exportPodCIDR and serviceSelector
         - Peer configuration: virtualRouters[].localASN (Cilium's own AS)
           and neighbors[].peerAddress / peerASN (the physical router)
         - serviceSelector's "match every Service" idiom: a matchExpressions
           rule keyed on a label that will never exist, using NotIn

         [YAML BLOCK: CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy — full annotated example]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: "cilium.io/v2alpha1"                            │
         │  kind: CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy                                │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: anihpj-rack0-bgp                                    │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    nodeSelector:        # which NODES run this policy        │
         │      matchLabels:                                            │
         │        rack: rack0                                           │
         │    virtualRouters:      # one Cilium-side BGP router         │
         │    - localASN: 64513    # Cilium's own AS number             │
         │      exportPodCIDR: true  # announce this node's Pod CIDR    │
         │      neighbors:         # the physical router to peer with   │
         │      - peerAddress: "10.0.0.1/32"  # ToR router's IP         │
         │        peerASN: 64512   # ToR router's AS number             │
         │      serviceSelector:   # WHICH Services get their IP        │
         │        matchExpressions: # advertised via BGP                │
         │        - key: somekey    # official "match every Service"    │
         │          operator: NotIn #   idiom: a key that never exists  │
         │          values: ["never-used-value"]                        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [VERSION NOTE: BGP Control Plane v1 vs. the newer v2 CRD split]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ⚠️  VERSION NOTE: Cilium's BGP config API has changed.      │
         │                                                              │
         │  This guide's reference version (v1.16.0) still fully        │
         │  supports the CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy shown above (a single   │
         │  CRD). Starting in v1.16, Cilium ALSO ships a newer,         │
         │  modular model that splits the same job across 4 CRDs:       │
         │                                                              │
         │  • CiliumBGPClusterConfig — which nodes, which local/peer    │
         │                            ASNs (the topology)               │
         │  • CiliumBGPPeerConfig — reusable peer settings: timers,     │
         │                            auth, address families            │
         │  • CiliumBGPAdvertisement — WHAT to advertise: PodCIDR,      │
         │                            Service IPs, by label selector    │
         │  • CiliumBGPNodeConfigOverride — per-node overrides          │
         │                                                              │
         │  CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy was marked deprecated in Cilium      │
         │  1.18, and support for it was REMOVED in 1.19 — budget       │
         │  time to migrate if you're running a newer Cilium.           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [YAML BLOCK: CiliumBGPClusterConfig — the newer 4-CRD model]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2alpha1  # -> cilium.io/v2 on 1.17+  │
         │  kind: CiliumBGPClusterConfig                                │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: anihpj-bgp-cluster                                  │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    nodeSelector:                                             │
         │      matchLabels:                                            │
         │        rack: rack0                                           │
         │    bgpInstances:                                             │
         │    - name: instance-64513                                    │
         │      localASN: 64513                                         │
         │      peers:                                                  │
         │      - name: tor-router                                      │
         │        peerASN: 64512                                        │
         │        peerAddress: "10.0.0.1"                               │
         │        peerConfigRef:                                        │
         │          name: anihpj-peer-config  # -> CiliumBGPPeerConfig  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.3 LoadBalancer IPAM (LB IPAM)
         - How Cilium assigns external IPs without a cloud provider: the
           CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool CRD (apiVersion cilium.io/v2alpha1)
         - Pool-based IP allocation: define blocks of spare IPs (a CIDR, or
           a start/stop range), Cilium hands them out as Services need one
         - Example: assign 192.168.1.240-250 as LoadBalancer IPs for anihpj
         - LB IPAM only ASSIGNS the IP — it does not, by itself, make that IP
           reachable. Something else (BGP, or L2 Announcements) must do that

         [DIAGRAM: LB IPAM — Pool-Based IP Allocation Flow]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  LB IPAM — Pool-Based IP Allocation for anihpj               │
         │                                                              │
         │  1. You define a POOL of spare IPs (no cloud provider!):     │
         │     192.168.1.240-192.168.1.250  (11 addresses)              │
         │                                                              │
         │  2. You create a Service, type: LoadBalancer, for anihpj:    │
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐        │
         │  │ Service "anihpj-svc"                             │        │
         │  │ type: LoadBalancer                               │        │
         │  │ status.loadBalancer.ingress[0].ip:  <pending...> │        │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘        │
         │                    │                                         │
         │                    ▼  LB IPAM operator sees the              │
         │                        unfulfilled request and claims        │
         │                        the next free IP from the pool        │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐        │
         │  │ Service "anihpj-svc"                             │        │
         │  │ status.loadBalancer.ingress[0].ip:  192.168.1.240│        │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘        │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚠️  The IP is now ASSIGNED — but nothing on the network     │
         │     yet knows how to ROUTE to it. That's BGP's job (12.2),   │
         │     or Cilium's L2 Announcements feature on flat LANs.       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [YAML BLOCK: CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool — assigning 192.168.1.240-250]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: "cilium.io/v2alpha1"  # -> cilium.io/v2 on 1.17+│
         │  kind: CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool                              │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: anihpj-lb-pool                                      │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    blocks:              # one or more ranges in this pool    │
         │    - start: "192.168.1.240"                                  │
         │      stop:  "192.168.1.250"   # 11 usable addresses          │
         │    serviceSelector:     # OPTIONAL: restrict this pool to    │
         │      matchLabels:       #   only Services with this label    │
         │        app: anihpj                                           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.4 BGP + LB IPAM Together — The Complete Bare-Metal LoadBalancer Picture
         - LB IPAM and BGP solve two DIFFERENT problems that people often
           lump together as "the LoadBalancer thing"
         - The full anihpj flow: LB IPAM assigns 192.168.1.240 to the Service
           -> BGP announces a route to 192.168.1.240 -> the ToR router sends
           matching traffic to the right node -> Cilium's eBPF datapath
           load-balances it to a healthy anihpj pod
         - Cilium also supports L2 Announcements (ARP/NDP replies) as a
           BGP-free alternative for flat, single-subnet LANs — out of scope
           for this chapter, but good to know the name exists

         [COMPARISON: LB IPAM vs. BGP Control Plane — Who Does What]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  LB IPAM  vs  BGP Control Plane — Who Does What              │
         │                                                              │
         │  "What IP does my Service get?"                              │
         │      → LB IPAM answers this. It ASSIGNS the IP from a        │
         │        configured pool (CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool).           │
         │                                                              │
         │  "How does the network find that IP?"                        │
         │      → BGP answers this. It ANNOUNCES the IP as a route      │
         │        to the upstream router (CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy).      │
         │                                                              │
         │  Neither one works alone: an IP nobody can route to is       │
         │  useless, and BGP has nothing new to announce until LB       │
         │  IPAM hands a Service a real IP to advertise.                │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Analogy: LB IPAM assigns the mailbox number. BGP          │
         │     updates the postal service's map so mail actually        │
         │     gets delivered to that mailbox.                          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 12):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Assuming BGP peering just works the moment you apply a
            CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy manifest
            → TRUTH: peering is a live TCP session — it fails to establish if the
              local/peer ASN is wrong on either side, or if a firewall/ACL between
              the node and the router blocks TCP port 179.
            → FIX: run cilium bgp peers (or cilium-dbg bgp peers) to see the
              session state. "Idle"/"Connect" means no TCP connection ever formed
              (check port 179); "Active" with flapping means an ASN or auth mismatch.

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Creating more LoadBalancer Services than a
            CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool has free addresses left
            → TRUTH: when a pool is exhausted, new LoadBalancer Services sit at
              EXTERNAL-IP: <pending> forever — Cilium cannot invent IP addresses
              it was never given.
            → FIX: size pools for growth up front, and watch kubectl get svc -A
              for stuck <pending> entries; add another cidr/start-stop block to
              the pool (or a second CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool) if you run out.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Treating a BGP-announced Service IP (e.g. 192.168.1.240)
            as if it were a Pod IP
            → TRUTH: 192.168.1.240 is a virtual Service IP the router forwards to
              whichever node announced it; the anihpj pod behind it has a totally
              different address from the Pod CIDR (e.g. 10.244.1.7), and Cilium's
              eBPF datapath still does the DNAT/load-balancing after the packet
              arrives at the node.
            → FIX: use cilium service list to see the mapping from a Service's
              external IP to its actual backend pod IPs — the route and the
              endpoint are two different layers.

    Q1. When would you use BGP with Cilium?

        tells Cilium to also announce the node's own Pod CIDR over BGP
        (not just Service IPs)?
        A) advertiseCIDR: true       B) exportPodCIDR: true
        C) podCIDRExport: enabled    D) announcePods: true

    Q5. Your cluster is running Cilium 1.19, and a CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy
        manifest you apply has no effect at all. What's the most likely
        explanation?
        A) CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy requires a Helm flag that wasn't set
        B) Support for the legacy CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy ("v1" BGP API) was
           removed in Cilium 1.19 — use CiliumBGPClusterConfig +
           CiliumBGPPeerConfig + CiliumBGPAdvertisement instead
        C) BGP requires a cloud provider and cannot run on Cilium 1.19
        D) The manifest's metadata.name must be exactly "default"

    Q6. A LoadBalancer Service has shown EXTERNAL-IP: <pending> for an
        hour, even though a CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool exists in the cluster.
        Name TWO plausible root causes, and how you'd confirm each one.

    Q7. Which CRD is responsible for actually ASSIGNING an external IP
        address to a Service of type LoadBalancer on a bare-metal Cilium
        cluster with no cloud provider?
        A) CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy         B) CiliumNetworkPolicy
        C) CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool       D) CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy

    Q8. True or False, and explain your answer: "Once LB IPAM assigns
        192.168.1.240 to the anihpj Service, external clients can
        immediately reach it — no further configuration is required."

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 12):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ BGP lets Cilium act as a real BGP speaker, peering with a physical
            router (or ToR switch) to announce routes for Service and Pod CIDR
            IPs — the bare-metal alternative to a cloud LoadBalancer API.

         ✅ This guide's reference version (Cilium 1.16.0) configures BGP via
            CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy (apiVersion cilium.io/v2alpha1); Cilium marked
            that CRD deprecated in 1.18 and removed its support in 1.19, in favor
            of a 4-CRD split (CiliumBGPClusterConfig / CiliumBGPPeerConfig /
            CiliumBGPAdvertisement / CiliumBGPNodeConfigOverride).

         ✅ LB IPAM (CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool) is a separate concern from BGP:
            it only ASSIGNS an IP from a configured pool to a Service — it does
            not, by itself, make that IP reachable from the outside network.

         ✅ BGP and LB IPAM are complementary, not redundant: LB IPAM answers
            "what IP does my Service get," BGP (or L2 Announcements) answers
            "how does the network find that IP."

         ✅ Real BGP problems are almost always an ASN mismatch or a blocked
            TCP port 179; real LB IPAM problems are almost always pool
            exhaustion or a serviceSelector label that doesn't match the Service.

    ─────────────────────────
         You've now made anihpj reachable from outside the cluster at the
         network layer — a BGP-announced route to an LB IPAM-assigned IP. But
         how do you actually SEE that traffic arrive, confirm which pod
         answered it, and prove whether Cilium allowed or denied it? Chapter 13
         introduces Hubble — Cilium's built-in observability platform, "the
         microscope for your Kubernetes network" — turning invisible eBPF-level
         packet flows into identity-aware, human-readable observability data.

Chapter 13: Hubble — The Microscope for Your Kubernetes Network
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ Why Hubble is "tcpdump for Kubernetes, but identity-aware" — the
            exact information gap it closes (pod names/namespaces/labels,
            not just raw IPs)
         ✅ The Hubble data path: Cilium Agent (eBPF Ring Buffer) → Hubble
            Relay → Hubble CLI / Hubble UI / Prometheus
         ✅ The essential hubble observe filters — --from-pod, --to-namespace,
            --verdict, --port — and how to read FORWARDED vs DROPPED
         ✅ How to launch Hubble UI locally with cilium hubble ui, and what
            its Service Map and Flow Table each actually show you
         ✅ Why Hubble Relay and Hubble UI are OPTIONAL components that must
            be explicitly enabled — they don't appear just because Cilium
            itself is installed
         ✅ Why Hubble's flow data is ephemeral (an in-memory ring buffer)
            unless you export it somewhere persistent
         ✅ How the SAME flow data shows up three ways — hubble observe text,
            Hubble UI's live graph, and (in Chapter 14) Prometheus metrics

    13.1 What is Hubble? — "tcpdump for Kubernetes, But 100x Better"
         - Hubble = Cilium's built-in observability platform
         - It shows you: who talked to whom, how much data, was it allowed/denied
         - Unlike tcpdump: Hubble understands K8s identities (pod names, namespaces, labels!)
         [COMPARISON: tcpdump output vs Hubble output for the same traffic]

         [tcpdump vs HUBBLE — Same Traffic, Different Understanding]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  tcpdump (raw packets):                                      │
         │  10.0.0.55.54321 > 10.0.0.99.5432: Flags [S], ...          │
         │  "What is 10.0.0.55? What is 10.0.0.99? Who knows..."      │
         │                                                              │
         │  Hubble (identity-aware flows):                              │
         │  anihpj/anihpj-55 (app=anihpj) → anihpj/postgres-0 (app=db) │
         │  Port 5432, TCP, FORWARDED, 2.5 MB                          │
         │  "Ah! The Django pod connected to PostgreSQL. Makes sense!" │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.2 Hubble Architecture — How Flow Data Gets to You
         - Cilium Agent: exports flow events via eBPF RingBuf
         - Hubble Relay: aggregates flows from ALL agents
         - Hubble UI: beautiful web dashboard
         - Hubble CLI: terminal-based flow viewer
         [DIAGRAM: Hubble data flow — pod → eBPF → Agent → Relay → UI/CLI]

         [HUBBLE ARCHITECTURE — Data Flow]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │  WORKER NODE 1                                        │    │
         │  │                                                       │    │
         │  │  anihpj Pod ──▶ Cilium Agent (eBPF) ──▶ gRPC ───────┼──┐ │
         │  │  (10.0.0.55)   exports flows to       to Hubble      │  │ │
         │  │                Ring Buffer             Relay          │  │ │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │ │
         │                                                            │ │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │ │
         │  │  WORKER NODE 2                                        │  │ │
         │  │                                                       │  │ │
         │  │  postgres Pod ─▶ Cilium Agent (eBPF) ──▶ gRPC ───────┼──┘ │
         │  │  (10.0.0.99)   exports flows to                       │    │
         │  │                Ring Buffer                            │    │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                                                              │
         │                    ┌──────────────────┐                      │
         │                    │   HUBBLE RELAY    │ ← Aggregates flows  │
         │                    │   (Deployment)    │    from all agents   │
         │                    └────────┬─────────┘                      │
         │                             │                                │
         │              ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐                │
         │              │              │              │                │
         │              ▼              ▼              ▼                │
         │  ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐        │
         │  │  HUBBLE CLI  │ │  HUBBLE UI   │ │  PROMETHEUS  │        │
         │  │  (terminal)  │ │  (browser)   │ │  (metrics)   │        │
         │  └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.3 Hubble CLI — Your Detective Toolkit
         - hubble observe (the main command — see ALL flows)
         - Filtering: --from-pod, --to-pod, --namespace, --verdict
         - hubble observe --verdict DROPPED (find blocked traffic!)
         - hubble observe --from-pod anihpj/anihpj-55 (see what one pod does)
         [COMMAND REFERENCE: Hubble CLI commands with examples]

         [HUBBLE CLI — Essential Commands]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  COMMAND                             WHAT IT SHOWS           │
         │  ──────────────────────────────────  ─────────────────────── │
         │  hubble observe                      ALL flows (firehose!)   │
         │  hubble observe --from-pod anihpj/   Only traffic FROM our   │
         │    anihpj-abcde                      Django pod              │
         │  hubble observe --to-namespace       Only traffic going TO   │
         │    anihpj                            our app namespace       │
         │  hubble observe --verdict DROPPED    All blocked traffic     │
         │  hubble observe --verdict FORWARDED  All allowed traffic     │
         │  hubble observe --port 5432          Only DB traffic         │
         │  hubble observe -o json              JSON output (for        │
         │                                      scripting!)            │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.4 Hubble UI — The Beautiful Web Dashboard
         - Service map: visual graph of all your services
         - Flow browser: searchable, filterable traffic log
         - Example: Open Hubble UI and watch anihpj traffic live
         - Launch it with `cilium hubble ui` — auto port-forwards to your browser
         - Namespace selector (top-left) scopes the whole dashboard to one namespace
         [DESCRIPTION: Hubble UI dashboard walkthrough with service map]

         [HUBBLE UI — LAUNCHING & ACCESSING THE DASHBOARD]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  $ cilium hubble ui                                          │
         │                                                              │
         │  • Opens a LOCAL browser tab — no manifest, no Ingress       │
         │  • Under the hood: port-forwards your machine to the         │
         │    hubble-ui Service already running in-cluster              │
         │    (ClusterIP — NOT exposed externally by default)           │
         │  • Default local address: http://localhost:12000             │
         │  • The command BLOCKS your terminal while the port-          │
         │    forward stays open — Ctrl+C stops it, re-run the          │
         │    command any time to reopen the dashboard                  │
         │  • First screen: a namespace-selector dropdown, top          │
         │    left — pick anihpj to see only that namespace's           │
         │    live flows                                                │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 cilium hubble ui is a convenience wrapper around a        │
         │     kubectl port-forward to the hubble-ui Service —          │
         │     it isn't a separate always-on public endpoint.           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         📖 Key Term: Hubble UI — Service Map & Flow Table
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  The Service Map is a LIVE topology graph — one node         │
         │  per Service/pod identity, one edge per flow Hubble          │
         │  has actually observed between them — auto-laid-out          │
         │  and updated in real time as traffic happens.                │
         │                                                              │
         │  The Flow Table sits BELOW the Service Map: a                │
         │  scrollable, filterable list of individual flow              │
         │  events (one row per flow) for whichever namespace           │
         │  is currently selected — the exact same underlying           │
         │  data `hubble observe` prints as text lines.                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  • Nodes/edges are color-coded by VERDICT — green for        │
         │    FORWARDED (allowed), red for DROPPED (denied)             │
         │  • Click any node or edge to filter the Flow Table           │
         │    down to just that Service's flows                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Service Map = the graph. Flow Table = the                 │
         │     searchable log underneath it. Same flow data —           │
         │     two different views onto it.                             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [HUBBLE UI SERVICE MAP — anihpj Namespace, Conceptual View]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Hubble UI Service Map — localhost:12000, ns: anihpj         │
         │                                                              │
         │    ┌─────────┐                                               │
         │    │ anihpj  │                                               │
         │    │ (Django)│                                               │
         │    └────┬────┘                                               │
         │         │                                                    │
         │         ├──✅──▶ [postgres]         5432/TCP FORWARDED        │
         │         │         (green edge — ALLOWED)                     │
         │         │                                                    │
         │         ├──✅──▶ [coredns]          53/UDP FORWARDED          │
         │         │         (green edge — ALLOWED)                     │
         │         │                                                    │
         │         └──❌──▶ [169.254.169.254]  443/TCP DROPPED           │
         │                   (red edge — Ch8's CCNP denies this)        │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Every edge here IS a real flow Hubble observed —          │
         │     same verdict data `hubble observe` prints, just          │
         │     drawn as a graph instead of text lines.                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.5 Hubble Across Multiple Clusters — Cross-Cluster Flow Visibility
         - Cluster Mesh (Ch17) lets pods in DIFFERENT clusters talk
           directly — it does NOT automatically give you ONE Hubble view
           spanning those clusters
         - Each cluster's Hubble Relay aggregates flows from ITS OWN
           cluster's Cilium Agents ONLY — there is no default "mesh-wide
           Hubble Relay" that merges multiple clusters' flows for you
         - To see the FULL picture of a cross-cluster flow — say, anihpj
           in us-east calling postgres in eu-west — you check hubble
           observe independently in BOTH clusters and correlate the two
           halves yourself; Hubble never stitches them into one trace
         - A flow observed in us-east shows ONLY us-east's own vantage
           point: the destination is whichever postgres backend the
           Global Service (17.5) actually resolved to, not some
           already-merged cross-cluster replay
         - Practical tip: --namespace, --from-pod, --to-pod, --verdict,
           etc. all work identically in either cluster's own hubble
           observe — no new flag is required, just the operational
           discipline of checking Hubble in BOTH clusters, not only the
           one you're already looking at

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  CLUSTER: us-east                                            │
         │    Agents ──▶ HUBBLE RELAY (us-east) ──▶ hubble observe      │
         │                                                              │
         │  CLUSTER: eu-west                                            │
         │    Agents ──▶ HUBBLE RELAY (eu-west) ──▶ hubble observe      │
         │                                                              │
         │  ❌ No arrow between the two Relays — Cluster Mesh (Ch17)     │
         │     wires the pod-to-pod DATA PATH, not Hubble's             │
         │     aggregation. There is no default "mesh-wide Hubble       │
         │     Relay" that merges both clusters' flows into one feed.   │
         │                                                              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         Recall 17.3-17.5: postgres is a Global Service, and
         service.cilium.io/affinity decides whether a us-east anihpj
         pod's traffic actually lands on the LOCAL us-east postgres
         backend or fails over to the eu-west replica. Whichever backend
         really gets picked is exactly what shows up as the DESTINATION
         in us-east's own hubble observe output — Hubble doesn't re-
         derive or guess at this, it just reports the real packet the
         us-east Agent's own eBPF datapath actually forwarded.

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 1 — Check the EGRESS side, in us-east:                 │
         │    $ hubble observe --from-pod anihpj/anihpj-55 \            │
         │        --to-namespace anihpj -o json                         │
         │    → shows: SOURCE anihpj-55, DEST = whichever postgres      │
         │      backend the Global Service resolved to (17.5),          │
         │      verdict FORWARDED, timestamp T                          │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2 — Check the INGRESS side, in eu-west:                │
         │    $ hubble observe --to-pod anihpj/postgres-0 -o json       │
         │    → shows: SOURCE = the remote caller's identity, DEST      │
         │      postgres-0, verdict FORWARDED, timestamp ≈ T            │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Hubble does NOT stitch these into one cross-cluster       │
         │     trace for you — YOU correlate the two halves, by         │
         │     matching five-tuple (or pod names) + timestamp.          │
         │                                                              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         💡 A note on hubble observe --cluster: Chapter 17 (17.2, Step 5)
            already used this flag as a post-connect sanity check.
            Treat it as filtering a CLUSTER'S OWN Hubble Relay down to
            flows tied to a particular cluster's identity metadata (as
            replicated by clustermesh-apiserver) — it is a LOCAL filter,
            not a way to reach INTO a remote cluster's Relay from here.
            Exactly which endpoint it matches against (source vs.
            destination), and which Cilium/Hubble versions support it,
            can vary — confirm against hubble observe --help on your
            installed version rather than assuming identical behavior
            everywhere. When in doubt, fall back to the two-cluster,
            manual-correlation method shown above — it works regardless
            of version.

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 13):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Assuming a plain restart (or re-running cilium hubble
            enable with no flags) turns on Hubble UI if it wasn't enabled at
            install time
            → TRUTH: Hubble Relay and Hubble UI are separate, OPTIONAL
              components (hubble.relay.enabled, hubble.ui.enabled) — if their
              Deployments/Services were never created, no restart brings them
              into existence.
            → FIX: Run cilium hubble enable --ui (cilium-cli), or helm
              upgrade cilium cilium/cilium --reuse-values --set
              hubble.relay.enabled=true --set hubble.ui.enabled=true, then
              confirm with cilium status.

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Treating Hubble flow history like a database you can
            query for "what happened last Tuesday"
            → TRUTH: Hubble stores flow events in a fixed-capacity, in-memory
              ring buffer per Cilium agent (and Relay) — older events are
              continuously overwritten as new ones arrive. There is no
              built-in persistence layer.
            → FIX: for retention beyond a few minutes/hours, export flow data
              out of Hubble — Prometheus metrics and Grafana dashboards (the
              subject of Chapter 14) are the standard way to keep history.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Expecting Hubble UI to be reachable at some public URL
            the moment it's enabled
            → TRUTH: the hubble-ui Service is a plain ClusterIP by default —
              nothing routes external traffic to it automatically, correctly
              enabled or not.
            → FIX: use cilium hubble ui for local, on-demand access via port-
              forward, or deliberately create an Ingress/Gateway if
              teammates need to reach it without running that command
              themselves.

         ❌ MISTAKE 4: Assuming a single hubble observe command, run in
            ONE cluster, shows the ENTIRE round trip for a flow that
            crosses a Cluster Mesh boundary
            → TRUTH: each cluster's Hubble Relay only aggregates flows
              from its OWN cluster's Cilium Agents (Ch17's Cluster Mesh
              wires pod-to-pod connectivity, not a shared Hubble
              aggregation layer) — a cross-cluster flow is really TWO
              separate flow events, one recorded by each side's Agent.
            → FIX: run hubble observe in BOTH clusters — the egress side
              where traffic leaves, the ingress side where it arrives —
              and correlate them yourself by five-tuple and timestamp;
              don't expect one terminal, in one cluster, to show the
              whole story.
