    Q1. What does Hubble show that tcpdump doesn't?
    Q2. How does Hubble get flow data from the kernel?
    Q3. What command shows only DROPPED traffic?
    Q4. What is Hubble Relay and why is it needed?

    Q5. Which command launches Hubble UI locally and automatically sets up
        the port-forward for you?
        A) hubble ui start             B) kubectl apply -f hubble-ui.yaml
        C) cilium hubble ui            D) helm install hubble-ui

    Q6. A teammate runs cilium hubble enable (no --ui flag) and says "Hubble
        UI should be reachable now." Why might they still get nothing at
        localhost:12000, and what's the actual fix?

    Q7. True or False: Hubble flow data is durably stored on disk by default,
        so hubble observe can answer "what happened last Tuesday" months
        later. Justify your answer in one sentence.

    Q8. In the Hubble UI Service Map for the anihpj namespace, you see a red,
        DROPPED edge from the anihpj pod to 169.254.169.254. Which earlier
        chapter's CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy is almost certainly
        responsible, and what Hubble CLI command would confirm that exact
        same verdict from the terminal?

    Q9. A teammate runs hubble observe in the us-east cluster, sees a
        FORWARDED flow from anihpj to postgres, and declares "the whole
        cross-cluster round trip through Cluster Mesh is healthy." What's
        wrong with that conclusion, and what would you actually do to
        confirm the eu-west side too?

    Q10. True or False: In a Cluster Mesh, a single shared Hubble Relay
         automatically aggregates flow data from every connected cluster
         the moment cilium clustermesh connect succeeds. Justify your
         answer in one sentence.

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 13):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ Hubble is Cilium's built-in observability platform — it turns raw
            eBPF flow events into identity-aware flows (who talked to whom,
            verdict, bytes), unlike tcpdump's raw IP:port view.

         ✅ The data path is Cilium Agent (eBPF Ring Buffer) → Hubble Relay
            (cluster-wide aggregation) → Hubble CLI / Hubble UI / Prometheus —
            three different consumers reading the SAME flow stream.

         ✅ Hubble CLI's hubble observe is the fast, filterable terminal view;
            Hubble UI adds a live Service Map (topology graph, color-coded by
            verdict) plus a Flow Table, launched locally with cilium hubble ui.

         ✅ Hubble Relay and Hubble UI are optional components that must be
            explicitly enabled (Helm values, or cilium hubble enable --ui) —
            they don't appear automatically just because Cilium is installed.

         ✅ Flow data lives in an in-memory ring buffer and is NOT persisted
            by default — anything worth keeping past the buffer's capacity
            must be exported, which is exactly where Chapter 14 picks up.

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         You've now seen individual flows two ways — as text with Hubble CLI,
         and as a live graph with Hubble UI's Service Map and Flow Table. But
         neither answers questions like "what's our HTTP error rate trending
         toward?" or "did that canary spike latency?" Chapter 14 turns
         Hubble's same flow stream into Prometheus metrics — HTTP, TCP, DNS,
         and drop counters your Grafana dashboards can graph, alert on, and
         page someone about.


Chapter 14: Hubble Metrics — Prometheus + Grafana Integration
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ The real hubble_* Prometheus metric names Hubble exports — flows,
            HTTP, TCP flags, DNS, drops, port-distribution, ICMP
         ✅ Why hubble.metrics.enabled is a Helm install/upgrade-time flag, not
            a runtime toggle — metrics aren't retroactive
         ✅ How to point a Prometheus Operator ServiceMonitor at the hubble-
            metrics Service (port 9965) so scraping actually works
         ✅ Why Hubble metrics (port 9965) and Cilium Agent metrics (port 9962)
            are two separate systems, not one
         ✅ How to design real Grafana panels straight from real Hubble metric
            names for anihpj
         ✅ Why the httpV2 metric flavor replaced the deprecated http flavor for
            L7 HTTP observability
         ✅ How a ServiceMonitor label mismatch silently stops Prometheus from
            ever scraping Hubble

    14.1 Hubble Metrics — Turning Flows into Numbers
         - What metrics Hubble exports: HTTP request rate, HTTP status
           codes, TCP flag counts, DNS query outcomes, ICMP, and drops
         - How to enable Hubble metrics: an install/upgrade-time Helm flag
         - The real metric flags: flow, httpV2 (http is deprecated), tcp,
           dns, drop, icmp, port-distribution

         [HUBBLE METRIC TYPES — Real Prometheus Names & What They Measure]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ HUBBLE METRIC TYPES (hubble.metrics.enabled flags)           │
         │                                                              │
         │ ▸ flag: flow                                                 │
         │   hubble_flows_processed_total                               │
         │   Every flow Hubble observed, labeled by verdict (FORWARDED  │
         │   / DROPPED).                                                │
         │                                                              │
         │ ▸ flag: httpV2                                               │
         │   hubble_http_requests_total,                                │
         │   hubble_http_request_duration_seconds                       │
         │   HTTP requests seen at L7, labeled by method + status code, │
         │   plus a latency histogram.                                  │
         │                                                              │
         │ ▸ flag: tcp                                                  │
         │   hubble_tcp_flags_total                                     │
         │   TCP flag counts — SYN, ACK, FIN, RST — per flow direction. │
         │                                                              │
         │ ▸ flag: dns                                                  │
         │   hubble_dns_queries_total, hubble_dns_responses_total       │
         │   DNS queries/responses, labeled by rcode (NOERROR,          │
         │   NXDOMAIN, SERVFAIL...).                                    │
         │                                                              │
         │ ▸ flag: drop                                                 │
         │   hubble_drop_total                                          │
         │   Dropped packets, labeled by drop reason (e.g. Policy       │
         │   denied).                                                   │
         │                                                              │
         │ ▸ flag: icmp                                                 │
         │   hubble_icmp_total                                          │
         │   ICMP traffic, labeled by type/code (echo-request, echo-    │
         │   reply, etc).                                               │
         │                                                              │
         │ ▸ flag: port-distribution                                    │
         │   hubble_port_distribution_total                             │
         │   Traffic volume broken down by destination port.            │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 http is DEPRECATED — httpV2 is the recommended flag for   │
         │    new installs; the two can't both be enabled at once.      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [EXAMPLE — RAW /metrics SCRAPE OUTPUT FROM hubble-metrics:9965]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ # curl -s hubble-metrics.kube-system:9965/metrics \          │
         │     | grep hubble_                                           │
         │                                                              │
         │ hubble_http_requests_total{method="GET",status="200"} 48213  │
         │ hubble_http_requests_total{method="GET",status="500"} 17     │
         │ hubble_dns_queries_total{rcode="NOERROR"} 91045              │
         │ hubble_drop_total{reason="Policy denied"} 302                │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 These are real Prometheus exposition-format lines — the   │
         │    same format Grafana's PromQL queries in 14.3 read from.   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [ENABLING HUBBLE METRICS — Helm Install/Upgrade Flags]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ $ helm upgrade cilium cilium/cilium -n kube-system \         │
         │     --reuse-values \                                         │
         │     --set hubble.enabled=true \                              │
         │     --set hubble.metrics.enableOpenMetrics=true \            │
         │     --set hubble.metrics.enabled="{dns,drop,tcp,flow,\       │
         │        port-distribution,icmp,httpV2}" \                     │
         │     --set hubble.metrics.serviceMonitor.enabled=true         │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 hubble.metrics.enabled takes a LIST of flags — each one   │
         │    turns on one or more hubble_* metrics from the table      │
         │    above.                                                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 This also creates a headless Service named hubble-metrics │
         │    exposing port 9965 on every cilium-agent node.            │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚠️ These are INSTALL/UPGRADE-time flags. Changing them means│
         │     a helm upgrade, which restarts every cilium-agent pod.   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         📖 Key Term: Hubble Metrics vs Cilium Agent Metrics
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Hubble metrics and Cilium Agent metrics are TWO SEPARATE    │
         │  Prometheus endpoints, even though both live inside the same │
         │  cilium-agent process:                                       │
         │                                                              │
         │  Hubble metrics     → port 9965, prefix hubble_*             │
         │                        (flow/HTTP/TCP/DNS/ICMP/drop)         │
         │  Cilium Agent       → port 9962, prefix cilium_*             │
         │                        (endpoint count, BPF map pressure,    │
         │                        policy import errors, agent health)   │
         │  Cilium Operator    → port 9963, prefix cilium_operator_*    │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Analogy: cilium_* metrics are the car's dashboard (engine │
         │    temp, oil pressure). hubble_* metrics are the dash-cam    │
         │    footage of the traffic around you — different job,        │
         │    different port.                                           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    14.2 Setting Up Prometheus to Scrape Hubble Metrics
         - ServiceMonitor configuration for the hubble-metrics Service
         - Why the ServiceMonitor's label selector must match Prometheus's
           own serviceMonitorSelector, or nothing gets scraped
         - Key metrics to watch for anihpj: HTTP error rate, Postgres
           traffic volume, DNS query rate, drop rate by reason

         [PROMETHEUS SERVICEMONITOR — Targeting hubble-metrics:9965]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ apiVersion: monitoring.coreos.com/v1                         │
         │ kind: ServiceMonitor                                         │
         │ metadata:                                                    │
         │   name: hubble-metrics                                       │
         │   namespace: kube-system                                     │
         │   labels:                                                    │
         │     k8s-app: hubble                                          │
         │     release: prometheus  # must match the Prometheus CR's    │
         │                          # own serviceMonitorSelector!       │
         │ spec:                                                        │
         │   selector:                                                  │
         │     matchLabels:                                             │
         │       k8s-app: hubble     # matches hubble-metrics Svc labels│
         │   namespaceSelector:                                         │
         │     matchNames:                                              │
         │       - kube-system       # hubble-metrics Svc lives here    │
         │   endpoints:                                                 │
         │     - port: hubble-metrics   # named Service port -> 9965    │
         │       interval: 30s                                          │
         │       path: /metrics                                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 hubble.metrics.serviceMonitor.enabled=true (Ch 14.1) makes│
         │    the Cilium Helm chart generate almost exactly this object │
         │    for you.                                                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [VERIFYING PROMETHEUS ACTUALLY SCRAPED IT]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ $ kubectl get servicemonitor hubble-metrics -n kube-system   │
         │ $ kubectl get svc hubble-metrics -n kube-system              │
         │   NAME             TYPE        PORT(S)                       │
         │   hubble-metrics   ClusterIP   9965/TCP                      │
         │                                                              │
         │ # Port-forward and read the raw exposition format directly:  │
         │ $ kubectl -n kube-system port-forward svc/hubble-metrics \   │
         │     9965:9965                                                │
         │ $ curl -s localhost:9965/metrics | grep hubble_http_requests │
         │                                                              │
         │  In the Prometheus UI (Status > Targets), confirm the hubble-│
         │  metrics target shows State: UP — not just "the object       │
         │  exists."                                                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Example PromQL for anihpj's HTTP error rate:              │
         │   sum(rate(hubble_http_requests_total{                       │
         │     destination_workload="anihpj",status=~"5.."}[5m]))       │
         │   /                                                          │
         │   sum(rate(hubble_http_requests_total{                       │
         │     destination_workload="anihpj"}[5m]))                     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    14.3 Grafana Dashboard for anihpj
         - Cilium ships a pre-built "Hubble Dashboard" (DNS, HTTP,
           networking, and policy views) via its example monitoring stack
         - Custom panels for anihpj: HTTP error rate, Postgres traffic
           volume, DNS query rate, drop rate by reason

         [GRAFANA DASHBOARD — anihpj Panel Layout]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ +--------------------+  +--------------------+               │
         │ | HTTP Error Rate    |  | DNS Query Rate     |               │
         │ | (anihpj, httpV2)   |  | (rcode breakdown)  |               │
         │ | 5xx / total, 5m    |  | NOERROR vs NXDOMAIN|               │
         │ +--------------------+  +--------------------+               │
         │ +--------------------+  +--------------------+               │
         │ | Postgres Traffic   |  | Drop Rate by Reason|               │
         │ | Volume (port 5432) |  | (policy vs invalid)|               │
         │ | flow bytes, 5m     |  | hubble_drop_total  |               │
         │ +--------------------+  +--------------------+               │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Row 1 answers "is anihpj healthy right now?" Row 2 answers│
         │    "is the DB or the network the bottleneck?" Same dashboard,│
         │    same time range.                                          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [PANEL -> PROMQL QUERY — Real Metric Names Used Above]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ PANEL                  PROMQL (simplified)                   │
         │ ----------------  ---------------------------------          │
         │ HTTP error rate        rate(hubble_http_requests_total       │
         │                          {status=~"5.."}[5m])                │
         │ DNS query rate         sum(rate(hubble_dns_queries_total     │
         │                          [5m])) by (rcode)                   │
         │ Postgres traffic       sum(rate(hubble_flows_processed_      │
         │                          total{destination_port="5432"}[5m]))│
         │ Drop rate by reason    sum(rate(hubble_drop_total[5m]))      │
         │                          by (reason)                         │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Every panel above maps DIRECTLY to a metric from the 14.1 │
         │    table — a Grafana dashboard is just PromQL against        │
         │    hubble_* series, rendered as graphs instead of raw        │
         │    numbers.                                                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 14):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Assuming Hubble metrics exist just because Hubble
            itself (hubble.enabled=true) is turned on
            → TRUTH: hubble.metrics.enabled is a SEPARATE flag with its own
              list of metric flags. Hubble can run with zero metrics exported.
            → FIX: Explicitly set hubble.metrics.enabled="{dns,drop,tcp,
              flow,icmp,httpV2}" (or the subset you need) at install/upgrade.

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Creating a ServiceMonitor whose labels don't match
            the Prometheus custom resource's own serviceMonitorSelector
            → TRUTH: the Prometheus Operator only watches ServiceMonitors
              matching ITS serviceMonitorSelector — a mismatch means silent
              non-scraping, with no error anywhere.
            → FIX: kubectl get prometheus -o yaml and confirm your
              ServiceMonitor's metadata.labels satisfy that selector.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Confusing hubble_* metrics with cilium_* agent metrics
            when debugging "why is my dashboard empty?"
            → TRUTH: they are on different ports (9965 vs 9962) with
              different Helm flags (hubble.metrics.enabled vs
              prometheus.enabled) — enabling one does NOT enable the other.
            → FIX: check which port your ServiceMonitor/scrape config
              actually targets before assuming metrics are "broken."

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 14):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. What metrics does Hubble export?
    Q2. How does Prometheus scrape Hubble metrics?
    Q3. What metric would you use to detect a DDoS attack on anihpj?

    Q4. Which Helm flag must be set — and at install/upgrade time, not
        at runtime — before ANY hubble_* metric is ever exported?
        A) hubble.enabled=true only
        B) hubble.metrics.enabled="{...}"
        C) hubble.relay.enabled=true
        D) prometheus.enabled=true

    Q5. Your Grafana dashboard for anihpj is empty. `kubectl get svc
        hubble-metrics -n kube-system` shows the Service exists and has
        endpoints. What should you check next?

    Q6. Which port does Hubble expose its OWN Prometheus metrics on by
        default — and how is that different from the Cilium Agent's
        own metrics port?
        A) Both use port 9965         B) Hubble: 9965, Agent: 9962
        C) Hubble: 9962, Agent: 9965  D) Both use port 9090

    Q7. Why was the deprecated http metric flag replaced by httpV2, and
        what happens if you try to enable both at once?

    Q8. Write the PromQL query (using a real hubble_* metric name) that
        computes anihpj's HTTP 5xx error rate as a percentage of total
        HTTP requests over a 5-minute window.

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 14):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ Hubble exports its own family of hubble_* Prometheus metrics
            (flow, httpV2, tcp, dns, drop, icmp, port-distribution) — a
            completely separate system from cilium_* agent metrics.

         ✅ hubble.metrics.enabled is a Helm install/upgrade-time flag that
            takes a LIST of metric flags; nothing is exported until named.

         ✅ hubble.metrics.serviceMonitor.enabled=true generates a
            Prometheus Operator ServiceMonitor targeting the hubble-metrics
            Service on port 9965 — its labels must match Prometheus's own
            serviceMonitorSelector or nothing gets scraped.

         ✅ httpV2 is the current, recommended HTTP metric flavor; the
            older http flavor is deprecated and can't run alongside it.

         ✅ A Grafana dashboard for anihpj is just PromQL against real
            hubble_* series — HTTP errors, DB traffic, DNS rate, and drop
            reason are each one query away from a live panel.

    ─────────────────────────
         Dashboards tell you something is wrong — Chapter 15 teaches you what
         to do next. You'll walk through four real anihpj debugging scenarios
         with Hubble CLI and UI: chasing down 500 errors, catching whoever is
         flooding PostgreSQL, proving a network policy is too strict, and
         comparing a canary deployment's health against its stable version.


Chapter 15: Troubleshooting with Hubble — Real-World Debugging
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ How to trace an HTTP 500 error back to the exact pod and endpoint
           with `hubble observe --http-status`
         ✅ How to find the top talker to a shared service (like Postgres) by
           aggregating `hubble observe -o json` flow counts
         ✅ How to catch a legitimate request being blocked with `hubble
           observe --verdict DROPPED` and read its drop reason
         ✅ How to fix a CiliumNetworkPolicy selector bug that blocks traffic
           it should actually allow
         ✅ How to compare HTTP error rate and latency between two versions
           of a canary rollout using Hubble
         ✅ Why Hubble flows are ephemeral (in-memory, per-node ring buffer)
           and what that means once the moment has passed
         ✅ Why CiliumNetworkPolicy has no native "rate limit" field, and
           what to reach for instead

    15.1 Scenario 1: "Why is my pod getting 500 errors?"
         - Use Hubble to see HTTP response codes per destination
         - Correlate with application logs

         📖 Key Term: Hubble Verdict
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Every flow Hubble reports carries a VERDICT — what          │
         │  Cilium's datapath actually did with that packet.            │
         │                                                              │
         │  FORWARDED — allowed through (matched an allow rule,         │
         │             or no policy applies to this pod at all)         │
         │  DROPPED   — blocked before delivery (policy deny, or        │
         │             a datapath-level problem — see 15.3 below)       │
         │  AUDIT     — would be DROPPED, but the policy is in          │
         │             audit mode (PolicyEnforcementMode: audit) —      │
         │             logged only, nothing is actually enforced        │
         │  ERROR     — Cilium itself hit a problem processing          │
         │             the packet — rare, not a policy decision         │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 --verdict filters on this exact field. `hubble            │
         │     observe --verdict DROPPED` is the single most            │
         │     useful filter for Scenario 3 (15.3) below.               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [DEBUG WALKTHROUGH: Tracing jobpost 500s to One Pod]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  DEBUG WALKTHROUGH: Tracing jobpost 500s to One Pod          │
         │                                                              │
         │  Symptom: users report intermittent 500s on the              │
         │  jobpost API. The error-rate dashboard says ~8% of           │
         │  requests fail — but not WHICH pod or endpoint.              │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 1 — Isolate the failing requests with Hubble:          │
         │  $ hubble observe --namespace anihpj \                       │
         │      --protocol http --http-status 500 --last 20             │
         │                                                              │
         │  Jul 10 09:14:22.118: anihpj/ingress-6b7:41230 ->            │
         │    anihpj/anihpj-77:8000 http-request FORWARDED              │
         │    (HTTP/1.1 GET .../api/jobpost/42/apply/)                  │
         │  Jul 10 09:14:22.402: anihpj/ingress-6b7:41230 <-            │
         │    anihpj/anihpj-77:8000 http-response FORWARDED             │
         │    (HTTP/1.1 500 284ms (GET .../jobpost/42/apply/))          │
         │  ... 17 more lines, ALL destined for anihpj-77 ...           │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ Every 500 in the last 20 requests hits the SAME           │
         │     pod: anihpj-77. anihpj-55 and anihpj-66 (the other       │
         │     two replicas) show zero 500s in the same window.         │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2 — Correlate with that ONE pod's app logs:            │
         │  $ kubectl logs anihpj-77 -n anihpj --tail=50                │
         │                                                              │
         │  ImportError: cannot import name 'salary_range' from         │
         │  'jobpost.serializers' — this replica is still on            │
         │  image v2.3.0; the rollout to v2.3.1 never finished.         │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Hubble told you WHICH pod and WHICH endpoint;             │
         │     kubectl logs explained WHY — a stuck rolling             │
         │     update left one stale replica serving old code           │
         │     against a serializer field only v2.3.1 defines.          │
         │     Fix: kubectl rollout status / rollout restart,           │
         │     or kubectl delete pod anihpj-77 to force a retry.        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    15.2 Scenario 2: "Who is flooding the database?"
         - Identify top talkers to PostgreSQL from Hubble flow data
         - Rate-limit with CiliumNetworkPolicy

         [DEBUG WALKTHROUGH: Database Flood Investigation]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  DEBUG WALKTHROUGH: Who's Flooding postgres-0?               │
         │                                                              │
         │  Symptom: Postgres CPU is pegged, and the anihpj app         │
         │  is logging "FATAL: sorry, too many clients already".        │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 1 — Aggregate recent flows INTO postgres-0 by          │
         │  source pod (hubble observe has no built-in "top             │
         │  talkers" flag — pipe JSON flows through jq/sort):           │
         │  $ hubble observe --to-pod anihpj/postgres-0 \               │
         │      --protocol tcp --last 2000 -o json \                    │
         │      | jq -r '.flow.source.pod_name' \                       │
         │      | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -5                   │
         │                                                              │
         │     1842 anihpj-worker-6f9c8d7b4-z9plm                       │
         │      203 anihpj-77                                           │
         │      198 anihpj-66                                           │
         │      197 anihpj-55                                           │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ One pod — anihpj-worker-6f9c8d7b4-z9plm (a Celery         │
         │     worker) — accounts for 1,842 of the last 2,000           │
         │     flows toward Postgres: ~9x any single web replica.       │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2 — Confirm live with --follow:                        │
         │  $ hubble observe --from-pod \                               │
         │      anihpj/anihpj-worker-6f9c8d7b4-z9plm \                  │
         │      --to-pod anihpj/postgres-0 --protocol tcp -f            │
         │                                                              │
         │  Jul 10 09:41:01.001: anihpj/anihpj-worker...:51001          │
         │    -> anihpj/postgres-0:5432 to-endpoint FORWARDED           │
         │    (TCP Flags: SYN)                                          │
         │  Jul 10 09:41:01.014: anihpj/anihpj-worker...:51002          │
         │    -> anihpj/postgres-0:5432 to-endpoint FORWARDED           │
         │    (TCP Flags: SYN)   <- a NEW connection 13ms later         │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ A brand-new TCP connection every ~13ms means the          │
         │     worker is opening one connection PER TASK instead        │
         │     of reusing a pool — a classic connection leak.           │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 3 — Contain it, then fix it for real:                  │
         │  ⚠️  Cilium's OSS CiliumNetworkPolicy has NO native          │
         │     rate-limit field — it can only ALLOW or DENY by          │
         │     identity, never throttle a connection RATE.              │
         │  💡 Containment: an identity-scoped CNP allowlist             │
         │     (below) stops anything unauthorized from reaching        │
         │     postgres-0 at all. The durable fix is at the app         │
         │     layer: give the Celery worker a connection pool          │
         │     (Django CONN_MAX_AGE, or PgBouncer in front of           │
         │     postgres-0) so it stops opening one socket per task.     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CNP FIX — Identity-Scoped Allowlist for postgres-0]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind: CiliumNetworkPolicy                                   │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: postgres-allow-known-clients-only                   │
         │    namespace: anihpj                                         │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    endpointSelector:                                         │
         │      matchLabels:                                            │
         │        app: postgres                                         │
         │    ingress:                                                  │
         │    - fromEndpoints:      # ONLY these identities may         │
         │      - matchLabels:       #  open a socket to Postgres       │
         │          app: anihpj      #  at all — anyone else is         │
         │      toPorts:             #  an implicit default-deny        │
         │      - ports:                                                │
         │        - port: "5432"                                        │
         │          protocol: TCP                                       │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 app: anihpj matches BOTH anihpj-web (55/66/77)            │
         │     and anihpj-worker — they share that label. This          │
         │     narrows WHO can connect; it does NOT slow down           │
         │     how FAST an already-allowed pod can connect.             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    15.3 Scenario 3: "My network policy is blocking legitimate traffic!"
         - Use hubble observe --verdict DROPPED to see what's blocked
         - Fix the policy selector, verify with Hubble

         [DEBUG WALKTHROUGH: Policy Troubleshooting]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  DEBUG WALKTHROUGH: The Allowlist Blocked a Teammate         │
         │                                                              │
         │  Symptom: right after applying the CNP above, the            │
         │  anihpj-worker pods start timing out against Postgres        │
         │  — traffic that SHOULD be allowed is now blocked.            │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 1 — Catch it with --verdict DROPPED:                   │
         │  $ hubble observe --to-pod anihpj/postgres-0 \               │
         │      --verdict DROPPED --last 20                             │
         │                                                              │
         │  Jul 10 10:02:15.334: anihpj/anihpj-worker...:38122          │
         │    <> anihpj/postgres-0:5432 Policy denied DROPPED           │
         │    (TCP Flags: SYN)                                          │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2 — Get the exact reason + the pod's own labels        │
         │  with -o json (drop_reason_desc + source.labels):            │
         │  $ hubble observe --to-pod anihpj/postgres-0 \               │
         │      --verdict DROPPED --last 1 -o json | jq '{              │
         │        time, src: .flow.source.pod_name,                     │
         │        labels: .flow.source.labels,                          │
         │        reason: .flow.drop_reason_desc }'                     │
         │                                                              │
         │  {                                                           │
         │    "time": "2026-07-10T10:02:15.334Z",                       │
         │    "src": "anihpj-worker-6f9c8d7b4-z9plm",                   │
         │    "labels": ["k8s:app=anihpj",                              │
         │               "k8s:component=worker"],                       │
         │    "reason": "POLICY_DENIED"                                 │
         │  }                                                           │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ reason: POLICY_DENIED confirms this IS a selector         │
         │     problem, not a datapath fluke — and src.labels           │
         │     shows the pod DOES carry app=anihpj. So why deny?        │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 3 — Re-read the policy: fromEndpoints matched          │
         │  matchLabels: {app: anihpj} — that part is fine on           │
         │  its own. The REAL bug is upstream: a second, more           │
         │  specific CNP (written for 15.2's containment) also          │
         │  requires component: web in the SAME matchLabels             │
         │  block — and matchLabels is an AND, so a pod with            │
         │  component: worker can never match it.                       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [THE BUG — an AND-only matchLabels selector]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # THE BUG — matchLabels is AND, not OR:                     │
         │  ingress:                                                    │
         │  - fromEndpoints:                                            │
         │    - matchLabels:                                            │
         │        app: anihpj                                           │
         │        component: web    # excludes component: worker!       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [THE FIX — matchExpressions with an In list]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # THE FIX — matchExpressions with an "In" list:             │
         │  ingress:                                                    │
         │  - fromEndpoints:                                            │
         │    - matchLabels:                                            │
         │        app: anihpj                                           │
         │      matchExpressions:                                       │
         │      - key: component                                        │
         │        operator: In                                          │
         │        values:                                               │
         │        - web              # anihpj-55/66/77                  │
         │        - worker            # anihpj-worker-...               │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 matchExpressions lives INSIDE the same                    │
         │     fromEndpoints entry as matchLabels — the two             │
         │     combine with AND, but "In" lets ONE expression           │
         │     itself match several values with OR-like effect.         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  STEP 4 — Verify with Hubble after re-applying:              │
         │  $ hubble observe --from-pod \                               │
         │      anihpj/anihpj-worker-6f9c8d7b4-z9plm \                  │
         │      --to-pod anihpj/postgres-0 --verdict FORWARDED -f       │
         │                                                              │
         │  Jul 10 10:09:47.552: anihpj/anihpj-worker...:41880          │
         │    -> anihpj/postgres-0:5432 to-endpoint FORWARDED           │
         │    (TCP Flags: SYN)                                          │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ FORWARDED, not DROPPED — legitimate traffic flows         │
         │     again, and the containment from 15.2 still holds:        │
         │     anything without app=anihpj is still denied.             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    15.4 Scenario 4: "Is our new canary deployment healthy?"
         - Compare error rates between v1 and v2 pods
         - Hubble HTTP flow data per deployment version

         [DEBUG WALKTHROUGH: Canary Health Verification]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  DEBUG WALKTHROUGH: Is the anihpj-v2 Canary Healthy?         │
         │                                                              │
         │  Context: Ch10's CiliumEnvoyConfig is live, shifting         │
         │  10% of jobpost API traffic to anihpj-v2 (canary),           │
         │  90% still on anihpj-v1 (stable) — both real Services.       │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 1 — Pull HTTP status codes per version:                │
         │  $ hubble observe --to-pod anihpj/anihpj-v2 \                │
         │      --protocol http -o json --last 500 \                    │
         │      | jq -r '.flow.l7.http.code' | sort | uniq -c           │
         │                                                              │
         │      471 200                                                 │
         │        4 404                                                 │
         │       25 500                                                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  $ hubble observe --to-pod anihpj/anihpj-v1 \                │
         │      --protocol http -o json --last 500 \                    │
         │      | jq -r '.flow.l7.http.code' | sort | uniq -c           │
         │                                                              │
         │      493 200                                                 │
         │        5 404                                                 │
         │        2 500                                                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ anihpj-v2: 25/500 = 5.0% error rate.                      │
         │  ✅ anihpj-v1: 2/500  = 0.4% error rate.                      │
         │     The canary is erroring ~12x more than stable.            │
         │                                                              │
         │  STEP 2 — Sanity-check latency from the compact view:        │
         │  $ hubble observe --to-pod anihpj/anihpj-v2 \                │
         │      --protocol http --http-status 200 --last 5              │
         │                                                              │
         │  Jul 10 11:03:02.117: anihpj/ingress-6b7:44210 <-            │
         │    anihpj/anihpj-v2-.. http-response FORWARDED               │
         │    (HTTP/1.1 200 812ms (GET .../api/jobpost/))               │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ v2's own 200s are also ~4x slower than v1's typical       │
         │     ~200ms for the same endpoint (checked the same way).     │
         │                                                              │
         │  DECISION: error rate up 12x AND latency up ~4x on           │
         │  identical traffic — roll anihpj-v2's weight back to 0       │
         │  in the CiliumEnvoyConfig (Ch10) before raising it.          │
         │  Hubble gave a real go/no-go number, not a guess.            │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    15.5 Your Hubble Debugging Toolkit — Quick Reference
         - Every flag used across Scenarios 1-4, in one place
         - The CCA exam tests recall of these flags directly

         [HUBBLE OBSERVE — QUICK-REFERENCE FLAG TABLE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  FLAG                      USE IT TO...                      │
         │  ────────────────────  ───────────────────────────────       │
         │  --namespace anihpj        Scope to our namespace            │
         │  --to-namespace / --from-  One-directional namespace         │
         │    namespace                scoping (traffic only IN         │
         │                           or only OUT of anihpj)             │
         │  --pod ns/name             Scope to ONE exact pod —          │
         │  --to-pod / --from-pod     ALWAYS ns/pod-name form,          │
         │                           e.g. anihpj/postgres-0             │
         │  --verdict V               FORWARDED, DROPPED, AUDIT,        │
         │                           or ERROR (15.3, KEY TERM)          │
         │  --protocol P               tcp, udp, http, dns, etc.        │
         │  --http-status / --http-   Find 500s / POSTs (15.1)          │
         │    method                                                    │
         │  --last N                  Show the N most recent            │
         │                           buffered flows                     │
         │  --follow  (-f)             Live-tail, like tail -f          │
         │                           (15.2's connection-leak catch)     │
         │  -o json                    Machine-readable — pipe to       │
         │                           jq to aggregate (15.2, 15.4)       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 15):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Assuming `hubble observe --since 2h` will
            always find matching flows from 2 hours ago
            → TRUTH: Hubble keeps flows in a per-node, IN-MEMORY
              ring buffer (default capacity around 4,095 flows) —
              high traffic can overwrite that entire window in
              seconds, and an agent restart empties it completely.
            → FIX: export anything you need to keep — Hubble
              Metrics to Prometheus (Ch14), the Hubble exporter to
              a file/SIEM, or capture a live --follow session
              yourself. Don't assume you can "just query it later."

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Filtering with `hubble observe --to-pod
            postgres-0` (bare pod name) and getting zero flows back
            → TRUTH: pod-scoped filters (--pod, --to-pod,
              --from-pod) expect the namespace/pod-name form —
              "postgres-0" alone will not match "anihpj/postgres-0",
              and mixing up --to- vs --from- silently flips which
              side of the conversation you're actually watching.
            → FIX: always qualify with the namespace
              (anihpj/postgres-0), and double-check direction —
              --to-pod = traffic heading TO that pod, --from-pod =
              traffic leaving it.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Treating every DROPPED verdict as "my
            CiliumNetworkPolicy must be blocking this"
            → TRUTH: DROPPED just means the packet was not
              forwarded — the actual reason (visible via -o json's
              drop_reason_desc, e.g. POLICY_DENIED) can also be a
              datapath-level cause that has nothing to do with
              your policy YAML at all.
            → FIX: read the reason code before touching a single
              selector — only chase a label/selector fix when the
              reason really is POLICY_DENIED.

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 15):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. How do you find which pod is sending the most traffic?
    Q2. What Hubble command shows HTTP status codes?
    Q3. How do you debug a network policy that's too restrictive?

    Q4. Your cluster is under heavy load and you run `hubble
        observe --namespace anihpj --verdict DROPPED --last 500`
        an hour after an incident, but see almost nothing from
        the incident window. What is the MOST likely explanation?
        A) DROPPED flows are never shown after the fact
        B) Hubble stores flows in a small in-memory, per-node ring
           buffer — under heavy traffic, an hour-old flow has
           almost certainly already been overwritten
        C) --verdict DROPPED only works when combined with --follow
        D) The anihpj namespace must be re-specified with
           --to-namespace for historical flows to appear

    Q5. A teammate runs `hubble observe --to-pod postgres-0` and
        gets zero results, even though anihpj is clearly talking
        to Postgres right now. What is the mistake?
        A) --to-pod requires the full namespace/pod-name form,
           e.g. anihpj/postgres-0 — a bare pod name won't match
        B) --to-pod only works with --follow
        C) hubble observe cannot filter on a destination pod
        D) StatefulSet pods like postgres-0 aren't visible to Hubble

    Q6. In `hubble observe --verdict DROPPED` JSON output, a flow's
        drop_reason_desc field reads POLICY_DENIED. What does that
        specific reason code tell you, and why does it matter that
        you check it before editing any CiliumNetworkPolicy YAML?

    Q7. During the anihpj canary rollout from Chapter 10 (a 90/10
        CiliumEnvoyConfig split between anihpj-v1 and anihpj-v2),
        which single technique would you use to decide whether to
        proceed or roll back — and which Hubble flag family does
        it depend on?

    Q8. Walk through this scenario using 15.1-15.3's techniques:
        `hubble observe --to-pod anihpj/postgres-0 --verdict
        DROPPED` shows a flow from anihpj-worker-xyz with reason
        POLICY_DENIED, and the pod's labels include app=anihpj but
        NOT component=web. Which specific selector construct in the
        CiliumNetworkPolicy is most likely responsible, and what
        one-line fix resolves it without loosening the policy to
        "allow everything with app=anihpj"?

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 15):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ `hubble observe --namespace/--http-status` turns "the
            API is throwing 500s somewhere" into an exact pod and
            endpoint in one command — Hubble's identity-aware, HTTP-
            literate view goes further than Ch9's node-scoped,
            L3/L4-only `cilium monitor`.

         ✅ Finding a "top talker" isn't a single flag — pipe
            `hubble observe ... -o json` through jq/sort/uniq -c to
            aggregate flow counts per source pod, since Cilium's OSS
            CiliumNetworkPolicy has no native rate-limit field to
            fall back on.

         ✅ `hubble observe --verdict DROPPED` plus -o json's
            drop_reason_desc is the fastest way to catch a policy
            selector bug — but always confirm the reason really is
            POLICY_DENIED before editing any YAML.

         ✅ Comparing HTTP status codes and latency between two
            versions (e.g. anihpj-v1/-v2 behind Ch10's
            CiliumEnvoyConfig canary) with Hubble turns "does the
            canary look okay?" into a real go/no-go number.

         ✅ Hubble's flow data lives in a small, in-memory,
            per-node ring buffer — it is a real-time lens, not a
            durable audit log; export it (Hubble Metrics, the
            exporter) if you need history.
