Chapter 16: Transparent Encryption — WireGuard for Free
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ This chapter is graded under the CCA Service Mesh domain (16%
            weight) — "Encrypting Traffic in Transit" is a Service Mesh
            subtopic, not a standalone Encryption domain
         ✅ Why encrypting pod traffic matters even inside a "private"
            cluster network — defense in depth, HIPAA/PCI-DSS/GDPR
            compliance drivers, and zero-trust rationale
         ✅ WireGuard vs IPSec — real tradeoffs (kernel version, cipher,
            hardware offload, config complexity) so you can justify a
            choice, not just name one
         ✅ How to enable transparent encryption with a single Helm flag —
            zero application code changes required
         ✅ How WireGuard tunnels actually form: one tunnel per NODE PAIR,
            never per pod — and how keys are exchanged and rotated
         ✅ The exam's favorite "gotcha" distinction: mutual authentication
            (Ch10.3, proves WHO is connecting) vs encryption (Ch16, protects
            WHAT'S on the wire) — and why you need both, not either

    16.1 Why Encrypt Pod Traffic?
         - Defense in depth: even if the network itself is compromised — a
           rogue node, a misconfigured route, a passive tap on a shared
           switch — data in flight stays unreadable without the key
         - Compliance drivers: HIPAA (health data), PCI-DSS (card data), and
           GDPR (EU personal data) all expect — or flatly require — data in
           transit to be encrypted between services, not just at rest
         - Zero-trust networking: identity-based policy (Ch4.4, Ch7) answers
           "is this connection ALLOWED?" — encryption answers "if someone
           taps the wire anyway, can they READ it?" — two separate guarantees
         - anihpj's own compliance case: postgres stores User/Author PII
           (name, email) — an auditor will ask whether THAT traffic is
           encrypted in transit, not only whether it's access-controlled
         [DIAGRAM: Unencrypted pod traffic vs WireGuard-encrypted]

         [DEFENSE IN DEPTH — Compliance & Zero-Trust Case for Encryption]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  LAYER 1 — Identity-based policy (Ch4, Ch7)                  │
         │    CiliumNetworkPolicy decides WHO may even ATTEMPT to       │
         │    connect — e.g. anihpj → postgres:5432, nothing else       │
         │         │  still assumes the network path itself is honest   │
         │         ▼                                                    │
         │  LAYER 2 — Mutual authentication (Ch10.3, SPIFFE/SPIRE)      │
         │    Both sides PROVE their identity before the connection     │
         │    is let through — stops IP-spoofing / impersonation        │
         │         │  still doesn't protect bytes already in flight     │
         │         ▼                                                    │
         │  LAYER 3 — Transparent encryption (THIS CHAPTER)             │
         │    WireGuard/IPSec — a compromised node, a misrouted packet, │
         │    or a passive tap on the wire sees only ciphertext         │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 HIPAA / PCI-DSS / GDPR audits ask about ALL THREE layers. │
         │     "The cluster network is private" is not an answer any of │
         │     them accept — attackers who reach layer 1 (compromised   │
         │     node, misconfigured VPC peering) still hit layer 3.      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.2 WireGuard vs IPSec — Which Should You Choose?
         - WireGuard (Cilium's recommended default): kernel-native since
           Linux 5.6 (CONFIG_WIREGUARD=m), a simpler protocol/codebase, and
           the modern ChaCha20-Poly1305 cipher
         - IPSec: the older IETF-standard approach, built on the Linux
           kernel's mature XFRM/ESP framework, using AES-GCM — and able to
           lean on AES-NI hardware crypto offload where the CPU supports it
         - Real tradeoff, not "WireGuard always wins": WireGuard's simpler
           cipher doesn't NEED AES-NI to be fast, but on a CPU that DOES have
           AES-NI, IPSec's hardware-accelerated AES-GCM can out-perform
           WireGuard's software ChaCha20-Poly1305 per core
         - IPSec has a bottleneck WireGuard doesn't: Cilium's IPSec
           decryption is limited to a single CPU core per tunnel — a busy
           node-pair can become CPU-bound on that one core
         - You pick ONE mode cluster-wide via encryption.type — Cilium does
           not run WireGuard and IPSec side-by-side in the same cluster
         [COMPARISON TABLE: WireGuard vs IPSec for Cilium]

         [WIREGUARD vs IPSEC — Real Tradeoffs for Cilium]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ASPECT            WIREGUARD         IPSEC                   │
         │  ───────────────   ───────────────   ────────────────────    │
         │  Kernel support    Native since 5.6  XFRM/ESP (mature)       │
         │  Cipher used       ChaCha20-Poly1305 AES-GCM (+AES-NI HW)    │
         │  HW crypto offload Not required      Uses AES-NI if present  │
         │  Node key mgmt     Auto keypair/node K8s Secret + KEYID      │
         │  Rotation model    Re-announce key   KEYID 1-15, increments  │
         │  Known bottleneck  None documented   Decrypt: 1 core/tunnel  │
         │  Cilium default    Recommended       Available, mature       │
         │  encryption.type=  wireguard         ipsec                   │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Neither is "wrong." WireGuard is the simpler modern       │
         │     default; IPSec earns its keep on hosts with AES-NI HW    │
         │     offload or shops with existing IPSec tooling/audits.     │
         │  ⚠️  Same-node pod-to-pod traffic is NEVER wire-encrypted by │
         │     either mode — it never leaves the host's kernel, so there│
         │     is no wire to tap. This is expected, not a misconfig.    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.3 Enabling Transparent Encryption — One Flag, Zero Code Changes
         - --set encryption.enabled=true --set encryption.type=wireguard
         - That's it! Cilium encrypts ALL pod-to-pod traffic
         - How to verify: cilium status | grep Encryption
         [COMMAND BLOCK: Enable and verify encryption]

         [ENCRYPTION ENABLED — Before vs After]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  WITHOUT ENCRYPTION            WITH WIREGUARD ENCRYPTION     │
         │                                                              │
         │  anihpj ──── plain text ────▶ postgres                      │
         │  😱 Anyone can sniff          anihpj ──🔒 encrypted──▶ postgres
         │     passwords!                ✅ Even if network is          │
         │                                  compromised, data is safe!   │
         │                                                              │
         │  🔧 NO CODE CHANGES REQUIRED!                                │
         │  Django still connects to postgres:5432 as before           │
         │  Cilium transparently encrypts at the kernel level           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.4 Node-to-Node Encryption
         - Encryption happens NODE-TO-NODE, not pod-to-pod: Cilium builds one
           WireGuard (or IPSec) tunnel per pair of nodes, and every pod's
           traffic crossing that node boundary rides the same tunnel
         - Same-node pod-to-pod traffic is never wire-encrypted by design —
           it never leaves the node's kernel, so there is no wire to tap
         - Key exchange: each node auto-generates its own WireGuard keypair
           and publishes only the PUBLIC key, via the CiliumNode custom
           resource's network.cilium.io/wg-pub-key annotation — no manual
           certificate exchange or PKI to run yourself
         - Control-plane nodes are excluded from node-to-node encryption by
           default, to avoid a bootstrapping problem: the connection used to
           push a NEW public key would itself be encrypted with the OLD key
           about to be replaced
         - Performance impact: Cilium's own documentation describes the
           overhead qualitatively as minimal/kernel-native rather than
           publishing one official benchmark percentage — treat any specific
           number you see elsewhere as environment-dependent, not a constant

         [WIREGUARD MESH — Node-to-Node Tunnels, Not Per-Pod]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │   NODE A (10.0.1.5)  ──── wg0 tunnel ────  NODE B (10.0.2.7) │
         │   [anihpj, jobpost pods] (encrypted) [postgres pod + replica]│
         │          (ALL pods on Node A share this ONE tunnel to Node   │
         │           B — encrypted, kernel-native, no per-pod tunnel)   │
         │                                                              │
         │  • 3 nodes = 3 tunnels total (one per PAIR: A-B, A-C, B-C)   │
         │    — NOT one tunnel per pod. Every pod on Node A sharing     │
         │    the same tunnel to Node B is what "transparent" means.    │
         │  • Each node auto-generates its own WireGuard keypair and    │
         │    publishes the PUBLIC key via the CiliumNode CR's          │
         │    network.cilium.io/wg-pub-key annotation — no manual PKI.  │
         │  • Control-plane nodes are excluded from this mesh by        │
         │    default — rotating a key over a connection encrypted BY   │
         │    that same key is a bootstrapping problem Cilium avoids.   │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Performance: WireGuard's ChaCha20-Poly1305 runs entirely  │
         │     in-kernel with no per-pod overhead — Cilium's own docs   │
         │     describe the cost as minimal, without a benchmark number │
         │     that would survive copy-pasting into another cluster.    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         📖 Key Term: Node-to-Node Encryption
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Node-to-node encryption means the WireGuard (or IPSec)      │
         │  tunnel is established between two NODES' host IPs — not     │
         │  between two individual pod IPs. Traffic from every pod on   │
         │  Node A bound for every pod on Node B rides the SAME tunnel. │
         │                                                              │
         │  • Encrypted:  any traffic that crosses the physical/virtual │
         │    link between two nodes                                    │
         │  • NOT encrypted: traffic between two pods on the SAME node  │
         │    (it never leaves that node's kernel — nothing to tap)     │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Analogy: it's not a private letter sealed per pod — it's  │
         │     an armored truck between two buildings. Everything inside│
         │     the truck (every pod's traffic) rides safe together;     │
         │     mail moved between two desks in the SAME building never  │
         │     gets loaded onto a truck at all.                         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.5 Encryption vs Mutual Authentication — "WHAT'S Protected" vs "WHO Is Connecting"
         - Ch10.3 taught Mutual Authentication (SPIFFE/SPIRE): both sides of
           a connection cryptographically PROVE their identity before the
           connection is allowed through — that is an authentication control
         - This chapter's WireGuard/IPSec is an encryption control: once a
           connection is allowed, the BYTES on the wire become unreadable to
           anyone who taps the network — it does not, by itself, check WHO
           is on either end of the wire
         - Neither replaces the other: mutual auth without encryption still
           leaves plaintext on the wire; encryption without mutual auth still
           lets an unverified peer connect (as long as policy allows it)
         - Real anihpj setup: a CiliumNetworkPolicy's authentication: mode:
           "required" block (Ch10.3) plus cluster-wide encryption.enabled:
           true (Ch16) together mean postgres only accepts PROVEN anihpj
           connections, and even a network tap sees only ciphertext

         [MUTUAL AUTH (Ch10.3) vs ENCRYPTION (Ch16) — WHO vs WHAT'S PROTECTED]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CONTROL         MUTUAL AUTH (Ch10.3)  ENCRYPTION (Ch16)     │
         │  ────────────    ────────────────────  ──────────────────    │
         │  Answers         WHO is connecting     What's on the wire    │
         │  Mechanism       SPIFFE/SPIRE SVID     WireGuard/IPSec tunnel│
         │  Enforced via    authentication.mode   encryption.type=      │
         │  Scope           Per policy rule (pod) Per node PAIR (host)  │
         │  Alone, it means ID proven, bytes open Bytes hid, ID unproven│
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Together: anihpj is PROVEN to be anihpj (Ch10.3) AND its  │
         │     traffic to postgres is UNREADABLE in transit (Ch16).     │
         │     The exam tests this pairing — neither one substitutes    │
         │     for the other.                                           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    16.6 Key Rotation and Encryption Performance Overhead
         - Ch16.4 covered how a key gets INTO the mesh — a per-node
           keypair, published as a public key via CiliumNode's
           network.cilium.io/wg-pub-key annotation. This section covers
           what happens to that key OVER TIME, and what turning
           encryption on actually costs in throughput
         - WireGuard rotation, honestly: Cilium's own documentation does
           not publish an automatic, scheduled rotation for a node's
           WireGuard keypair. Under normal operation a node generates its
           keypair once, at cilium-agent startup, and keeps it indefinitely
         - Rotating it today is a manual, per-node operation — regenerate
           that node's local key material and restart its cilium-agent.
           The agent then mints a fresh keypair and republishes the new
           public key through the SAME wg-pub-key annotation mechanism
           from 16.4 — there is no separate rotation API or CLI verb
         - ⚠️  This is a genuinely under-specified area of Cilium's own
           project — even Cilium's own contributors have open questions
           about testing WireGuard key rotation. Treat "how often we
           rotate WireGuard keys" as YOUR organization's policy to define,
           not a behavior Cilium enforces for you
         - IPSec's story is different, and better documented: 16.2 already
           noted IPSec's per-node state lives in a Kubernetes Secret keyed
           by a KEYID (1-15, wrapping back to 1). Rotating it means
           patching that Secret with a fresh pre-shared key and an
           incremented KEYID
         - Cilium documents an explicit dual-key OVERLAP window for that
           rotation: the agent tracks, per remote endpoint, which KEYID
           that peer is currently using — so it keeps decrypting with the
           OLD key for any peer that hasn't picked up the new one yet,
           while it encrypts new outbound traffic with the NEW key
         - That overlap is time-bounded, not indefinite: by default,
           Cilium removes the old key only after a 5-minute grace window,
           and agents typically pick up a changed key within about 1
           minute of the change — a real safety cushion, not a race
         - The number of active keys briefly rises during that window:
           from 2 keys per remote node per IP family at steady state, up
           to 4 per remote node per IP family mid-rotation — documented
           Cilium behavior, not a leak or a misconfiguration if you see it
         - Performance, honestly: 16.2 already established WHY the
           tradeoff exists — WireGuard's ChaCha20-Poly1305 is designed to
           be fast in software with no AES-NI dependency; IPSec's AES-GCM
           leans heavily on AES-NI hardware offload where the CPU has it
         - In practice: on nodes WITHOUT AES-NI, WireGuard usually wins;
           on nodes WITH AES-NI, IPSec can be competitive with — or
           faster than — WireGuard. Neither mode "always wins"
         - Resist quoting one fixed percentage for either mode: expect
           typically single-digit to low-double-digit percent throughput
           overhead versus unencrypted traffic, varying by CPU, kernel
           version, MTU, and packet size. Cilium's own docs deliberately
           avoid publishing one portable benchmark number, and this guide
           won't invent one either — benchmark encryption.enabled=true on
           YOUR node type before writing a number into a capacity plan
         - anihpj-relevant angle: anihpj → postgres traffic is small,
           frequent, latency-sensitive queries, not bulk transfer — that
           traffic shape is exactly where "just benchmark it" matters
           most, since small-packet overhead behaves differently from a
           large-payload throughput test

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ASPECT            WIREGUARD         IPSEC                   │
         │  ───────────────   ───────────────   ────────────────────    │
         │  Rotation trigger  Manual, per node  Manual, KEYID bump      │
         │  Fixed cadence?    Not documented    Not automatic either    │
         │  Agent restart?    Yes, regenerates  No, hot-reloaded        │
         │  Overlap window    Not documented    Dual-key, ~5 min        │
         │  Normal key count  1 per peer        2 per IP family         │
         │  Keys mid-rotation n/a (no overlap)  Up to 4/IP family       │
         │  Pubkey update via wg-pub-key annot. K8s Secret + KEYID      │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 IPSec's overlap is DOCUMENTED (Cilium tracks per-         │
         │     endpoint which KEYID a peer uses); WireGuard's key       │
         │     lifecycle is NOT scheduled or automated by Cilium —      │
         │     treat any cadence you pick as YOUR ops policy, not a     │
         │     Cilium built-in.                                         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Use 16.2's comparison for "which mode is faster on day one";
           use the checklist below for "which mode fits how THIS cluster
           changes, and gets audited, over time"

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ☐ CHECK 1: Do your nodes have AES-NI?                       │
         │     $ grep -m1 -o aes /proc/cpuinfo                          │
         │     Found on every node  → IPSec is a real perf contender    │
         │     Not found anywhere   → WireGuard's software cipher wins  │
         │                                                              │
         │  ☐ CHECK 2: How often do nodes join or leave the cluster?    │
         │     Frequent churn (autoscaling, spot/preemptible nodes)     │
         │     → IPSec's KEYID+Secret rotation is scriptable today;     │
         │       WireGuard's key refresh is a manual, per-node step     │
         │                                                              │
         │  ☐ CHECK 3: Does compliance mandate a fixed rotation cadence?│
         │     Yes → IPSec's documented dual-key overlap window makes   │
         │     a scheduled, auditable rotation safer to automate today  │
         │                                                              │
         │  ☐ CHECK 4: What's the real traffic shape? (anihpj →         │
         │     postgres chatty small packets vs. bulk data transfer)    │
         │     Don't assume a percentage — benchmark on YOUR node       │
         │     type/kernel/MTU before writing one into an SLA           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 16):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Expecting to see an encryption toggle per POD, the same
            way a CiliumNetworkPolicy targets a pod's labels
            → TRUTH: Encryption is configured cluster-wide and applied per
              NODE PAIR — there is no per-pod encryption flag to flip.
            → FIX: Ask "is node-to-node traffic protected cluster-wide?" —
              not "did I turn on encryption for THIS pod?"

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Trying to run WireGuard on some nodes and IPSec on
            others (or flipping encryption.type without a plan) in one cluster
            → TRUTH: encryption.type is a single cluster-wide setting —
              Cilium does not support mixing WireGuard and IPSec at once.
            → FIX: Pick one mode for the whole cluster. If you must switch,
              treat it as a deliberate migration, not a per-node toggle.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Assuming "we enabled encryption" means the connection
            is fully secured end-to-end
            → TRUTH: Encryption alone only protects confidentiality on the
              wire — it does not verify WHO is connecting. That's mutual
              authentication's job (Ch10.3), a separate control.
            → FIX: For anything the exam (or an auditor) calls "secure," pair
              authentication: mode: "required" (Ch10.3) WITH
              encryption.enabled: true (Ch16) — not one or the other.

         ❌ MISTAKE 4: Assuming Cilium auto-rotates WireGuard keys on some
            schedule, the way many TLS certificates auto-renew
            → TRUTH: Cilium does not document a built-in, scheduled
              WireGuard key-rotation job (Ch16.6) — a node's keypair is
              generated once at agent startup and normally stays put.
            → FIX: If a compliance policy requires periodic key rotation,
              build and own that schedule yourself (regenerate + restart
              the agent per node) — don't assume Cilium runs it for you.

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 16):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. What encryption types does Cilium support?
    Q2. Do you need to change your application code for encryption?
    Q3. How do you enable WireGuard encryption?
    Q4. What is the performance impact of WireGuard encryption?

    Q5. True or False: Cilium's WireGuard encryption creates a separate
        tunnel for every PAIR of communicating PODS.

    Q6. Which limitation is specific to Cilium's IPSec implementation and
        does NOT apply to WireGuard?
        A) It cannot use the AES-GCM cipher
        B) Decryption is limited to a single CPU core per tunnel
        C) It requires a GPU for crypto offload
        D) It only works in tunnel/VXLAN routing mode

    Q7. Your team added authentication: mode: "required" to a
        CiliumNetworkPolicy governing anihpj → postgres traffic (Ch10.3) and
        tells a compliance auditor the connection is "encrypted." Is that
        accurate? What is actually guaranteed, and what would you add to
        also guarantee the bytes on the wire are unreadable?

    Q8. Why are Cilium's own control-plane nodes typically excluded from
        node-to-node WireGuard encryption by default?

    Q9. True or False: Cilium automatically rotates each node's WireGuard
        keypair on a fixed schedule, the way many TLS certificate tools
        auto-renew.

    Q10. During an IPSec key rotation in Cilium, why doesn't traffic to a
         peer that hasn't picked up the new key yet get dropped?

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 16):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ Encryption belongs to the CCA Service Mesh domain (16% weight) —
            "Encrypting Traffic in Transit" is a Service Mesh subtopic, not a
            standalone Encryption domain, and it's graded alongside Ch10.3.

         ✅ Encrypt pod traffic for defense in depth: HIPAA/PCI-DSS/GDPR
            compliance and zero-trust both require more than "the network is
            private" — identity policy, mutual auth, AND encryption together.

         ✅ WireGuard (kernel-native since Linux 5.6, ChaCha20-Poly1305) is
            Cilium's simpler modern default; IPSec (XFRM/ESP, AES-GCM,
            AES-NI-capable) earns its keep on hardware with crypto offload —
            pick ONE mode cluster-wide, never mix them.

         ✅ Encryption is NODE-TO-NODE, not per-pod: one WireGuard/IPSec
            tunnel per pair of nodes carries every pod's traffic crossing
            that boundary; same-node pod traffic is never wire-encrypted
            because it never leaves the node's kernel.

         ✅ Encryption (WHAT'S protected on the wire) and mutual
            authentication (Ch10.3, WHO is connecting) are separate controls
            — the exam expects you to pair authentication: mode: "required"
            with encryption.enabled: true, not treat either as sufficient
            alone.

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         You've now covered every layer that protects a single connection —
         identity policy, mutual auth, and encryption. Chapter 17 zooms out
         from ONE cluster to MANY: Cluster Mesh, the CCA's own standalone
         Cluster Mesh domain (10% weight), connects multiple Kubernetes
         clusters so anihpj running in us-east can talk directly to a
         postgres pod in eu-west — no VPN, no custom routing, just Cilium.


Chapter 17: Cluster Mesh — Connecting Multiple Kubernetes Clusters
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ This ONE chapter carries the ENTIRE Cluster Mesh exam domain (10%
            weight) — both official subtopics: multi-cluster benefits AND
            cross-cluster service discovery/load balancing
         ✅ The 4 real prerequisites: unique cluster ID (1-255), unique cluster
            name, non-overlapping Pod CIDRs, mutual node/pod IP reachability
         ✅ The real cilium clustermesh enable / connect / status CLI
            workflow — actual commands, not hand-waved steps
         ✅ Why cilium clustermesh connect only needs to run in ONE
            direction — Cilium establishes the reverse link automatically
         ✅ The real global-service mechanism: service.cilium.io/global —
            it's a plain Service annotation, not a new CRD
         ✅ The real service-affinity mechanism: service.cilium.io/affinity
            — how "prefer local, fail over to remote" actually works
         ✅ The 3 Cluster Mesh mistakes that break connectivity SILENTLY
            (no error message — just traffic that mysteriously never arrives)

    17.1 Why Connect Multiple Clusters?
         - High availability across regions (US + EU + Asia)
         - Disaster recovery
         - Data locality / compliance
         - Example: anihpj running in us-east and eu-west clusters
         [DIAGRAM: Two clusters connected via Cluster Mesh]

         [CLUSTER MESH — anihpj Global Deployment]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                              │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────┐    ┌─────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │   CLUSTER: us-east      │    │   CLUSTER: eu-west       │  │
         │  │   (Virginia, USA)       │    │   (Frankfurt, Germany)   │  │
         │  │                         │    │                          │  │
         │  │  ┌──────┐  ┌─────────┐  │    │  ┌──────┐  ┌─────────┐  │  │
         │  │  │anihpj│  │postgres │  │    │  │anihpj│  │postgres │  │  │
         │  │  │ pod  │  │  pod    │  │    │  │ pod  │  │  pod    │  │  │
         │  │  └──┬───┘  └────┬────┘  │    │  └──┬───┘  └────┬────┘  │  │
         │  │     │           │       │    │     │           │       │  │
         │  │     └───────────┘       │    │     └───────────┘       │  │
         │  │     Local traffic ✅    │    │     Local traffic ✅    │  │
         │  └───────────┬─────────────┘    └───────────┬─────────────┘  │
         │              │                              │                │
         │              │    ┌──────────────────┐      │                │
         │              └────│  CLUSTER MESH     │──────┘                │
         │                   │  (Encrypted Tunnel)│                       │
         │                   │                    │                       │
         │                   │  us-east pod ←───▶ eu-west pod           │
         │                   │  (Direct pod-to-pod!)                    │
         │                   └──────────────────┘                       │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 anihpj pod in US can talk DIRECTLY to postgres in EU!    │
         │     Just use the same service name: postgres.anihpj.svc      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    17.2 Setting Up Cluster Mesh — Step by Step
         - Prerequisites: Both clusters running Cilium
         - Step 1: Enable Cluster Mesh on both clusters
         - Step 2: Establish trust (exchange certificates)
         - Step 3: Connect the clusters
         - Step 4: Verify: hubble observe --cluster us-east
         [COMMAND BLOCK: Complete Cluster Mesh setup]

         📖 Key Term: Cluster ID & Cluster Name
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ Every cluster joining a Cluster Mesh needs TWO identifiers,  │
         │ set at install time and NEVER changed afterward:             │
         │                                                              │
         │ • cluster.id  — a number from 1-255 (0 is RESERVED — it means│
         │   "not part of any mesh" and Cilium will refuse to start the │
         │   mesh with it: error "ClusterID 0 is reserved")             │
         │ • cluster.name — a human-readable name, ≤32 chars, lowercase │
         │   alphanumeric + dashes only, must start/end alphanumeric    │
         │                                                              │
         │ 💡 Default max mesh size is 255 clusters (IDs 1-255). Cilium's│
         │    maxConnectedClusters option raises this to 511 clusters,  │
         │    but trades away some cluster-local security-identity space│
         │    to do it — most deployments never need to touch this.     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [COMPLETE CLUSTER MESH SETUP — us-east ↔ eu-west]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ PREREQUISITES — verify all 4 before running any command:     │
         │   ✅ Both clusters already running Cilium as their CNI        │
         │   ✅ Unique cluster.id per cluster (1-255 — see Key Term)     │
         │   ✅ Unique cluster.name per cluster (us-east / eu-west here) │
         │   ✅ Pod CIDRs NON-OVERLAPPING across every cluster & node    │
         │      (10.1.0.0/16 for us-east, 10.2.0.0/16 for eu-west —     │
         │      never reuse a range, or routing silently breaks — 17.4) │
         │                                                              │
         │ STEP 1 — Install Cilium with a cluster identity (per ctx):   │
         │   $ cilium install --context us-east \                       │
         │       --set cluster.name=us-east --set cluster.id=1          │
         │   $ cilium install --context eu-west \                       │
         │       --set cluster.name=eu-west --set cluster.id=2          │
         │                                                              │
         │ STEP 2 — Enable Cluster Mesh on BOTH clusters (deploys the   │
         │   clustermesh-apiserver + generates/exchanges mTLS certs):   │
         │   $ cilium clustermesh enable --context us-east              │
         │   $ cilium clustermesh enable --context eu-west              │
         │                                                              │
         │ STEP 3 — Wait until each API server is actually ready:       │
         │   $ cilium clustermesh status --context us-east --wait       │
         │   $ cilium clustermesh status --context eu-west --wait       │
         │                                                              │
         │ STEP 4 — Connect the clusters (run from ONE side only — see  │
         │   the Pitfall box below for why running it twice is harmless │
         │   but unnecessary):                                          │
         │   $ cilium clustermesh connect --context us-east \           │
         │       --destination-context eu-west                          │
         │                                                              │
         │ STEP 5 — Verify the mesh is actually forwarding traffic:     │
         │   $ cilium clustermesh status --context us-east --wait       │
         │   $ cilium connectivity test --context us-east \             │
         │       --multi-cluster eu-west                                │
         │   $ hubble observe --cluster us-east                         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         💡 cilium clustermesh connect is a ONE-WAY command that builds a
            TWO-WAY connection. Cilium's own docs are explicit: the peering
            "only needs to be done in one direction. The connection will
            automatically be established in both directions." Running it
            from both sides is redundant, not wrong.

    17.3 Service Affinity — "Prefer Local, Fail Over to Remote"
         - How Cilium routes traffic in a Cluster Mesh
         - Local preference: traffic stays in the same cluster when possible
         - Failover: automatically route to remote cluster if local pod is down
         [DIAGRAM: Service affinity — local first, remote as backup]

         📖 Key Term: Global Service
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ A Global Service is an ordinary K8s Service carrying the     │
         │ annotation service.cilium.io/global: "true". Cilium then     │
         │ load-balances it across backend pods in EVERY connected      │
         │ cluster that has a Service of the SAME name + namespace,     │
         │ also marked global — not just its own cluster's backends.    │
         │                                                              │
         │ apiVersion: v1                                               │
         │ kind: Service                                                │
         │ metadata:                                                    │
         │   name: postgres                                             │
         │   namespace: anihpj                                          │
         │   annotations:                                               │
         │     service.cilium.io/global: "true"  # opt into cross-      │
         │                                      # cluster load-balancing│
         │ spec:                                                        │
         │   selector:                                                  │
         │     app: postgres                                            │
         │   ports:                                                     │
         │   - port: 5432                                               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [SERVICE AFFINITY — Local First, Remote Only on Total Local Failure]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                 Client pod: anihpj (us-east)                 │
         │                          │                                   │
         │                          │ curl postgres.anihpj.svc          │
         │                          ▼                                   │
         │           ┌──────────────────────────────┐                   │
         │           │  GLOBAL SERVICE: postgres     │                  │
         │           │  service.cilium.io/global:    │                  │
         │           │    "true"                     │                  │
         │           │  service.cilium.io/affinity:  │                  │
         │           │    "local"                    │                  │
         │           └──────────────┬────────────────┘                  │
         │                          │                                   │
         │               Are there HEALTHY backends                     │
         │               in the LOCAL cluster (us-east)?                │
         │                          │                                   │
         │            ┌─────────────┴─────────────┐                     │
         │            ▼ YES                   ▼ NO (all local down)     │
         │   🟢 Route to LOCAL           🔴 Fail over to REMOTE           │
         │      us-east postgres pod        eu-west postgres pod        │
         │      Remote (eu-west) is          (ONLY reached when every   │
         │      never even considered         local backend is down)    │
         │                                                              │
         │ 💡 affinity: "local" = prefer healthy LOCAL backends; only    │
         │    send traffic to a REMOTE cluster's backends if EVERY local│
         │    backend is unavailable or failing health checks.          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         💡 The affinity annotation has THREE valid values — memorize all
            three, the exam tests the DEFAULT as much as the named modes:

         [service.cilium.io/affinity — Valid Values]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ VALUE          BEHAVIOR                                      │
         │ ─────────────  ───────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ "local"        Prefer healthy LOCAL backends; use remote ONLY│
         │                if every local backend is down/unhealthy      │
         │ "remote"       Prefer healthy REMOTE backends; use local only│
         │                if every remote backend is down/unhealthy     │
         │ "none"         NO preference — spread traffic across every   │
         │                healthy backend, local or remote, evenly.     │
         │                This is the DEFAULT if the annotation is      │
         │                absent entirely (same as writing "none").     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    17.4 Cluster Mesh Architecture — What's Actually Running Under the Hood
         - The clustermesh-apiserver: the ONE component that makes it work
         - It watches each cluster's K8s API (Services, Endpoints, Nodes,
           identities) and republishes that state over an etcd-compatible
           key/value protocol, secured with mutual TLS
         - It must be REACHABLE from every OTHER cluster in the mesh —
           exposed via a Kubernetes Service of type LoadBalancer, NodePort,
           or ClusterIP

         [CLUSTERMESH-APISERVER — The Cross-Cluster Bridge]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CLUSTER: us-east                                            │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐      │
         │  │ clustermesh-apiserver                               │     │
         │  │ watches K8s API: Services, Endpoints, Nodes,        │     │
         │  │ identities  →  exposed via LoadBalancer/NodePort/   │     │
         │  │ ClusterIP so OTHER clusters can reach it            │     │
         │  └───────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                          │                                   │
         │                          │  mTLS-secured, etcd-compatible    │
         │                          │  key/value protocol (both ways)   │
         │                          ▼                                   │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐      │
         │  │ clustermesh-apiserver                               │     │
         │  │ watches K8s API: Services, Endpoints, Nodes,        │     │
         │  │ identities  →  exposed via LoadBalancer/NodePort/   │     │
         │  │ ClusterIP so OTHER clusters can reach it            │     │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘      │
         │  CLUSTER: eu-west                                            │
         │                                                              │
         │ 💡 Each cluster's Cilium agents connect OUT to the REMOTE     │
         │    cluster's clustermesh-apiserver — this is why it must be  │
         │    reachable across the network boundary (VPC peering, VPN,  │
         │    or a public LB), not just reachable from inside its own   │
         │    cluster.                                                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         💡 Four requirements decide whether the mesh actually works —
            each one fails SILENTLY (no error banner) if you skip it:

         [Cluster Mesh Requirements — Quick Reference]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ • CLUSTER ID — unique per cluster, valid range 1-255 (0 is   │
         │   reserved/disabled). Duplicate IDs corrupt cluster-local    │
         │   security identities — pods may get the WRONG identity.     │
         │                                                              │
         │ • CLUSTER NAME — unique per cluster, ≤32 chars, lowercase    │
         │   alphanumeric+dashes only. A collision makes the            │
         │   clustermesh-apiserver reject the configuration outright.   │
         │                                                              │
         │ • POD CIDR — non-overlapping across EVERY cluster and node.  │
         │   Overlap breaks cross-cluster routing SILENTLY — traffic    │
         │   is misrouted or dropped, with no obvious error.            │
         │                                                              │
         │ • DATAPATH MODE — every cluster in the mesh must use the     │
         │   SAME mode (encapsulation OR native-routing). Mixing modes  │
         │   silently drops cross-cluster packets.                      │
         │                                                              │
         │ • API SERVER REACHABILITY — clustermesh-apiserver must be    │
         │   reachable FROM every other cluster's agents, or status     │
         │   hangs and global Services never show remote backends.      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    17.5 Global Services — Declaring and Sharing Services Across the Mesh
         - service.cilium.io/global: "true" — the ONLY thing that makes
           a Service participate in cross-cluster load balancing at all
         - service.cilium.io/shared — controls whether THIS cluster's
           backends are exposed TO other clusters (default: shared)
         - service.cilium.io/affinity — covered in depth in 17.3 above

         [Global Service Annotations — Full Reference]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ service.cilium.io/global = "true"                            │
         │   → Opts this Service into cross-cluster load balancing.     │
         │     With no other annotation, this is ALL you need.          │
         │                                                              │
         │ service.cilium.io/shared = "true" (default) | "false"        │
         │   → "true":  share THIS cluster's backends with the rest     │
         │     of the mesh (the normal case)                            │
         │   → "false": CONSUME remote backends but do NOT expose       │
         │     this cluster's own backends to other clusters            │
         │                                                              │
         │ service.cilium.io/affinity = "local" | "remote" | "none"     │
         │   → "local":  prefer local, fail over to remote only if      │
         │     ALL local backends are unhealthy (see 17.3)              │
         │   → "remote": prefer remote, fail over to local only if      │
         │     ALL remote backends are unhealthy                        │
         │   → "none" (default if omitted): no preference — spread      │
         │     across every healthy backend, local or remote            │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [anihpj Real-World Example — Global Postgres Service]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ SCENARIO: anihpj's PostgreSQL runs PRIMARY in us-east, with a│
         │ read-replica in eu-west. EU traffic should read the LOCAL    │
         │ replica whenever it's healthy, but the app must still work if│
         │ the eu-west replica pod is temporarily down.                 │
         │                                                              │
         │ # Applied identically in BOTH clusters — same name+namespace │
         │ # is what makes Cilium treat them as ONE global service:     │
         │ apiVersion: v1                                               │
         │ kind: Service                                                │
         │ metadata:                                                    │
         │   name: postgres                                             │
         │   namespace: anihpj                                          │
         │   annotations:                                               │
         │     service.cilium.io/global: "true"                         │
         │     service.cilium.io/shared: "true"  # default: export      │
         │                                       # local backends too   │
         │     service.cilium.io/affinity: "local" # eu-west reads its  │
         │                                          # own replica first,│
         │                                          # falls back to     │
         │                                          # us-east if it dies│
         │ spec:                                                        │
         │   selector:                                                  │
         │     app: postgres                                            │
         │   ports:                                                     │
         │   - port: 5432                                               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 17):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Letting two clusters share the same Pod CIDR range
            → TRUTH: Cluster Mesh requires non-overlapping Pod CIDRs across
              EVERY cluster and node — this isn't a soft recommendation.
            → FIX: Plan CIDR ranges before day one (e.g. 10.1.0.0/16 for
              us-east, 10.2.0.0/16 for eu-west). Overlapping ranges break
              cross-cluster routing silently — packets vanish, no error.

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: Reusing cluster.id (or leaving it at the default) on
            a second cluster
            → TRUTH: Every cluster in the mesh needs a UNIQUE cluster.id in
              1-255. cluster.id 0 is RESERVED and means "not meshed."
              Duplicate IDs corrupt cluster-local security identities.
            → FIX: Track assigned cluster IDs in one place (a spreadsheet
              or an IaC variable file) before running cilium install.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Assuming Cluster Mesh works the moment cilium
            clustermesh connect returns — without checking reachability
            → TRUTH: The clustermesh-apiserver must be reachable FROM every
              other cluster (LoadBalancer/NodePort exposure + firewall/
              security-group rules that allow it). A misconfigured
              firewall leaves status hanging or global Services never
              showing remote backends — with no obvious error message.
            → FIX: Always run cilium clustermesh status --wait AND
              cilium connectivity test --multi-cluster <ctx> before
              trusting the mesh — don't stop at "the connect command
              didn't fail."

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 17):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. What's the main use case for Cluster Mesh?
    Q2. Can pods in different clusters communicate directly?
    Q3. What is service affinity in the context of Cluster Mesh?

    Q4. Two clusters, us-east (cluster.id=1) and eu-west, are being joined
        into a Cluster Mesh. A teammate suggests leaving eu-west's
        cluster.id at its default value. What's wrong with that, and what
        is the valid range for cluster.id?

    Q5. Your us-east cluster uses Pod CIDR 10.1.0.0/16 and your eu-west
        cluster ALSO uses 10.1.0.0/16. What breaks, and why does Cluster
        Mesh require non-overlapping Pod CIDRs in the first place?

    Q6. Which annotation makes a plain Kubernetes Service participate in
        cross-cluster load balancing at all?
        A) service.cilium.io/mesh          B) service.cilium.io/global
        C) cilium.io/clustermesh: "true"   D) spec.type: ClusterMesh

    Q7. A Service is annotated service.cilium.io/global: "true" and
        service.cilium.io/affinity: "local". All backend pods in the
        LOCAL cluster fail their health checks. What does Cilium do next,
        and why is this different from annotating affinity: "none"?

    Q8. You ran cilium clustermesh connect --context us-east
        --destination-context eu-west successfully — no errors. A
        teammate says the mesh must be fully working now. What ELSE must
        you verify before trusting that global Services will actually
        route cross-cluster, and which two commands would you run?

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 17):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ Cluster Mesh needs 4 real prerequisites: a unique cluster.id
            (1-255, 0 reserved), a unique cluster.name (≤32 chars, lower-
            case+dashes), non-overlapping Pod CIDRs, and mutual reachability

         ✅ The real setup workflow is cilium install --set cluster.id/
            cluster.name per cluster, then cilium clustermesh enable,
            connect --destination-context, and status --wait to verify

         ✅ cilium clustermesh connect only needs to run in ONE direction
            — Cilium establishes the reverse connection automatically

         ✅ Global Services are plain Services carrying service.cilium.io/
            global: "true" — no new CRD, cross-cluster LB is annotation-
            driven, and service.cilium.io/shared controls backend exposure

         ✅ service.cilium.io/affinity controls routing preference: "local"
            (prefer local, fail over to remote only on total local failure),
            "remote" (the mirror image), or "none" (the default — no
            preference, spread across every healthy backend everywhere)

    ⏭️  Next Chapter Preview:
    ─────────────────────────
         You've just spent an entire chapter making pods in DIFFERENT
         clusters reach each other. Chapter 18 turns the question around:
         how does a pod reach the OUTSIDE world with a predictable,
         whitelist-able IP? You'll meet the Egress Gateway — designated
         gateway nodes that give anihpj a fixed source IP when it calls an
         external payment API — and see how Egress Gateway and Cluster
         Mesh combine for geo-specific, highly-available outbound traffic.


Chapter 18: Egress Gateway — Controlling Outbound Traffic
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ Why default egress SNATs a pod's traffic to whichever NODE it
            happens to run on — the source IP an external API sees changes
            every time the pod is rescheduled
         ✅ CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy's real fields: selectors
            (podSelector/namespaceSelector + optional nodeSelector),
            destinationCIDRs/excludedCIDRs, egressGateway/egressGateways
         ✅ Why egress gateway requires BPF masquerade + kube-proxy
            replacement enabled, and Kubernetes (not kvstore) as Cilium's
            identity store
         ✅ Why Egress Gateway is explicitly NOT compatible with Cluster
            Mesh — a policy's gateway node must live in the SAME cluster
            as the pods it selects
         ✅ How to get HA egress via the egressGateways (plural) list —
            sticky per-endpoint gateway assignment plus automatic failover
         ✅ Why this chapter (Egress Connectivity Requirements) plus
            Chapter 12 (BGP/LB IPAM) together are the COMPLETE "BGP &
            External Networking" domain — the CCA's smallest at 6%, but
            still fully testable
         ✅ Why Egress Gateway (fixed OUTBOUND IP) and Ch12's LB IPAM/BGP
            (INBOUND Service IPs) are mirror-image problems, not the same
            feature

    18.1 The Egress Problem — What IP Does Your Pod Use to Call the Internet?
         - Default egress: Cilium masquerades (SNAT) a pod's traffic to
           whichever NODE it happens to be running on — there is no per-pod
           egress identity on the wire once the packet leaves the cluster
         - The problem: the SAME anihpj pod can leave via a DIFFERENT
           source IP every time it's rescheduled, because it might land on
           a different node — nodes themselves get replaced too (scaling,
           upgrades), so even a "stable" node IP eventually rotates
         - Why this matters: external partners (payment processors, banking
           APIs, partner systems) often gate access by IP allowlist — a
           moving-target source IP is simply incompatible with that model
         - Ch8 already introduced anihpj calling api.stripe.com under a
           DNS/toFQDNs allowlist; this chapter adds the OTHER half of that
           relationship — what source IP Stripe itself sees
         [DIAGRAM: Default egress — same pod, different source IP per node]

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  DEFAULT EGRESS — anihpj Calls api.stripe.com, 3 Nodes       │
         │                                                              │
         │  node-1 (10.0.1.5)   anihpj pod ──SNAT──▶ src 10.0.1.5       │
         │  node-2 (10.0.1.6)   anihpj pod ──SNAT──▶ src 10.0.1.6       │
         │  node-3 (10.0.1.7)   anihpj pod ──SNAT──▶ src 10.0.1.7       │
         │                              │                               │
         │                              ▼                               │
         │                    ┌──────────────────┐                      │
         │                    │  api.stripe.com   │                     │
         │                    │  "Who is calling  │                     │
         │                    │   me? A different │                     │
         │                    │   IP every time!" │                     │
         │                    └──────────────────┘                      │
         │                                                              │
         │  Each node masquerades (SNAT) the pod IP to ITS OWN node     │
         │  IP before the packet leaves the cluster. Stripe's           │
         │  allowlist would need ALL THREE node IPs — and a new         │
         │  entry every time a node is replaced (scaling, upgrades).    │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚠️  Node IPs are NOT stable identity — they change on       │
         │     node replacement. An allowlist keyed on them rots.       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    18.2 Cilium Egress Gateway — Fixed IP for Outbound Traffic
         - How it works: one or more "gateway nodes" are designated to
           masquerade ALL matching egress traffic with one FIXED,
           externally-routable IP — regardless of which node the pod
           actually runs on
         - CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy (apiVersion cilium.io/v2, cluster-
           scoped) is the CRD: selectors picks the source pods,
           destinationCIDRs/excludedCIDRs pick the external ranges this
           applies to, and egressGateway picks the gateway node + IP
         - Example: anihpj calling Stripe's payment API needs to appear
           from ONE fixed IP that anihpj's team gets allowlisted once —
           not re-allowlisted every time a pod reschedules

         📖 Key Term: Egress Gateway & Masquerade IP
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Egress Gateway masquerades (SNAT) egress traffic from       │
         │  selected pods to a FIXED IP that lives on a designated      │
         │  "gateway node" — instead of each node's own IP.             │
         │                                                              │
         │  • CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy (apiVersion cilium.io/v2,       │
         │    cluster-scoped — no metadata.namespace) selects WHICH     │
         │    pods (selectors), WHICH external ranges                   │
         │    (destinationCIDRs/excludedCIDRs), and WHICH node plus     │
         │    IP does the masquerading (egressGateway).                 │
         │  • Masquerade IP = egressIP (explicit) or the first IPv4     │
         │    on a named interface — never both at once.                │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Analogy: every pod gets a temporary visitor badge         │
         │     with a DIFFERENT name each time it leaves the            │
         │     building. Egress Gateway is the one badge-printer        │
         │     everyone routes through — one name, every time.          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [YAML BLOCK: CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy — anihpj → Stripe, fixed IP]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: cilium.io/v2                                    │
         │  kind: CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy                             │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: anihpj-stripe-egress  # cluster-scoped, no ns field │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    selectors:                                                │
         │    - podSelector:                                            │
         │        matchLabels:                                          │
         │          app: anihpj          # plain k8s labels — NOT       │
         │          io.kubernetes.pod.namespace: anihpj  # "k8s:"-fix!  │
         │    destinationCIDRs:                                         │
         │    - "203.0.113.0/24"   # Stripe's published API range       │
         │    excludedCIDRs:         # OPTIONAL: carve-out, SNAT'd      │
         │    - "203.0.113.128/25"  # normally (skips the gateway)      │
         │    egressGateway:                                            │
         │      nodeSelector:        # which NODE is the gateway        │
         │        matchLabels:                                          │
         │          egress-gateway: "true"                              │
         │      egressIP: "198.51.100.10"  # fixed IP Stripe allows     │
         │      # interface: eth1  <- alternative to egressIP, NEVER    │
         │      #                     both at the same time             │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 selectors[].podSelector is a PLAIN Kubernetes label       │
         │     selector — no "k8s:" prefix like CNP's endpointSelector  │
         │     (Ch7-8). io.kubernetes.pod.namespace is matched as-is.   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  REQUIREMENTS — Enabling Egress Gateway                      │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ BPF masquerade enabled (bpf.masquerade=true)              │
         │  ✅ kube-proxy replacement enabled                            │
         │     (Cilium requires BOTH together, not either/or)           │
         │  ✅ Identity allocation mode = kubernetes (the default)       │
         │     — egress gateway does NOT support kvstore mode           │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 All three are install-time Helm settings — no             │
         │     CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy YAML can turn them on          │
         │     by itself.                                               │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    18.3 Egress Gateway with Cluster Mesh
         - Egress Gateway and Cluster Mesh (Ch17) are BOTH real Cilium
           features, but Cilium's own docs are explicit that they do NOT
           combine into one cross-cluster policy — a policy's gateway node
           must be in the SAME cluster as the pods it selects
         - The real pattern for anihpj's us-east / eu-west Cluster Mesh
           (Ch17) is one INDEPENDENT CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy per cluster
           — each with its own local gateway node and its own allowlistable
           IP, which is exactly how you get geo-specific egress IPs
         - High availability is still available WITHIN a single cluster:
           the egressGateways (plural) list lets you name multiple
           candidate gateway nodes; Cilium assigns each pod to one and
           fails it over automatically if that gateway node goes unhealthy
         [DIAGRAM: Egress Gateway across a Cluster Mesh — per-cluster policies]

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  EGRESS GATEWAY + CLUSTER MESH — Per-Cluster, Never Cross    │
         │                                                              │
         │  CLUSTER: us-east            CLUSTER: eu-west                │
         │  ┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐             │
         │  │ anihpj pods       │       │ anihpj pods       │           │
         │  │ Policy: us-egress │       │ Policy: eu-egress │           │
         │  │ gateway: us-gw    │       │ gateway: eu-gw    │           │
         │  │ egressIP: .10     │       │ egressIP: .20     │           │
         │  └─────────┬─────────┘       └─────────┬─────────┘           │
         │            │                           │                     │
         │            ▼                           ▼                     │
         │     US compliance API          EU payment API                │
         │     (allowlists .10 ONLY)      (allowlists .20 ONLY)         │
         │                                                              │
         │  ⚠️  Cilium's own docs: egress gateway is NOT compatible     │
         │     with Cluster Mesh — a policy's gateway node MUST be      │
         │     in the SAME cluster as the pods it selects.              │
         │  ✅ FIX: one independent CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy PER        │
         │     cluster — that's how real deployments get                │
         │     geo-specific egress IPs across a Cluster Mesh.           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  HA EGRESS — egressGateways List (Multiple Candidates)       │
         │                                                              │
         │  egressGateways:              # note the PLURAL field        │
         │  - nodeSelector:                                             │
         │      matchLabels: {egress-gw: "true"}                        │
         │    egressIP: "198.51.100.10"    # candidate gateway #1       │
         │  - nodeSelector:                                             │
         │      matchLabels: {egress-gw-2: "true"}                      │
         │    egressIP: "198.51.100.11"    # candidate gateway #2       │
         │                                                              │
         │  Each anihpj pod (via its CiliumEndpoint) is assigned to     │
         │  ONE gateway, sticky for its lifetime — NOT round-robin      │
         │  across every candidate.                                     │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 If gateway #1's node fails a health check, its            │
         │     endpoints are reassigned to gateway #2 automatically     │
         │     — built-in failover, no manual YAML edit needed.         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    18.4 Egress Connectivity Requirements — The Official CCA Exam Subtopic
         - "Egress Connectivity Requirements" is the exam blueprint's OWN
           name for this chapter's subtopic (see the OFFICIAL CCA BLUEPRINT
           panel in the front matter) — everything in 18.1-18.3 above IS
           that subtopic, this section just names it explicitly
         - The requirement boils down to three real pieces, all covered
           above: (1) a designated gateway node with a real, routable IP
           the external party can actually allowlist, (2) the
           CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy CRD wiring source pods to that node,
           and (3) the install-time prerequisites from the box above (BPF
           masquerade + kube-proxy replacement + Kubernetes identity store)
         - Interaction with Ch4.7's Datapath Models: Egress Gateway is
           agnostic to the ENCAPSULATION axis (Tunnel vs. Native Routing) —
           traffic reaches the gateway node the same way regardless, then
           gets masqueraded there. It does NOT relax Ch4.7's separate
           requirement that kube-proxy replacement (a BPF datapath choice)
           be enabled; that's a hard Egress Gateway prerequisite on its own
         - Together, Chapter 12 (BGP + LB IPAM — how EXTERNAL clients reach
           IN to a Service) and Chapter 18 (Egress Gateway — how a pod's
           OWN traffic reaches OUT with a fixed IP) form the entire "BGP &
           External Networking" domain (6% weight) — the smallest domain,
           but still tested and never "skippable"
         [COMPARISON: Egress Gateway vs BGP/LB IPAM — outbound vs inbound]

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  EGRESS GATEWAY (Ch18) vs BGP + LB IPAM (Ch12)               │
         │                                                              │
         │  "My pod calls OUT to an external API — what IP does         │
         │   the OTHER side see?"                                       │
         │     → Egress Gateway answers this                            │
         │       (CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy). Fixes the OUTBOUND        │
         │       source IP.                                             │
         │                                                              │
         │  "An external client calls IN to my Service — what IP        │
         │   do THEY connect to?"                                       │
         │     → LB IPAM + BGP answer this (Ch12). Assign +             │
         │       announce an INBOUND Service IP.                        │
         │                                                              │
         │  💡 Same exam domain (BGP & External Networking, 6%),         │
         │     two opposite traffic directions — outbound vs            │
         │     inbound.                                                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ⚠️  Common Pitfalls (Chapter 18):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ❌ MISTAKE 1: Assuming the gateway node's egressIP is automatically
            reachable/allowlisted just because it's written in the YAML
            → TRUTH: Cilium does not provision, register, or announce that
              IP to anyone outside the cluster — egressIP (or the
              interface's first IPv4) must ALREADY be a real, routable
              address on the external network, exactly like any node IP.
            → FIX: confirm the gateway node's IP is routable and already
              allowlisted with the external party BEFORE writing the
              policy — test with a manual curl/dig from that node first.

         ❌ MISTAKE 2: A CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy silently does nothing
            because of a selector mismatch
            → TRUTH: selectors uses a PLAIN Kubernetes label selector
              (podSelector/namespaceSelector), NOT Cilium's own "k8s:"-
              prefixed EndpointSelector syntax from CiliumNetworkPolicy
              (Ch7-8) — mixing the two conventions means podSelector
              matches ZERO pods, and traffic just leaves via the normal
              default SNAT path. No error is ever thrown.
            → FIX: verify actual matches (e.g. kubectl get pods --show-
              labels, and check the policy's selected endpoints) before
              assuming the manifest itself is the problem.

         ❌ MISTAKE 3: Confusing Egress Gateway with LB IPAM/BGP (Ch12) as
            if they were the same feature
            → TRUTH: Egress Gateway fixes the OUTBOUND source IP a pod
              uses calling OUT to an external service; LB IPAM + BGP
              (Ch12) assign and announce an INBOUND IP so external clients
              can call IN to a Service. Different direction, different
              CRDs, different problems.
            → FIX: ask "is this MY pod calling OUT, or an EXTERNAL client
              calling IN?" — outbound = Egress Gateway (Ch18), inbound =
              LB IPAM/BGP (Ch12).

    🧪 Practice Questions (Chapter 18):
    ────────────────────────────────
    Q1. Why might you need a fixed egress IP?
    Q2. What CRD configures egress gateway?
    Q3. Can egress gateway work with Cluster Mesh?

    Q4. Which two fields inside a CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy's egressGateway
        (or an entry in egressGateways) can NEVER both be set at once?
        A) nodeSelector and egressIP
        B) interface and egressIP
        C) nodeSelector and interface
        D) destinationCIDRs and excludedCIDRs

    Q5. True or False, and explain your answer: "A single
        CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy can select pods running in the us-east
        cluster and designate a gateway node running in the eu-west
        cluster, as long as the two clusters are joined via Cluster Mesh."

    Q6. Your team applies a CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy whose
        selectors[].podSelector.matchLabels includes
        "k8s:io.kubernetes.pod.namespace: anihpj", but traffic keeps
        leaving through the normal per-node SNAT path instead of the
        gateway. What's the most likely mistake, and how would you
        confirm it?

    Q7. Write a CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy that lets anihpj pods (namespace
        anihpj) reach ONLY 203.0.113.0/24 (Stripe's published API range)
        via a gateway node labeled egress-gateway=true, masquerading with
        a fixed IP of 198.51.100.10.

    Q8. Which TWO conditions must BOTH be true before Cilium will let you
        enable the egress gateway feature at all?
        A) BPF masquerade enabled AND kube-proxy replacement enabled
        B) IPv6 enabled AND Hubble enabled
        C) Cluster Mesh enabled AND BGP enabled
        D) Native Routing mode AND netkit device mode

    📝 Chapter Summary (Chapter 18):
    ────────────────────────────────
         ✅ Default Cilium egress SNATs a pod's traffic to whichever NODE
            happens to run it — that source IP changes every time the pod
            is rescheduled, breaking any external IP allowlist.

         ✅ CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy (apiVersion cilium.io/v2, cluster-
            scoped) fixes this: selectors picks source pods,
            destinationCIDRs/excludedCIDRs picks external ranges, and
            egressGateway (or the HA egressGateways list) designates the
            node(s) plus the egressIP/interface that do the masquerading.

         ✅ Egress Gateway requires BPF masquerade + kube-proxy replacement
            enabled and Kubernetes (not kvstore) as Cilium's identity
            store — and is explicitly NOT compatible with Cluster Mesh: a
            policy's gateway must live in the same cluster as its pods.

         ✅ In a Cluster Mesh, "cross-cluster egress" really means one
            independent CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy PER cluster, each with
            its own local gateway node and its own allowlistable IP — not
            a single policy spanning clusters.

         ✅ Chapter 12 (BGP + LB IPAM, INBOUND Service reachability) and
            Chapter 18 (Egress Gateway, OUTBOUND fixed source IP) together
            are the complete "BGP & External Networking" domain — the
            CCA's smallest (6%) but still fully testable domain.

         time budget for the real 90-minute exam, the documentation pages

         chapters of study into a passing score on the first attempt.

Chapter 19: CCA Exam Strategy — How to Pass First Time
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    🎯 What You'll Learn:
    ─────────────────────
         ✅ Exactly how the 90-minute clock and ~60-70 questions map across the 8
            official CCA domains — and why you must budget by WEIGHT, not guesswork
         ✅ Which domains deserve the MOST study time (Architecture 20%, Network
            Policy 18%) versus the least (BGP & External Networking 6%)
         ✅ Which docs.cilium.io pages to bookmark, and how to search them fast,
            before the exam clock starts running
         ✅ The 10 most common CCA exam mistakes — each with a concrete FIX and a
            High/Medium/Low priority for your final review pass
         ✅ Exam-day and study-logistics pitfalls (webcam/ID checks, unrealistic
            time budgets) that are separate from — and just as costly as — gaps
            in Cilium knowledge

            never eats your whole time budget
         ✅ How to walk into exam day with a checklist instead of anxiety

    19.1 Exam Format Deep Dive
         - 90 minutes, multiple choice, online proctored
         - Open book (cilium.io docs allowed!)
         - ~60-70 questions across the 8 OFFICIAL Linux Foundation domains
           (see the OFFICIAL CCA BLUEPRINT panel in the front matter for the
           full subtopic-to-chapter map — this table is the time-budget view)

    19.3 Top 10 CCA Exam Mistakes to Avoid
         - Mistake 1: Confusing K8s NetworkPolicy with CiliumNetworkPolicy
         - Mistake 2: Not knowing Hubble CLI flags
         - Mistake 3: Forgetting about identity-based policies
         - Mistake 4: Mixing up ingress and egress directions
         - Mistake 5: Not understanding eBPF basics
         - Mistake 6: Overlooking Cluster Mesh concepts
         - Mistake 7: Not knowing Cilium CRDs
         - Mistake 8: Forgetting about L7 policy capabilities
         - Mistake 9: Not practicing with real YAML
         - Mistake 10: Spending too long on one question
         [TABLE: Each mistake with fix and priority]
