Appendix D: Glossary — Cilium & eBPF Terminology
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    D.1  A-Z glossary: 100+ terms defined

         This glossary defines every term this guide actually uses, the way
         THIS guide defines it — not a generic external gloss. Where a term
         has a specific field name, CRD, or literal value in Cilium's own
         YAML/CLI, that exact spelling is given. Cross-references point to
         the chapter/section that teaches the term in full (e.g. "Ch4.7").

         — A —
         AS / ASN (Autonomous System / Autonomous System Number) — An AS is
           a network under one organization's control; its ASN identifies
           it on the internet's routing map (e.g. AS 64512). Private ASNs
           live in the 64512-65534 range — safe for lab and on-prem BGP
           peering with Cilium (Ch12.1).
         Advertisement (BGP) — A route a BGP speaker tells its peer about,
           e.g. "I can reach 192.168.1.240/32." Cilium advertises both
           Service IPs (via serviceSelector) and, optionally, each node's
           own Pod CIDR (via exportPodCIDR: true) (Ch12.1-12.2).

         — B —
         BGP (Border Gateway Protocol) — The internet's routing protocol,
           repurposed by Cilium so bare-metal/on-prem clusters can announce
           Service and Pod CIDR routes to a physical router without a cloud
           LoadBalancer API. Cilium peers over a plain TCP session on port
           179 (eBGP — differing ASNs on each side) (Ch12.1).
         BGP Speaker — Any device running BGP; in a Cilium cluster, that's
           both the physical ToR router AND the Cilium agent on each BGP-
           enabled node. Two speakers form a peering session and exchange
           advertisements (Ch12.1).
         BPF (Berkeley Packet Filter) — The 1992 ancestor of eBPF, created
           by Steven McCanne and Van Jacobson as a simple virtual machine
           for filtering packets (the technology behind `tcpdump -i eth0
           'port 80'`). Extended into eBPF in 2014 (Ch3.2).

         — C —
         CCNP (CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy) — The cluster-scoped sibling
           of CiliumNetworkPolicy: same spec fields (endpointSelector,
           ingress, egress...) plus nodeSelector (host firewalling), but NO
           metadata.namespace field — its rules apply everywhere at once.
           Enforced by the SAME eBPF rule engine as CNP; needs a
           ClusterRoleBinding, not a namespaced Role (Ch8.1).
         Cilium Agent — The DaemonSet that runs on EVERY node: loads eBPF
           programs, manages pod networking (CNI), load balancing, policy
           enforcement, encryption, identity, and Hubble flow export — all
           via eBPF in the kernel (Ch4.1-4.2).
         Cilium CLI — The `cilium` command-line tool: installs Cilium
           (wraps Helm), and is your command center for `cilium status`,
           `cilium monitor`, `cilium endpoint`, `cilium policy`, `cilium
           clustermesh`, etc. (Ch5-Ch6).
         Cilium Operator — The Deployment (1-2 replicas) handling
           cluster-wide tasks the per-node Agent shouldn't duplicate: IPAM,
           identity garbage collection, CiliumNode cleanup, etcd compaction
           (Ch4.1). Exposes its own Prometheus metrics on port 9963,
           prefix cilium_operator_* (Ch14.1).
         cilium-dbg — The real binary name behind commands like `cilium
           config` and `cilium bgp peers`, run via `kubectl exec` into the
           Agent pod itself (as opposed to the standalone Cilium CLI, which
           wraps Helm from outside the cluster). This guide shortens
           `cilium-dbg config` to "cilium config" for readability (Ch6.4).
         CiliumBGPClusterConfig (+ CiliumBGPPeerConfig, CiliumBGPAdvertise-
           ment, CiliumBGPNodeConfigOverride) — The newer, modular 4-CRD
           BGP configuration model (apiVersion cilium.io/v2alpha1, moving
           to cilium.io/v2 on 1.17+) that Cilium ALSO ships starting in
           v1.16, splitting topology (ClusterConfig), reusable peer
           settings (PeerConfig), what-to-advertise (Advertisement), and
           per-node overrides (NodeConfigOverride) into separate objects
           (Ch12.2).
         CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy — The cluster-scoped CRD (apiVersion
           cilium.io/v2, no metadata.namespace) that masquerades selected
           pods' egress traffic to a FIXED IP on a designated gateway node.
           Fields: selectors (podSelector/namespaceSelector), destination-
           CIDRs/excludedCIDRs, and egressGateway/egressGateways (Ch18.2).
         CiliumEndpoint (CEP) — The namespaced CRD holding one pod's
           Cilium-managed state: its numeric identity, IP, applied
           policies, and networking status (Ch4.3).
         CiliumEnvoyConfig — The CRD that hands Cilium's per-node Envoy a
           slice of RAW Envoy xDS config (Listeners, Routes, Clusters) —
           Cilium's escape hatch for L7 behavior eBPF alone can't do:
           weighted canary splits, header-based routing, retries. K8s does
           NOT validate the embedded xDS — typos surface only in the
           Cilium agent's logs (Ch10.4).
         CiliumExternalWorkload — The cluster-scoped CRD letting a VM or
           bare-metal machine running Cilium join the mesh as if it were a
           node (Ch4.3).
         CiliumIdentity — The cluster-scoped CRD mapping a label set to a
           numeric security identity, e.g. {app: anihpj} → 12345 (Ch4.3,
           Ch4.4).
         CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool — The CRD (apiVersion cilium.io/v2alpha1,
           moving to v2 on 1.17+) that hands out real external IPs to
           `type: LoadBalancer` Services from a configured pool/range —
           the mechanism behind LB IPAM (Ch12.3).
         CiliumLocalRedirectPolicy (CLRP) — A namespace-scoped CRD that
           redirects a pod's LOCAL traffic to a different destination on
           the same node (e.g. intercepting DNS) (Ch4.3).
         CiliumNetworkPolicy (CNP) — Cilium's namespace-scoped network
           policy CRD (apiVersion cilium.io/v2): the same endpointSelector/
           ingress/egress shape K8s NetworkPolicy uses, extended with L7
           HTTP/gRPC/Kafka rules, DNS (toFQDNs) rules, and Policy Audit
           Mode. Checked BEFORE any matching CCNP for the same traffic
           (Ch7.2-7.3, Ch7.7).
         CiliumNode — The cluster-scoped CRD tracking one node's IPAM
           allocation state — which pod IPs are allocated from that node's
           slice of the cluster Pod CIDR (Ch2.3, Ch4.3). Also carries the
           node's public WireGuard key via the network.cilium.io/wg-pub-key
           annotation (Ch16.4).
         Cluster ID (cluster.id) — A per-cluster number, 1-255, required
           for Cluster Mesh; 0 is RESERVED ("not part of any mesh"). Set at
           install time, never changed afterward; duplicates corrupt
           cluster-local security identities (Ch17.2).
         Cluster Mesh — Cilium's feature connecting multiple Kubernetes
           clusters so pods in DIFFERENT clusters reach each other
           directly (e.g. anihpj in us-east calling postgres in eu-west),
           built on the clustermesh-apiserver plus 4 real prerequisites:
           unique cluster.id, unique cluster.name, non-overlapping Pod
           CIDRs, mutual reachability (Ch17.1-17.2).
         Cluster Name (cluster.name) — A human-readable, ≤32-character,
           lowercase alphanumeric+dashes identifier, unique per cluster in
           a mesh (e.g. "us-east") — the second of Cluster Mesh's two
           mandatory identifiers alongside Cluster ID (Ch17.2).
         clustermesh-apiserver — The ONE component that makes Cluster Mesh
           work: it watches its own cluster's Services/Endpoints/Nodes/
           identities and republishes that state over an mTLS-secured,
           etcd-compatible key/value protocol, reachable from every OTHER
           cluster's Cilium agents (Ch17.4).
         CNI (Container Network Interface) — A SPECIFICATION (not
           software) defining ADD/DEL/CHECK/VERSION commands that kubelet
           executes against a plugin BINARY (e.g. /opt/cni/bin/cilium-cni)
           to give a new pod a network. Calico, Flannel, and Cilium are all
           CNI plugins that implement the same spec differently (Ch1.2).
         Conntrack (Connection Tracking) — The mechanism that lets a
           datapath skip a full policy/NAT decision for packets belonging
           to an ALREADY-established connection. iptables/IPVS share ONE
           finite kernel table (nf_conntrack) for this across the whole
           node; Cilium's eBPF datapath keeps its own per-service BPF CT
           maps instead, and socket-level LB skips conntrack entirely for
           many ClusterIP flows (Ch4.5, Ch11.1-11.2).
         CRD (Custom Resource Definition) — The Kubernetes mechanism
           Cilium uses to store its own state in the K8s API. This guide's
           reference version registers 9 core CRDs: CiliumNetworkPolicy,
           CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy, CiliumNode, CiliumIdentity,
           CiliumEndpoint, CiliumExternalWorkload, CiliumLocalRedirect-
           Policy, CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy, and CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy —
           with more (CiliumEnvoyConfig, CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool, the v2
           BGP CRDs) added by later feature chapters (Ch4.3).

         — D —
         Datapath — The actual code path a packet takes through the
           kernel and eBPF programs, from "packet arrives" to "packet
           delivered": pre-filter → identity lookup → policy enforcement →
           conntrack → forward/deliver → Hubble export (Ch4.5, Ch4.7).
         Datapath Model — Cilium's own term for WHICH VARIANT of the
           datapath runs on a node: the combination of (a) Encapsulation
           Mode (Tunnel vs. Native Routing), (b) Pod Device Mode (veth vs.
           netkit), and (c) Host-Routing Mode (Legacy vs. eBPF). These 3
           axes are independent and never change WHAT traffic is allowed —
           only HOW allowed traffic physically moves. An official CCA
           Architecture-domain subtopic (Ch4.7).
         DNAT (Destination Network Address Translation) — Rewriting a
           packet's destination address — e.g. a ClusterIP like
           10.100.50.10:5432 rewritten to a real backend pod IP like
           10.0.0.99:5432. Cilium's socket-level LB does this rewrite once,
           at connect() time, instead of per-packet (Ch2.4, Ch10.2).
         DSR (Direct Server Return) — A load-balancing forwarding mode
           where the backend pod replies DIRECTLY to the client, skipping
           the ingress node entirely — preserves the client's real source
           IP, but needs IP-option or Geneve-header room (an MTU cost). The
           opposite tradeoff from SNAT; Hybrid mode uses DSR for TCP and
           SNAT for UDP to get both (Ch11.4).

         — E —
         eBPF (extended Berkeley Packet Filter) — Sandboxed programs that
           run safely inside the Linux kernel — no kernel source changes,
           no kernel modules, no reboots — verified before running,
           JIT-compiled to native code, and able to read/write eBPF Maps.
           Cilium is the largest production user of eBPF; this guide's
           "JavaScript for the kernel" analogy (Ch3.1).
         eBPF Host-Routing — The datapath option that lets a packet cross
           from the host network namespace into a pod's veth (or netkit)
           device by BYPASSING iptables and the upper host stack entirely,
           for a faster namespace switch. Auto-enabled whenever the
           running kernel supports it; netkit ALWAYS uses it, while veth
           can use either this or Legacy routing (Ch4.7, axis c).
         eBPF Map — The ONLY way an eBPF program persists data, and the
           ONLY way userspace (the Cilium Agent) reads it back. Cilium uses
           several map types for different jobs: BPF_MAP_TYPE_HASH
           (pod IP → identity), LRU_HASH (connection tracking, auto-
           evicting), ARRAY (policy rules by identity), LPM_TRIE (CIDR →
           identity, longest-prefix match), PERCPU_HASH (lock-free
           counters), and RINGBUF (event stream to Hubble) (Ch3.5).
         eBPF Verifier — The kernel component that inspects an eBPF
           program BEFORE it's allowed to load: walks every code path,
           checks for infinite loops, bounds violations, invalid pointers,
           unreachable code, and a hard instruction-count ceiling. Only a
           program that PASSES gets JIT-compiled and attached (Ch3.1,
           Ch3.3).
         Egress Gateway — Designates one or more "gateway nodes" to
           masquerade ALL matching pods' egress traffic to one FIXED,
           externally-allowlistable IP, regardless of which node the pod
           actually runs on — fixing the problem of a pod's outbound
           source IP changing every time it's rescheduled. Configured via
           CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy; requires BPF masquerade + kube-proxy
           replacement + Kubernetes-mode identity; NOT compatible with
           Cluster Mesh (one independent policy per cluster instead); the
           plural egressGateways list adds HA/failover (Ch18.2-18.3).
         Endpoint — Cilium's term for anything it manages an identity and
           policy state for — in practice, almost always a pod, tracked
           via the CiliumEndpoint CRD and visible with `cilium endpoint
           list` (Ch4.3, Ch6.2).
         Envoy — The L7 proxy Cilium runs ONE instance of PER NODE (never
           per pod) to handle HTTP-aware traffic: canary weighting
           (CiliumEnvoyConfig), Ingress, and Gateway API. Plain L3/L4
           traffic (e.g. anihpj → postgres) never touches it at all — only
           this "per-node, not per-pod" fact is what "sidecar-less"
           actually means (Ch10.2).
         ExternalIPs — A Service type whose traffic takes the SAME eBPF
           path as NodePort (TC/XDP hook on the node's external interface,
           SNAT by default), plus a CVE-2020-8554 MITM mitigation NodePort
           alone doesn't need (Ch11.2).

         — F —
         FQDN (Fully Qualified Domain Name) — A DNS name like
           api.stripe.com; see toFQDNs for how Cilium turns FQDN rules into
           enforceable, dynamically-updated egress policy (Ch7.5).

         — G —
         Gateway API — The Kubernetes-SIG-standard, role-oriented
           successor to Ingress: a FAMILY of CRDs (GatewayClass, Gateway,
           HTTPRoute, GRPCRoute, TLSRoute) instead of one do-everything
           object. Cilium implements it via the SAME per-node Envoy as
           Ingress and L7 canaries — Gateway API is Path B with a richer
           front door, not a new "Path C" (Ch10.5).
         GatewayClass — The Gateway API resource declaring WHICH
           controller implements a Gateway; Cilium's is literally named
           "cilium" (gatewayClassName: cilium), the same string used for
           ingressClassName: cilium (Ch10.5).
         Global Service — An ordinary K8s Service carrying the annotation
           service.cilium.io/global: "true" — no new CRD. Cilium then
           load-balances it across backend pods in EVERY connected Cluster
           Mesh cluster with a Service of the same name+namespace, also
           marked global (Ch17.3, Ch17.5).
         Grafana — The dashboarding tool this guide points at Hubble's
           Prometheus metrics (hubble_* on port 9965) to build panels like
           HTTP error rate, DNS query rate, and drop rate by reason for
           anihpj (Ch14.3).
         GRPCRoute — A Gateway API kind for native gRPC routing — one of
           the protocol-extensibility wins Gateway API has over Ingress,
           which is HTTP(S)-only by design (Ch10.5).

         — H —
         Host-Routing Mode — The Datapath Model axis governing HOW a
           packet crosses from the host network namespace into a pod's
           device: Legacy (still traverses iptables and the regular Linux
           stack) vs. eBPF Host-Routing (bypasses both). A genuinely
           separate axis from Pod Device Mode, not just another name for
           veth-vs-netkit (Ch4.7, axis c).
         Hubble — Cilium's built-in observability platform: "tcpdump for
           Kubernetes, but identity-aware" — it shows WHO talked to whom
           (pod names/namespaces/labels, not just raw IPs), how much data,
           and whether it was FORWARDED or DROPPED (Ch13.1).
         Hubble CLI — The `hubble observe` terminal tool; the fast,
           filterable (--from-pod, --to-namespace, --verdict, --port) text
           view onto the same flow stream Hubble UI and Prometheus also
           read (Ch13.3).
         Hubble Metrics — Hubble's own Prometheus endpoint, exposed on the
           hubble-metrics Service, port 9965, metric prefix hubble_* (flow,
           httpV2, tcp, dns, drop, icmp, port-distribution flags) — a
           SEPARATE system from the Cilium Agent's own metrics (port 9962,
           prefix cilium_*). httpV2 replaced the now-deprecated http flag
           (Ch14.1).
         Hubble Relay — The Deployment that aggregates flow events from
           EVERY Cilium Agent's eBPF Ring Buffer cluster-wide, so Hubble
           CLI/UI/Prometheus see one unified stream instead of per-node
           silos. Optional — must be explicitly enabled (Ch13.2, Ch4.1).
         Hubble UI — The web dashboard (`cilium hubble ui`, default
           localhost:12000) built on Hubble Relay's aggregated stream: a
           live Service Map (topology graph, color-coded by verdict) plus
           a Flow Table underneath it (the same data `hubble observe`
           prints as text). Optional, ClusterIP-only by default (Ch13.4).
         Hubble Verdict — The field every Hubble flow carries recording
           what Cilium's datapath actually did with that packet: FORWARDED
           (allowed), DROPPED (blocked), AUDIT (would be dropped, but the
           policy is in audit mode — logged only), or ERROR (Cilium itself
           hit a problem, not a policy decision). Filterable with
           `hubble observe --verdict` (Ch15.1).
         HTTPRoute — The Gateway API kind carrying actual HTTP routing
           rules, owned by the application team (not the platform team
           that owns the Gateway). Its backendRefs list has a native
           weight field for traffic splitting — no vendor annotation, no
           hand-fed Envoy xDS required (Ch10.5).
         Hybrid Mode — See DSR: the forwarding mode that uses DSR for TCP
           and SNAT for UDP, trading complexity for low TCP latency without
           a UDP MTU hit (Ch11.4).

         — I —
         Identity (security identity) — Cilium's replacement for IP-based
           firewall rules: a pod's labels (e.g. {app: anihpj, component:
           django}) hash to a 32-bit numeric identity (e.g. 12345), stored
           in eBPF maps as IP→Identity and Identity→Policy. A pod restart
           gets a new IP but the SAME labels, so the SAME identity — no
           policy ever needs to be rewritten (Ch4.4).
         Ingress — The original Kubernetes API for routing external HTTP
           traffic into the cluster: ONE object mixing infra
           (ingressClassName), host/path routing, and app wiring together,
           HTTP(S)-only, no native traffic-split field. Cilium ships a
           real Ingress controller (ingressClassName: cilium) that hands
           traffic to the same per-node Envoy via the TPROXY kernel
           facility (Ch10.5).
         IPAM (IP Address Management) — How pods get their IP addresses.
           Cilium's IPAM is CRD-backed (the CiliumNode CRD tracks each
           node's allocation) and supports several modes: Kubernetes
           host-local, Cluster Pool, and Multi-Pool (Ch2.3).
         IPSec — The older, IETF-standard encryption option for pod
           traffic, built on the Linux kernel's XFRM/ESP framework using
           AES-GCM (with optional AES-NI hardware offload). Cilium's IPSec
           decryption is limited to a single CPU core per tunnel — a real
           bottleneck WireGuard doesn't share (Ch16.2).
         iptables — The legacy Linux firewall/NAT mechanism kube-proxy and
           traditional CNIs rely on: sequential, O(n) rule-chain matching
           that degrades badly at scale (thousands of Services) and
           depends on the single, finite nf_conntrack table (Ch3.6,
           Ch11.1).

         — J —
         JIT Compilation (Just-In-Time) — The step, right after the
           verifier passes an eBPF program, that turns its bytecode into
           native x86/ARM machine instructions — why eBPF programs run at
           near-native speed instead of being interpreted (Ch3.1, Ch3.3).

         — K —
         kube-proxy — Kubernetes' traditional Service-routing component,
           implemented via iptables or IPVS chain rules. Cilium replaces
           it entirely rather than running alongside it (Ch2.5).
         kube-proxy replacement — Cilium's eBPF load balancer taking over
           kube-proxy's job at the socket, TC, and XDP hooks — O(1) hash
           lookups instead of O(n) chain walks, no shared conntrack
           bottleneck. Enabled with kubeProxyReplacement=true; verify with
           `cilium-dbg status` showing "KubeProxyReplacement: True", not
           just that the Helm flag was accepted (Ch11.2-11.3).

         — L —
         L2 Announcements — Cilium's BGP-free alternative for assigning
           reachability to a LoadBalancer IP on a flat, single-subnet LAN,
           via ARP/NDP replies instead of BGP route advertisements
           (Ch12.4).
         L7 (Layer 7) Policy — A CiliumNetworkPolicy rule that inspects
           application-layer data — HTTP method + path, gRPC method, Kafka
           topic, DNS name — something K8s' native NetworkPolicy cannot do
           at all (Ch7.2, Ch7.4).
         LB IPAM (LoadBalancer IPAM) — The CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool-driven
           mechanism that assigns real external IPs to `type: LoadBalancer`
           Services from a configured pool, with zero cloud provider
           involved. Only ASSIGNS the IP — making it reachable is BGP's
           (or L2 Announcements') separate job (Ch12.3-12.4).
         LPM_TRIE — The eBPF map type used for longest-prefix-match
           lookups — e.g. mapping a CIDR block like 10.0.0.0/16 to a
           cluster/identity, the same structure behind a "zip code →
           region" style lookup (Ch3.5).

         — M —
         Maglev — The consistent-hashing algorithm Cilium's eBPF load
           balancer uses: a large lookup table (M slots, M much greater
           than N backends) built from each backend's own permutation, so
           adding/removing ONE backend reshuffles roughly 1% of the table
           instead of nearly every existing connection the way naive
           hash(conn) % N hashing would (Ch11.2).
         Masquerade / Masquerade IP — Rewriting a packet's source address
           (a form of SNAT) as it leaves a node. Default egress masquer-
           ades to whichever node a pod happens to run on (an unstable
           source IP); Egress Gateway masquerades instead to one FIXED IP
           on a designated gateway node (Ch18.1-18.2).
         matchExpressions — A CiliumNetworkPolicy/CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy
           selector field supporting operators like In and NotIn. It lives
           alongside matchLabels inside the same selector entry (combined
           with AND), letting ONE expression match several values with
           OR-like effect — e.g. component In [web, worker] — or, with
           NotIn on a never-used key, the official "match everything"
           idiom used in BGP's serviceSelector (Ch12.2, Ch15).
         matchLabels — The basic CiliumNetworkPolicy selector field:
           exact-match key:value pairs (e.g. app: postgres) identifying
           WHICH pods a rule targets or allows from (Ch7.3).
         mTLS / Mutual Authentication — Cilium's feature (Beta) requiring
           BOTH sides of a connection to cryptographically prove their
           identity before it's let through, via authentication: mode:
           "required" inside a CiliumNetworkPolicy rule. Built on SPIFFE/
           SPIRE. Proves WHO is connecting — a SEPARATE guarantee from
           encryption, which protects WHAT'S on the wire (Ch10.3, Ch16.5).
         MTU (Maximum Transmission Unit) — The maximum packet size a link
           allows before fragmentation (1500 bytes by default). Relevant
           to encapsulation overhead (Tunnel mode adds header bytes;
           Native Routing avoids it) and to DSR (which needs header room
           to encode service metadata) (Ch1.5, Ch4.6, Ch11.4).

         — N —
         Native Routing (Direct Routing) — The Datapath Model encapsu-
           lation option with NO extra headers — full MTU, pure L3
           routing — but it requires the underlying network (or BGP) to
           already know how to route each node's Pod CIDR (Ch4.6, Ch4.7
           axis a).
         netkit — A newer Linux kernel device type (kernel ≥ 6.8,
           CONFIG_NETKIT=y) built specifically for Cilium to replace veth
           pairs, aiming to reduce a pod's network-namespace overhead to
           near-zero. Supports Layer 3 (recommended, skips ARP) or Layer 2
           mode; a per-cluster/per-node-pool INSTALL-TIME choice with no
           hot-swap for already-running pods; falls back to veth
           automatically on unsupported kernels (Ch4.7, axis b).
         Network Namespace (Linux) — The kernel isolation mechanism giving
           each container its own interfaces, routing table, iptables
           rules, and IP addresses — a "private network room." All
           containers in one pod share ONE network namespace via the pause
           container pattern (Ch2.1-2.2).
         NodePort — A Service type whose traffic enters via a TC/XDP hook
           on the node's external interface, gets Maglev-balanced across
           that node's own backend set, and is SNAT'd by default (Ch11.2).
         Node-to-Node Encryption — How Cilium's WireGuard/IPSec encryption
           actually works: ONE tunnel per PAIR of nodes (not per pod or per
           connection) — every pod's traffic crossing that node boundary
           rides the same tunnel. Same-node pod-to-pod traffic is NEVER
           wire-encrypted, because it never leaves the node's kernel.
           Control-plane nodes are excluded by default to avoid a key-
           rotation bootstrapping problem (Ch16.4).

         — O —
         Overlay Network — A virtual network built on top of an existing
           one via encapsulation (VXLAN/Geneve Tunnel mode is Cilium's
           overlay option) — contrast with Underlay Network (Ch1.5, Ch4.6).

         — P —
         Path A / Path B — This guide's own naming (Ch10.2) for Cilium
           service mesh's two data paths: Path A is plain L3/L4 traffic
           (e.g. anihpj → postgres), rewritten at the SOCKET layer by eBPF
           with no proxy involved at all. Path B is L7 (HTTP) traffic,
           redirected to the per-node Envoy for canary weighting, header
           routing, Ingress, and Gateway API — all of which are Path B,
           never a separate "Path C" (Ch10.2, Ch10.5).
         Pause Container — The tiny (~700KB), do-nothing-but-sleep infra
           container created FIRST in every pod, which creates and holds
           the pod's network namespace so a crashing app container never
           takes the pod's networking down with it (Ch2.2).
         Pod CIDR — The IP range a node (or the whole cluster) allocates
           pod addresses from, e.g. 10.0.0.0/24 per node inside a cluster-
           wide 10.0.0.0/16. MUST be non-overlapping across every cluster
           and node in a Cluster Mesh (Ch2.3, Ch17.2).
         Policy Audit Mode — A CiliumNetworkPolicy setting layered on TOP
           of an already-enforcing endpoint: a matching DENY only LOGS a
           "would-deny" verdict to Hubble instead of actually blocking the
           packet. The safe rollout pattern is Audit → Monitor → Enforce
           (Ch7.6, Ch7.8).
         Policy Enforcement Mode — A SEPARATE axis from whether a rule
           allows specific traffic: whether an endpoint checks policy AT
           ALL. Three literal values — default (allow until first policy
           selects it, then enforce that direction only), always (deny-all
           even with zero matching rules), never (ignore all policy,
           always allow) — set cluster-wide via policyEnforcementMode
           (Ch7.8).
         Policy Verdict — The `cilium monitor --type policy-verdict`
           output for one flow: action allow/deny/audit plus match L3-L4
           (a real selector/port matched) or match none (nothing did). A
           policy DENY is a SILENT drop — no RST, no ICMP — which is why a
           "hanging" connection is a classic policy symptom (Ch9.2).

         — R —
         ReferenceGrant — The Gateway API object that must explicitly
           allow a Route in one namespace to reference a backend Service
           in another — a cross-namespace safety guardrail Ingress has no
           equivalent for (Ch10.5).
         RINGBUF — The eBPF map type used as a circular event buffer —
           the mechanism carrying flow events from the kernel to the
           Cilium Agent and on to Hubble (Ch3.5, Ch4.5).

         — S —
         Service Affinity — How Cluster Mesh routes traffic for a Global
           Service: "local" (prefer healthy local backends, fail over to
           remote only if EVERY local backend is down), "remote" (mirror
           image), or "none" (the DEFAULT if omitted — spread evenly across
           every healthy backend anywhere) (Ch17.3).
         service.cilium.io/affinity — The Service annotation implementing
           Service Affinity; valid values "local" | "remote" | "none"
           (Ch17.3, Ch17.5).
         service.cilium.io/global — The Service annotation ("true") that
           is the ONLY thing needed to opt a Service into cross-cluster
           load balancing — see Global Service (Ch17.5).
         service.cilium.io/shared — Controls whether THIS cluster's
           backends are exposed TO the rest of the mesh (default "true");
           "false" consumes remote backends without exposing local ones
           (Ch17.5).
         Service Mesh — The layer handling service-to-service traffic
           management (mTLS, canary routing, observability) between pods.
           Cilium's version is built into the Agent/per-node Envoy rather
           than requiring a Sidecar in every pod (Ch10.1-10.2).
         Session Affinity — Pinning a client's connections to the same
           backend pod. Keyed differently depending on origin: an
           in-cluster client (socket-LB path) is pinned by its network-
           namespace cookie (no packet exists yet at connect() time); an
           external client (NodePort/ExternalIPs) is pinned by source IP
           (Ch11.4).
         Sidecar — A proxy container injected INTO every pod needing
           service-mesh features — the traditional approach (e.g. Istio)
           that Cilium's per-node Envoy avoids; "sidecar-less" means no
           PER-POD proxy, not "no proxy anywhere" (Ch10.1-10.2).
         SNAT (Source Network Address Translation) — Rewriting a packet's
           SOURCE address, typically to the node's own IP as it leaves the
           cluster (default egress masquerade) or as a Service's default
           return-path forwarding mode (contrast DSR) (Ch11.4, Ch18.1).
         Socket-Level Load Balancing — Cilium choosing a Service's backend
           by rewriting a POD'S OWN SOCKET at connect() time, inside the
           kernel via an eBPF cgroup hook — BEFORE a single network packet
           is built. This is what makes Path A (Ch10.2) proxy-free (Ch10.2,
           Ch11.2).
         SPIFFE (Secure Production Identity Framework For Everyone) — The
           STANDARD behind Cilium's Mutual Authentication feature: it
           defines how every workload gets a verifiable identity document,
           an SVID (Ch10.3).
         SPIRE — The production implementation of SPIFFE that actually
           ISSUES SVIDs: a SPIRE Server (root of trust for the cluster)
           plus a SPIRE Agent on every node (verifies a pod's node+labels,
           then requests its SVID). Cilium's Mutual Authentication is only
           validated against SPIRE (Ch10.3).
         SVID (SPIFFE Verifiable Identity Document) — The X.509
           certificate a SPIRE Agent hands a pod, carrying an identity like
           spiffe://<domain>/<path> — what each side of a Mutual
           Authentication handshake presents to prove who it is (Ch10.3).

         — T —
         TC (Traffic Control) — The eBPF hook point AFTER XDP where
           Cilium attaches its MAIN eBPF programs — every pod packet
           passes through TC ingress/egress on its way in or out
           (BPF_PROG_TYPE_SCHED_CLS) (Ch3.4).
         TLSRoute — A Gateway API kind that routes by SNI without
           terminating TLS — another protocol Ingress has no equivalent
           for (Ch10.5).
         toFQDNs — The CiliumNetworkPolicy rule type restricting WHICH
           RESOLVED IPs a pod may connect to after a DNS lookup, paired
           with a toPorts.rules.dns rule restricting WHICH NAMES may be
           resolved in the first place. Cilium watches the pod's own DNS
           traffic and dynamically updates the allowed IP set as an FQDN
           re-resolves — it does not pin to IPs at apply time (Ch7.5,
           Ch20.2).
         TPROXY — The Linux kernel facility Cilium's eBPF datapath uses to
           redirect Ingress/Gateway API traffic to the per-node Envoy
           (Path B) without a separate Ingress-only proxy Deployment
           (Ch10.5).
         Tunnel Mode — The Datapath Model encapsulation option (VXLAN or
           Geneve) that works on ANY underlying network with zero network
           configuration, at the cost of a small per-packet header
           overhead — Cilium's default (Ch4.6, Ch4.7 axis a).

         — U —
         Underlay Network — The physical/base network that an Overlay
           Network (or Native Routing) runs on top of, or depends on
           already knowing how to route (Ch1.5, Ch4.6).

         — V —
         veth (virtual Ethernet pair) — The traditional Linux pod-
           networking device: ONE pair per pod, one end in the pod's
           network namespace, one end in the host namespace — what every
           other CNI (Calico, Flannel) also uses, and Cilium's automatic
           fallback on kernels below netkit's 6.8 floor (Ch4.7, axis b).
         VXLAN / Geneve — The two encapsulation protocols Cilium's Tunnel
           mode can use to carry cross-node pod traffic inside a UDP
           header (Ch4.6).

         — W —
         WireGuard — Cilium's recommended default transparent-encryption
           protocol: kernel-native since Linux 5.6, using the ChaCha20-
           Poly1305 cipher, enabled with a single Helm flag
           (encryption.type=wireguard) and zero application code changes.
           You pick ONE mode (WireGuard or IPSec) cluster-wide — Cilium
           never mixes the two (Ch16.2-16.3).

         — X —
         XDP (eXpress Data Path) — The EARLIEST eBPF hook point, at the
           NIC driver level, before the kernel even allocates a socket
           buffer — capable of dropping packets at line rate, but too
           early to have pod identity, so it is NOT Cilium's default
           enforcement hook (that's TC) (Ch3.4).

         — Z —
         Zero Trust — The default-deny security posture Cilium enables:
           without any policy, all pods can talk to all pods; a real
           CiliumNetworkPolicy/CCNP strategy flips this to "nothing is
           allowed unless a rule says so" (Ch7.1, Ch7.7).

    D.2  Top 20 Must-Know Terms for the CCA Exam

         A curated, highest-value subset for last-minute review — every
         term above is fair game, but if time is short, know these 20 cold.

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
